<meta>This page follows the title page and table of contents. It introduces the practical philosophy of alchemy through a series of mnemonic verses, a common practice used to hide secrets from the uninitiated while providing a rhythmic guide for practitioners.</meta>

There is a fountain in the mud, where a snake lies deep within:
It flies out at once unless you close the cracks on all sides.
There is a vein of dew; when a pleasant part of it rises,
The final stages bring forth the poisons that were absorbed.
After the blowing twins <note>original: "gemes" (likely *geminos*). In alchemical allegory, this often refers to the dual nature of vapors or the use of bellows.</note>, strong giants grew;
While we stand above, elephants perish by a single blast.
The water of life <term>aqua vitæ: In alchemy, this refers to a potent solvent or highly rectified spirit used to "animate" dead matter, not merely alcohol.</term> is what is joined to this well-ground substance;
If you perform the rite correctly, the body finally becomes gentle.
For when well-cleansed and washed in its own sweat,
That which was black and previously darkened is rendered white <note>This describes the "Albedo" or whitening, the stage of purification in the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.
One's wish is granted when it becomes well-washed by its own means;
Let it not be known unless the body was sick.
Nor does the divine stone <note>original: "petra diuina," a synonym for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> turn first white and then lemon-yellow
Until the dregs of the waste <note>original: "fecis urina." While it literally means "dregs of urine," alchemists often used such terms to describe the "mercurial water" or the impurities drained from the vessel.</note> are removed from the vessel of nature.
If anyone is perhaps unable to weigh these verses,
If you examine them well, you will be wealthy in all things.
If you dissolve the fixed and make the dissolved fly <note>This refers to the core alchemical maxim *Solve et Coagula*: "dissolve and coagulate." You must turn solids into liquids/vapors and back again.</note>,
And turn it into a stone, I shall make you live in safety.
Join the separated, separate the joined, and rejoin the separated once more.
Liquefy the hardened, and join the liquefied; then I shall call you blessed.
Say that the Arsenic <note>original: "arsenicum." In this context, it refers to the "soul" or volatile principle of the metal, rather than just the modern poison.</note> is the soul, but the spirit is Quicksilver <note>original: "argentum viuum." Mercury.</note>;
The "Lime" <note>original: "calx." The dry powder left after a metal is burned or oxidized, representing the physical body.</note> is said to be the body.
If a white woman <note>Representing Mercury or the Moon.</note> is married to a red husband <note>Representing Sulfur or the Sun.</note>,
They soon embrace, and in that embrace they conceive.
Destroy the captured thing first through a suitable *bensar* <note>original: "bensar." An obscure alchemical term, likely referring to a specialized vessel or a catalytic salt used in the transmutation process.</note>;
Grind the mass thus made once it has been gently extracted.

<meta>This page continues the alchemical instructions from the previous leaf. The previous page ended with the instruction to "make the dissolved fly," and this page begins by explaining how to capture that "flying" substance to begin the process of stabilization or "fixing."</meta>

Capture the flying spirit <note>original: "Spiritum volantem." In alchemical theory, this refers to mercury or any substance that vaporizes (volatilizes) easily when heated.</note>
And draw it into a ray of the sun <note>original: "radium solis." This symbolizes the application of solar heat or the "sulfuric" principle required to stabilize the vapor.</note>
So that it may be duly fixed <note>original: "fixetur." To "fix" a substance is to transform it from a volatile gas or liquid into a solid that can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating.</note>
And let the fixed become volatile;
Then cook it gently
And from a portion of it, create earth
Which you must place in moisture
So that it may be perfectly moistened.
Quickly join the moist substance,
Grind it with the amalgam <term>amalgama: A mixture of metals, often involving mercury, used as a foundational material for further transformation.</term>
Upon a very hard stone;
Then place it in its own vessel
So that it may be perfectly calcined <note>original: "calcinetur." Calcination is the process of heating a substance to high temperatures until it is reduced to a fine powder or ash.</note>
Afterwards, soak it in its own milk <note>original: "suo lacte." This refers to "mercurial milk" or "virgin's milk," a white liquid solvent used to "nourish" the material.</note>
So that it may be multiplied from within:
Which is roasted for a time.

It is clothed in a white mantle <note>original: "alba clamide." This signifies the *Albedo* or "whitening" stage, a crucial point of purification in the Great Work.</note>,
And having been multiplied in a royal fashion
It is reduced to ash:
To which you must return its own sweat <note>original: "sudorem suum." The condensed vapors or liquid that were previously extracted from the substance are reunited with the solid base.</note>:
Until, with a royal diadem,
The King <note>original: "Rex." In alchemical allegory, the King represents the perfected Gold or the final "Red" state of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is duly crowned.
And being most easily dissolved
May it enter into the body,
And if you wish to work more subtly
Make the fixed volatile
With a powerful blast <note>original: "flamine." This refers to the "spirit" of the fire or the use of bellows to increase the heat.</note>
Then return it to the earth
With the moderation of fire
And with such an order maintained
It will be duly drawn forth
Until it flows most smoothly

->And thus you shall have the stone, from which you shall always rejoice.<-

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a set of practical instructions regarding the "flying spirit" (mercury) and the process of "fixing" substances. This page marks the formal beginning of the first book of the "Summa Perfectionis" (The Height of Perfection), widely considered the most important alchemical text of the Middle Ages. The author, writing under the name Geber, moves from specific recipes to a theoretical and systematic overview of the entire art.</meta>

BOOK ONE
A ii

# HERE BEGINS THE BOOK OF GEBER <note>original: "IN CIPIT LIBER GEBER." Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–813), a foundational figure in chemistry and alchemy. However, modern scholars believe this specific text, the *Summa Perfectionis*, was likely written by a 13th-century European monk known as "Pseudo-Geber."</note>

## Chapter One

All our science, which we have abbreviated from the books of the ancients, we have gathered here into one single summary <note>original: "summā vnam." This refers to the *Summa Perfectionis*, the work's title, implying a complete and perfect compendium of knowledge.</note> through various compilations in our volumes. And that which was diminished in the books previously written by us, we have compensated for in this supreme tradition of our book, and we have supplied their defects with brief speech. That which was hidden by us in one part, we have made manifest in that same part in this our volume, so that the completion of such an excellent and noble part of philosophy may be open to the wise.

Know therefore, dearest son, that in this work the entire operation of the art is sufficiently contained in general chapters and a universal discussion, without any omission. By God! Whoever shall work according to this book will rejoice to have found the true end of this art. 

But know, dearest son, that he who is ignorant of the natural principles within himself is already very far removed from our art, because he does not possess the true root upon which to found his intention. And he who knows his natural principles and all the causes of minerals, but has not yet attained the true end and profit of this most hidden art, nevertheless has an easier access to the principles of this art than the one upon whom ignorance falls regarding his intention and the method of this our work; the latter is quite far from the entrance of the art.

<margin>X</margin>
But he who knows the principles and causes of all minerals, and the mode of their generation—which consists of the intention of nature—is indeed only a little removed from the completion of the work, without which our science cannot be perfected. For art cannot imitate nature in every aspect of the work, but it imitates it as much as it duly can. 

Therefore, dearest son, we reveal a secret to you: craftsmen err in this because they desire to imitate nature in every single difference of properties and actions. Work, therefore, to study in our volumes, and strive to revolve them in your mind very often, so that you may acquire the true intention from our manner of speaking; for in them you will find that upon which you ought to found your mind, and you shall know how to repel errors from yourself through them—those errors in which you might try to imitate nature in the craftsmanship of your work.

## A GENERAL DISCOURSE ON THE ORDER of the execution of all the chapters.

### Chapter 2.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut drop cap 'P' containing stylized flowers and foliage.</image-desc>
We will therefore briefly set forth for you all the impediments by which a craftsman is hindered in the work from reaching the true end. Secondly, we shall indeed dispute against the ignorant and the sophists <term>sophisticos: In alchemy, "sophists" refers to fraudulent practitioners who use deceptive tricks to mimic the appearance of gold or silver without performing true transmutation.</term>, who, because of their ignorance and lack of skill...

<summary>This page introduces the "Summa Perfectionis," where the author (Geber) claims to have condensed all ancient and personal alchemical knowledge into one complete guide. He emphasizes that success depends on understanding "natural principles" and the "causes of minerals" rather than blindly trying to copy every minor detail of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, alchemy, Summa Perfectionis, natural principles, mineral causes, imitation of nature, alchemical theory, sophists, Jabir ibn Hayyan</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory portion of Book One of the *Summa Perfectionis*. Following the previous page’s assertion that an alchemist must understand natural principles, Geber now outlines his plan to refute critics and then categorizes the physical and mental obstacles that prevent a person from succeeding in the "Great Work."</meta>

BOOK

...who, by seeking the mastery and profit of this art, actually destroy the art and claim it does not exist. We will therefore set down all their arguments and then destroy them most clearly, so that it will be plain enough to prudent men that none of their sophisms <note>original: "sophismata." Deceptive or fallacious arguments intended to mislead.</note> contain the truth. And then we will dispute concerning the natural principles which are the intention of nature: and likewise concerning the manner of their generation and their mixing together through the work of nature. Third, we will discuss their defects, according to the science of the ancient philosophers. Fourth, we will narrate the principles which serve the intention of this our work, in which we can imitate nature, and the manner of mixing and altering according to the natural course, with their causes to be brought back to the goal of our work.

# GENERAL DISCOURSE
# regarding the execution of the first chapter on the impediments to this art.

## Chapter 3

There are, therefore, two general types of impediments that come upon this work: Natural impotence, and the lack of necessary things or the preoccupation of labor <note>This refers to either a lack of funding and equipment or being too busy with other work to focus on alchemy.</note>. However, we say that natural impotence is manifold: on the part of the craftsman’s organs <note>original: "organi artificis." In medieval science, "organs" refers to the physical tools of the body, such as hands and eyes.</note>, and on the part of the soul. On the part of the craftsman's organs, it is manifold: either because the organ is weak, or entirely corrupted. Finally, regarding the soul’s impotences, it is manifold: either because the soul is perverse in its organs, possessing no rectitude or reason in itself because of those organs—such as the soul of an insane person or a fool—or because it is "fantastic" <note>original: "fantastica." Referring to an over-active or unstable imagination that cannot focus on reality.</note>, improperly and easily receptive to contrary forms, and suddenly stretching from one thing to be known to its opposite, and from one universal truth to its opposite.

# PARTICULAR DISCOURSE
# on the impediments to this work on the part of the craftsman’s body

## Chapter 4

We have now determined for you the impediments of this work in general. Now, indeed, we address you in a special discourse in this chapter, and more aptly, we will relate to you all those impediments most fully in order. We say, therefore: that if anyone does not have his organs complete, he cannot reach the fulfillment of this work by himself—as, for example, if he were blind, or had his extremities cut off <note>original: "detrucatus." Meaning missing hands or fingers needed for delicate lab work.</note>, since he is not helped by the limbs through which this art is perfected, which serve as nature’s ministers. If, however, the craftsman’s body were weak and sick, such as the body of those with fevers or lepers—whose limbs fall away—or those struggling at the very end of life, or old men of decrepit age, he does not reach the completion of the art. Therefore, by these natural impotences of the body, the craftsman is hindered in his intention.

<summary>This page outlines Geber's systematic approach to alchemy, starting with a refutation of skeptics and moving into a "medical" assessment of the alchemist himself. It establishes that successful alchemy requires both a sound body (complete limbs and health) and a stable, rational mind.</summary>

<keywords>natural principles, sophisms, physical impediments, mental health, alchemy, craftsman's organs, soul, Pseudo-Geber</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
### DISCOURSE ON THE IMPEDI-
### ments on the part of the craftsman’s soul

#### Chapter 5

We have previously set before you one chapter in which we described, in clear and plain language, the impediments depending on the craftsman’s body. It remains for us to describe briefly the impediments on the part of the soul itself, which most greatly hinder the completion of this work. We say, therefore, that he who does not possess a natural talent <note>original: "ingenium naturale"</note> and a soul that subtly scrutinizes natural principles, the foundations of nature, and the crafts which are able to follow nature in the properties of her actions, will not find the true root of this most precious science. Just as there are many who are stiff-necked <note>meaning stubborn or inflexible</note> and void of talent in all discernment; who can scarcely understand common speech, and likewise learn common public works only with difficulty. Among these we also find many who have a fickle soul, believing any fantasy whatsoever; but what they believe they have found to be true is fantastic, entirely devious from reason, full of error, and remote from natural principles, because their brain, filled with many smoky vapors <note>a reference to the medieval medical theory that physical vapors clouded the mind</note>, cannot receive the true intention of natural things. There are also others besides these who have a shifting soul, moving from opinion to opinion and from will to will; such as those who now believe this thing and want the same, without

<column-break/>

any foundation of generation. Yet after a little while, they believe something else similarly, and want something else. And these are so unstable that they can scarcely complete the smallest part of what they intend, but rather leave it diminished. There are likewise others who cannot see any truth in natural things any more than beasts can, such as the mentally captive <note>insane</note>, the mad, and children. There are others who despise science and do not think it exists; whom science likewise despises, and repels them from the goal of this most precious work. And there are those who are slaves serving money; affirming this wonderful science, yet they fear to incur its expenses. Therefore, although they affirm it and investigate it according to reason, they nevertheless do not arrive at the experience of the work <note>practical experimentation</note> because of the greed for money. To these, therefore, this science of ours does not come. For how shall he who is ignorant of, or neglects to investigate, the science reach it easily?

### DISCOURSE ON THE IMPEDI-
### ments from outside that hinder the work.

#### Chapter 6

We have reduced to two chapters all the impediments delaying the end of this art which arise from the root principles, according to the nature of the craftsman of this precious business. It is expedient, therefore, for us only to describe the impediments coming from the outside from

<summary>This page discusses the psychological and intellectual requirements for a successful alchemist, warning that those with dull minds, unstable wills, or greed will fail to master the "precious science." It categorizes various mental failings—such as stubbornness, fantasy, and indecision—that prevent a practitioner from understanding the laws of nature.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, craftsman's soul, natural talent, impediments, scientific method, greed, reason vs fantasy, medieval psychology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the requirements for the Great Work, focusing on the external and internal obstacles that prevent success in alchemy.</meta>

->BOOK<-

by the contingent fortunes and accidents by which this most glorious Work <note>original: "Opus"; the Great Work of alchemy</note> is hindered. We see, therefore, certain astute and ingenious men, by no means ignorant of the works of nature, who follow her principles and works as closely as possible. Their investigation is not based on fantasy in all those things which are governed by the actions of nature beneath the lunar circle <note>The "sublunary" sphere, or the Earthly realm where things are subject to change and decay</note>. Yet these men, crushed by extreme poverty and a lack of means, are forced to set aside this most excellent Mastery <note>original: "Magisterium"; the completion of the alchemical process</note>. There are also many others besides those just mentioned who are curious, but held back by various cares and anxieties of this kind, occupying themselves entirely with every worldly business; from such people, our precious science flees. Now, therefore, it is sufficiently clear to you from the preceding chapters what the distracting impediments to this art are.

### A DISCOURSE WHICH CONCLUDES the quality of the artist's essence,

### Chapter 7

WE CONCLUDE, therefore, from what has already been said, that it is necessary for the artist <note>The practitioner or alchemist</note> of this work to be naturally learned and perfected in the sciences of philosophy. For no matter how much money and naturally profound genius <note>original: "ingenium"; innate talent or wit</note> he may possess, or how much desire for this craft, he will nonetheless not achieve its end unless he has acquired natural philosophy through formal instruction <note>original: "doctrina"</note>. This is because that which is not attained through natural genius is aided in its deficiency by instruction. It is necessary, therefore, for the artist to be assisted by the deepest scrutiny and natural industry. For no matter how much knowledge he acquires through instruction, unless he is also assisted by natural industry, he will not be invited to such most precious feasts. Indeed, he might correct an error in an instant through his own industry, for which he would not know how to apply a remedy if he were founded upon instruction alone; and similarly, he might remedy an error in an instant through the knowledge acquired by natural instruction, which he could not avoid through industry alone. For art is assisted by genius, and genius is likewise assisted by art. It is also necessary for him to be of a constant will in the work, so that he does not presume to try now this way, now that; for our art does not consist of, nor is it perfected in, a multitude of things. For there is **one stone** and **one medicine** in which the mastery consists, to which we add no foreign thing, nor do we take anything away, except for that which we remove as superfluous during the preparation. It is also necessary for him to persist diligently in the work until its consummation, so that he does not leave the work unfinished; for he would acquire neither knowledge nor profit from a diminished work, but rather despair. It is also expedient that he be not ignorant of the principles and primary roots of this art, which concern the essence of the work. For he who is ignorant of the beginning will not find the end. And we tell you all those principles in a complete discourse, sufficiently open and manifest to the prudent, according to the nature of this

<column-break/>

<note>Vocabulary: Work, Mastery, natural philosophy, artist, genius/talent, instruction, one stone, one medicine, principles, roots</note>

<summary>This chapter outlines the essential qualities of a successful alchemist, emphasizing that one must possess both innate talent (ingenium) and formal education (doctrina), while warning that poverty and worldly distractions are the primary obstacles to completing the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, natural philosophy, the Great Work, the One Stone, alchemical education, poverty, perseverance, mastery</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory requirements for the practitioner of the "Great Work" or alchemy, focusing on the psychological and financial temperament required of the alchemist.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

the requirements of our art: It is similarly expedient for the craftsman to be temperate and slow to anger, lest he suddenly, through an impulse of rage, scatter and destroy what has already been begun. Likewise, let him guard his money, lest he presumptuously distribute it in vain; for if by chance he does not discover the art, he might be left in misery and despair. Or, perhaps, when he has already approached the end of this <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterij"; the completion of the alchemical process</note> through his own diligent searching, his funds might be exhausted, and the wretched man would be forced by poverty to abandon the true goal. This is just as those who, in the beginning, ignorantly waste their entire treasure; when they finally draw near to the secret, they no longer have the means by which to work. Whence I say, that such men are buried in sorrow, both because they squandered their money on useless things, and because they lose the most noble knowledge which they would have soon discovered. For it is not necessary to consume all your goods, since at a low price (if you are not ignorant of the principles of the art, and have rightly understood what we have handed down to you) you shall arrive at the completion of the mastery. If, therefore, you lose your wealth by not attending to our warnings—which we have written for you in this little book in an open and manifest way—do not unfairly gnaw at us, nor join blasphemies against us, but impute it to your own ignorance and presumption. Therefore, this science is not well-suited to the poor or the needy, but rather it is their enemy and adversary. Nor should he strive to find a <term>sophistical</term> <note>In this context, "sophistical" refers to fraudulent or "fake" alchemy—making things that merely look like gold rather than being true gold.</note> goal for the work, but let him be intent on the completion alone, since our art is preserved in the Divine power, and He bestows it or withdraws it from whom He wills. He is glorious and sublime, filled with all justice and goodness; for perhaps, as a punishment for a sophistical work, He might deny the art to you, and thrust you into the path of error, and from error into perpetual unhappiness and misery. Most wretched and unhappy is he to whom God, after the end of his work and labor, always denies the sight of the truth. For such a man always concludes and ends the span of his life in error; established in perpetual labor and obsessed by every misfortune and unhappiness, he loses all the consolation, joy, and delight of this world, and consumes his life in sorrow without profit. Let him likewise study, when he is engaged in the work, to seal in his mind all the signs which appear in any <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctione"; the process of heating or boiling substances in a liquid.</note>, and to inquire into their causes. These, therefore, are the things which are necessary for a craftsman suitable for our art. If, indeed, any one of these things we have narrated should be lacking in him, let him not cling to this art.

<column-break/>

<note>Vocabulary: mastery, decoction, sophistry (deceptive practice), our art (alchemy), the poor man, the craftsman.</note>

# GENERAL DISCOURSE
### Concerning the arguments of the Sophists who deny the art.

### Chapter 8.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial Q featuring a bow and arrow design.</image-desc>

Since in the summary of this little book of ours we have already promised you all the obstacles to this work and have sufficiently given you the adherence to the doctrine of this art, it is now expedient, according to the intention of our proposal, to dispute against the sophists <note>Sophists: here referring to those who use clever but fallacious arguments to deceive others or deny the truth.</note> and the ignorant. First, we shall set forth their own reasons, according to what we promised from the beginning that we would determine. Finally, we shall destroy them all, so that by manifest demonstration it may be clear to wise men that their arguments contain nothing of the truth.

# PARTICULAR DISCOURSE
### Concerning those who deny the art entirely.

### Chapter 9.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial S featuring a vertical staff or rod.</image-desc>

There are, however, various people who deny and seek to destroy the art. Some do so simply <note>Simpliciter: meaning they deny its existence entirely or on fundamental grounds.</note>, others based on the statements of those who suppose it to exist. For there are some asserting that the art does not exist at all, strengthening their sophistical opinion in this way: The species and diversities of things are distinct because the proportions of the elements to one another in their mixture are diverse and distinct. For an ass is different in species from a man because it had a much different proportion of elements in its composition. Thus it is to be inferred in the other diversities of things, and therefore also in minerals. Therefore, if the proportion of the mixable things by which the form and perfection of a thing is attained is unknown, how shall we know how to form the mixture? But we are ignorant of the proportion of the sun <note>Sun: in alchemical terms, this refers to Gold.</note>, the moon <note>Moon: in alchemical terms, this refers to Silver.</note>, or the elements; therefore, we must also be ignorant of the forms in the thing itself. From these points they conclude: This art is useless and impossible. Similarly, they also argue in another way, seeking to destroy our mastery <term>magisterium: the technical mastery or "Great Work" of alchemy</term>. For they say, even if you knew the proportion of the elements, you are ignorant of the manner of their mixing with one another, since nature procreates these things in caverns and mines and hidden places. Therefore, since you are ignorant of the manner of their mixing, you are likewise ignorant of how to do this. Similarly, they argue again: If you knew this properly in the mixtures, you would be ignorant of the action of heat from the agent, by whose mediation the thing is thus perfected. For nature has a certain quantity of heat by which it brings metals into being, the measure of which you do not know. Likewise, you are ignorant of other differences of the acting causes of nature, without which nature could not perfect its intent; therefore, since these are unknown, the entire manner of acting in this art will be likewise unknown. Furthermore, they even bring an argument from experience against you. For this science has been sought by wise men for so long that if it were possible to reach it by any way...

<summary>The author begins a formal defense of alchemy by listing the common arguments used by skeptics, specifically the difficulty of knowing the exact elemental proportions and natural conditions required to create metals.</summary>
<keywords>sophists, elemental proportions, gold, silver, minerals, alchemy, natural heat, magisterium</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical debate regarding the possibility of alchemy, specifically addressing the arguments against the transmutation of metals.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

->reached: for by now they would have completed it a thousand times over. Similarly, since the philosophers have not dared to hand down this knowledge in their volumes, nor do we find the manifest truth within them, it is probable enough from this that this science does not exist. Likewise, many princes of this world, possessing infinite treasure and a wealth of philosophers, have desired to discover this art; yet they have not been able to reach the fruit of this most precious art. This is certainly a sufficient argument that the art is frivolous. <note>The author is presenting the "Skeptical" position here, arguing that if alchemy were real, the rich and powerful would have already succeeded.</note> **Proof.** Similarly, we are not able to follow nature in the delicate mixing of species. For we do not know how to fashion a donkey, or other similar things, whose mixtures are weak and manifest to the senses. Therefore, we will be much more ignorant of how to fashion the mixture of metals (which is the strongest), which is also entirely hidden from our senses and experiences. A sign of this is the difficulty of resolving the elements from them. Similarly, we do not see an ox transformed into a goat, nor any other species reduced into another by any other artifice. How then, since metals differ in species, do you strive to transform them into one another according to species, so that you make such a species from another? This seems absurd enough to us, and remote from the truth given by natural principles. Similarly, nature perfects metals over thousands of years; how can you endure through thousands of years in the artifice of transmutation, when you can scarcely reach the limit of a hundred years? If, however, you were to respond in this way: "That which nature cannot perfect in a great space of time, we fulfill in a short time through our artifice, because artifice completes the defect of nature in many things." We say, however, that this is impossible, especially in metals, since they are most subtle substances; for they require a tempered <term>decoction</term><note>A process of heating or "cooking" a substance to extract its essence or mature it.</note> so that their own humidity may be thickened within themselves according to equality, and not flee from them and leave them deprived of all humidity, by which they sustain striking and extension <note>Referring to malleability—the ability of metal to be hammered and stretched.</note>. If, therefore, through your artifice you wish to shorten the time of nature's decoction in minerals and metallic bodies, it will be necessary to do this through an excess of heat; which will not balance, but rather dissipate and destroy the humidity by dissolving it from their bodies. For only tempered heat thickens humidity and perfects the mixture, not an exceeding fire. Similarly, being and perfection are given by the stars, as the primary perfectors and movers of the matter of corruption and generation, toward the non-being and being of species. This happens suddenly and in an instant, when one or more stars arrive through their motions at a determined position in the firmament, from which the "being" of perfection is granted; because every single thing acquires its being in a moment from a certain position of the stars. And there is not just one position, but many, and diverse from one another, just as their effects are diverse.

# BOOK

And we cannot summon the diversity and distinction of these [celestial bodies] from one another, since they are unknown and infinite to us. How then will you supply the defect in your work arising from ignorance of the diverse positions of the stars and their motion? And yet, even if you knew the certain position of one or more stars by which perfection is granted to metals, you would still not complete the work to your intent. For the preparation of any work to receive its form through artifice does not happen in an instant, but is successive <note>meaning it happens in stages over time</note>. Therefore, the form will not be given to the work since it is not instantaneous. Similarly, this is the order in natural things: for it is easier to destroy a cause than to build one. But we can scarcely destroy gold; how then do we presume to construct it? Beyond these sophistical arguments, and others less apparent than these, they believe they can kill this divine art. These are all the persuasions of the sophists, who simply deny that our art exists. I shall set down the arguments of those who deny the art based on supposition, along with the destruction of those arguments, in what follows. But from here, we must move to the killing of these arguments just posed, first setting forth our true intention for the completion of the work.

### Chapter 10.

We say, therefore, that the principles upon which nature founds its action are of diverse composition and very strong: and they are **sulfur** and **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum," the liquid metal mercury</note>, as certain of the philosophers say. Therefore, because they are of such diverse composition, they are most difficult to resolve. But so that their thickening <note>original: "inspissatio"</note> and hardening may occur in such a way that they can withstand striking and extension by the driving of a hammer without shattering, it happens only through this: that the viscous moisture in their mutual mixture is dissolved through successive cooking <note>original: "decoctionem"</note> in the mine. But we hand down to you a general rule, dearest son: that the thickening of any moisture does not occur unless there is first an exhalation of the most subtle parts of the moisture, and a preservation of the coarser parts of the moisture—provided it is a mixture where the moisture exceeds the dry, and a true mixture of dry and moist. Thus the moist is tempered by the dry, and the dry by the moist, and this substance becomes one in its parts, entirely pure and tempered between hard and soft, and extensible when struck. But this does not happen except through the long-lasting mixing of viscous moisture and subtle earthiness through their smallest parts, until the moisture becomes one with the dry, and the dry with the moist. And of such a subtle vapor, there should be no sudden resolution, but rather a gradual one, and in...

<summary>The author refutes skeptical "sophists" who argue alchemy is impossible due to astronomical complexity, then begins explaining the chemical principles of sulfur and mercury as the foundation of metals.</summary>

# BOOK ONE
->VII<-

for thousands of years. This occurs because the substance of nature's principles is uniform; if the release <note>original: "resolutio," referring here to the breakdown or evaporation of matter</note> of excess moisture happened suddenly, the moisture would not be able to separate from the dry matter due to the powerful bond they share. Instead, the moisture would dissolve along with the dry matter, and the whole substance would vanish into smoke. Because of the strong union they have with one another, the wet cannot be separated from the dry during a sudden release. We see clear evidence of this in the sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again</note> of spirits; when a sudden release is triggered through sublimation, the wet does not separate from the dry, nor the dry from the wet in a way that their mixtures are totally divided into parts. Rather, their entire substance rises together, or very little of their components is dis-

<column-break/>

-solved. Therefore, this kind of subtle, smoky release—which is successive, long-lasting, and steady—is the true cause of the thickening <note>original: "inspissationis," the process of a liquid becoming dense or solid</note> of metals. We are unable to produce this thickening in the same manner. Therefore, in this specific process, we lack the power to follow nature. Indeed, we cannot imitate nature in every specific detail of its actions. Our intention, therefore, is not to follow nature in its initial principles, nor in the exact proportion of the elements to be mixed, nor in the specific way they are blended together, nor in the balanced application of the thickening heat. All these things are impossible for us and entirely unknown. It remains for us, then, to destroy the arguments of the sophists <note>Here referring to those who use clever but false arguments to deny the validity of alchemy</note> who, in their ignorance, deny this most excellent science.

### Chapter 10.

We say, therefore, that the principles upon which nature founds its action are of diverse composition and very strong: and they are sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note>, as some of the philosophers say. Therefore, because they are of very diverse composition, they are very difficult to resolve. But so that their thickening and hardening may occur in such a way that they can withstand striking and extension by the compulsion of the hammer <note>malleability</note> without shattering, it happens only through this: that the viscous moisture in their mutual mixture is dissolved through successive decoction <note>term: decoctio; a process of heating or boiling to extract essence or mature a substance</note> in the mine. Thus the moist is tempered by the dry, and the dry by the moist; and this substance becomes one in its parts, entirely pure and tempered between hard and soft, and extensible under striking. But this does not happen except through the long-lasting mixture of viscous moisture and subtle earthiness down to the smallest parts, until the moisture becomes one with the dry, and the dry with the moist. And the resolution of this kind of subtle vapor does not happen suddenly; rather, it happens gradually, and in...

for thousands of years. In such a case, the moisture would be dissolved along with the dryness, as if the whole were vanishing into smoke. ### A DISCOURSE ON THE SO-
### lutions to the sophists
### who deny the
### art.

->❦<-

### Chapter 11.

They have said, therefore, that we are ignorant of the proportion of the elements, the manner of their mixing with one another, the balancing of heat in the thickening of metals, and the natural accidents that follow such actions. We certainly grant this to them; but they do not destroy our divine science <note>The author refers to alchemy as a "divine science" to elevate it above mere chemical recipes.</note> because of this, since we neither wish to know those things, nor are we able to, nor can they be applied to our work. Instead, we adopt another principle for this, and another method for the generation of metals, in which we are able to follow nature. If they also say that the philosophers and princes of this world have desired this knowledge and have not found it, we respond that they lie. For it is well established that certain princes (though few) and especially the ancients and the wise men found in our own time, have already investigated this science through their own industry. However, they did not wish to hand it down to such people, neither by word of mouth nor by writings, since they are unwor-

<column-break/>

-thy of it. Therefore, because they have not seen anyone possessing this science, error has fallen upon their minds, leading them to estimate that no one has found this knowledge. Furthermore, if they argue fantastically, asserting our impotence by claiming we cannot imitate nature even in "weak mixtures"—such as in the mixing <note>The biological generation or breeding</note> of a donkey or an ox—and therefore cannot do so in "strong" ones <note>The durable, inorganic structure of minerals</note>, we expose their error in many ways. We show that there is no logical necessity in their mode of arguing that would force us to concede our art does not exist. They strengthen their own fantasy and error by admitting arguments from similarity or from the greater to the lesser, which contain no necessity, but only contingency, as is most often the case. We also show, by demonstrating through another path, that they assign no apparent similarity between the weak composition of the mixture of animals and the firm and strong composition of minerals. And this is because in animals and other living things, where the composition is weak, the perfection is not achieved solely by proportion, nor by the things mixed in proportion, nor by the qualities of the things mixed, nor by the commingling that follows from their mutual action and passion (which arises from the aggregation of those primary qualities). Rather, it is the soul <note>original: "anima"</note>, according to the opinion of many, which comes from the secrets of nature, such as from the fifth essence <term>Quintessence: the purest, fifth element beyond earth, air, fire, and water</term> or from the Prime Mover. We say this according to the opinion of many, and we are ignorant of this hidden thing. Therefore, although there is a "weak mixture" in these living things, we do not know how to perfect them, because we do not know how to infuse that which makes them perfect: the soul.

<summary>The author defends alchemy against critics (sophists) by arguing that while humans cannot create life (souls), they can master the "strong" compositions of minerals and metals through different natural principles. He asserts that the art is real but kept secret by the wise from those who are unworthy.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, sophists, metals, generation of minerals, soul, quintessence, logic, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of alchemy, specifically addressing the differences between biological life and mineral perfection.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

Through this it is certainly clear that it is not a defect on the part of the mixture <note>original: "mixtio"; the physical blending of elements</note> that we cannot create an ox or a goat, but rather from a defect in the infusion of the soul. For we would know how to make a composition—weak or weaker, strong or stronger—by following the way and course of nature and imitating our own craft. In metals, therefore, the perfection is less than in those living things, and their perfection is concerned more with proportion and composition than with anything else. Therefore, since there is less perfection in them than in the others we have already described, we can perfect them more freely, whereas we cannot do so with the others. Thus, God the Most High and Glorious diversifies perfections from one another in many forms. For in those things where the composition according to nature was weak, He placed a greater and nobler perfection, which is according to the soul. But He established some things of a stronger and firmer composition, such as stones and minerals, yet in them He placed a lesser and more ignoble perfection, which arises from the manner of the mixture. It is clear, then, that the comparison made by critics is not a good one; for we are not ignorant of how to form an ox or a goat by reason of its physical composition, but rather by its perfecting form <note>The "form" here refers to the Aristotelian concept of the soul or essence that makes a thing what it is</note>, because the perfection in an ox or a goat is nobler and more hidden than that which exists in a metal. If, however, they argue otherwise—that species is not changed into species—we say they lie again, as they are accustomed to do more often than they speak the truth on these matters. For indeed, species is changed into species according to this path, when an individual of one species is changed into

<column-break/>

another. For we see both naturally and through natural craft that a worm is changed into a fly, which differs from it in species; and a strangled calf into bees <note>A reference to the ancient belief in "bugonia," where life was thought to spontaneously generate from decaying carcasses</note>; and wheat into tares; and a strangled dog into worms through the boiling of putrefaction <note>term: putrefactio; the breakdown of matter which alchemists believed was necessary for new growth</note>. But we do not do this ourselves; rather, nature does it, whom we serve as ministers. Similarly, we do not change metals, but nature does, for whom we prepare the material according to our craft; for nature acts through itself, not us. We are truly its administrators. And if they argue by another reason to sophistically strengthen their opinion—that nature perfects metals over thousands of years, while you cannot endure for thousands—we say that nature, acting upon its own principles according to the opinion of the ancient philosophers, perfects them in thousands of years. But because we are not able to follow those specific principles, it does not matter whether nature perfects these things in thousands of years, or more, or fewer, or in a moment; their persuasion does not hold. That we cannot imitate nature in its initial principles we have already determined briefly in the preceding negative discourse, and we shall demonstrate it with a more complete discourse in the following sections. Nevertheless, according to the opinion of some evident and perceptive men, nature perfects its intent suddenly: namely, in one day or a shorter time. Even if this is true, we are still not able to imitate nature in its starting principles, which we have proven sufficiently by manifest demonstration; however, the remainder of this...

<summary>The author argues that alchemy is possible because metals have a simpler "perfection" than living animals, and asserts that species can indeed change into one another, as seen in spontaneous generation.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, species transformation, spontaneous generation, nature vs art, mineral composition, putrefaction, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the theoretical possibility of alchemy and the role of celestial influence on the formation of metals. This page continues the defense of the "art" (alchemy) against skeptics.</meta>

# BOOK

->**Nature**<-

<column-break/>

Regarding the question, we confess that we concede the whole truth of it. And if they say that a perfection is given to metals by the position of one or more stars—a perfection which we do not know—we say that it is not necessary for us to know this position. For there is no species of things that can be generated or corrupted <note>original: "generabilium & corruptibilium"; referring to the Aristotelian concept of physical things that are born and decay</note> that does not undergo generation and corruption through some of its individuals every day. By this, it is clearly evident that the position of the stars is every day perfecting and corrupting some individual of every species. Therefore, it is not necessary to wait for a specific position of the stars; it would indeed be useful, but it is sufficient only to arrange the nature of the thing, so that nature itself, which is wise, may arrange it with the fitting positions of moving bodies. For nature cannot bring a thing to perfection without the motion and position of moving bodies. 

Wherefore, if you have arranged the artifice of nature and have duly considered whatever contingencies belong to this mastery <note>term: "magisterij" (Mastery); a common alchemical term for the Great Work or the process of transmutation</note>, the work will be perfected under the due position through the nature fitting to it, without further consideration of the stars. For when we see a worm brought into being from a dog or another rot-prone animal, we do not immediately consider the position of the stars, but rather the dispositions of the surrounding air and other causes that perfect the putrefaction, aside from the stars. And from such consideration, we are able to produce worms into being according to nature. For nature finds the fitting position for itself, even if it is unknown to us. 

And if they say that perfection is given in an instant, but our preparation does not happen in an instant, and they conclude from this that it will not be perfected by artifice, and therefore the art is false: We say that their heads must be empty of human 

<column-break/>

reason, and that they are more like beasts than men. For they conclude from premises that have no relation to the conclusion drawn. This argument holds as much weight as saying: "A donkey runs, therefore you are a goat." And this is because, even if the preparation does not happen in an instant, this does not prevent the form or perfection from being given to the prepared material in an instant. For the preparation is not the perfection itself, but the enabling <note>original: "habilitatio"</note> to receive the form. 

But if they also say that it is easier to destroy natural things than to construct them by artifice, and since we can hardly destroy gold, they conclude it is impossible to construct it: We respond to them that they do not conclude by necessity that we are forced to believe gold cannot be constructed. For while it is destroyed with difficulty, it is constructed with even greater difficulty; however, it is not impossible for it to be constructed. We assign the cause of this to be that it has a strong composition, and therefore it has a more difficult resolution <note>term: "resolutionem" (Resolution); the chemical breaking down of a substance into its component parts</note>, and thus it is destroyed with difficulty. And this is what makes them opine that its construction is impossible, because they are ignorant of artificial destruction according to the course of nature. They perhaps attempted it, but because it is of a strong composition, they did not succeed. 

Therefore, my dearest son, we have sufficiently brought to you the fantasies of the sophists. It remains, therefore, since we have made you attentive by our promise, to come to those things to be determined according to the path of the promise, which are the principles of the intention of nature, the essence of which we must...

<summary>The author defends alchemy against skeptics by arguing that nature (not just astrology) governs the perfection of metals and that the difficulty of creating gold does not make it impossible. He dismisses the "sophists'" logical fallacies, comparing their arguments to nonsense about donkeys and goats.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, astrology, generation and corruption, perfection of metals, gold, nature vs art, sophistry, chemical resolution</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discourse on the principles of the alchemical art, transitioning from a general promise of future proofs to a specific critique of the various schools of thought and their errors.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
<note>original: "PRIMVS"</note>

We shall dispute more sufficiently in the following sections. After that determination, we shall also speak of those things which are the principles of our <term>mastery</term> <note>magisterium: the supreme skill or "Great Work" of alchemy</note>. In the first tradition, we shall speak specifically of each of the principles; in the following, we shall speak universally. For now, let us first bring forward the arguments of those who deny the art based on their assumptions, and the refutations of those arguments.

---

->❦<-

### A UNIVERSAL DISCOURSE on the arguments of the sophists, who deny the art based on false assumptions:

#### Chapter 12.

Assuming this art to exist, we find it to be multifaceted according to various intentions. Some indeed place it in <term>spirits</term> <note>spiritibus: volatile substances that vaporize when heated, such as mercury or sulfur</note>. Others in <term>bodies</term> <note>corporibus: solid metals</note>. Others in salts, alums, nitres, and boraxes; while others affirm that this art and mastery is found in all vegetable things. Out of all these, some have handed down this divine mastery to posterity by judging it well in part and poorly in part, while others have judged it poorly in its entirety. From the multiplicities of their errors, we have gathered the truth through the conjecture of difficult and laborious caution, and through long and

<column-break/>

tedious experience involving many expenses. Their error very often campaigned against the disposition and reason of our mind and brought us to despair. May they be cursed forever, because they left blasphemies to posterity; out of their own error, they poured confusion over those studying philosophy, and after their deaths, they left behind not the truth, but rather a diabolical instigation. And I should be cursed myself, unless I correct their errors and hand down the truth in this science, as this art best requires. For this mastery needs neither a hidden speech nor one that is entirely manifest. We hand it down, therefore, in such a speech that it will not happen to be hidden from the wise; yet this same tradition will be most profound to the mediocre, and for the foolish, it will conclude their boundaries beyond recovery. Returning therefore to the proposal, we say that those who place the art in "spirits" are diverse in many ways. Some assert it is necessary to seek the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>lapidem philosophorum: the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</note> from <term>quicksilver</term> <note>argento viuo: mercury</note>. Others from sulfur and its relative, arsenic. And among these, some seek it from <term>marcasite</term> <note>marchesita: iron pyrites or similar metallic minerals</note>, some from <term>tutty</term> <note>tutia: zinc oxide</note> and <term>magnesia</term> <note>magnesia: likely manganese ore or a similar dark mineral</note>, and some from <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>sale amoniaco: ammonium chloride</note>. Those who place it in "bodies" say it is in lead, or in any of the other metallic bodies. Others say it is in glass, and some in gems, and others in diversities of salts, alums, nitres, and boraxes; and others in every kind of vegetable thing. And to each one of those making an assumption, there is an adversary according to his own supposition.

<summary>The author critiques the various schools of alchemy that mistakenly seek the Philosophers' Stone in specific substances like mercury, sulfur, or plants, promising to reveal the truth through a balanced "middle" style of writing.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Philosophers' Stone, spirits, metallic bodies, mercury, sulfur, arsenic, chemical salts, alchemical errors, mastery</keywords>

# BOOK

And whoever opposes these things believes in a simple art: they are thrice opposed. And for the most part, both sects are void of reasoning.

### DISCOURSE ON VE-
### getables by suppo-
### sition in sul-
### phur.
<note>The term "vegetables" here likely refers to the "growing" or "living" quality of minerals in alchemical theory, rather than plants.</note>

#### Chapter xiii.

Certain people, therefore, supposing that they could find it <note>the Elixir or Stone</note> in sulphur, expended their labor on sulphur; and being ignorant of the perfection of its preparation, they left that preparation incomplete. For they believed that cleansing and purification alone would constitute the perfection of the preparation. This is done through sublimation <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again, used to purify minerals</term>, and therefore it was brought to their minds to think that sublimation alone in sulphur is the perfection of its preparation. They introduced the same thinking regarding its partner, namely in arsenic. Coming therefore to the "projection" <note>the act of casting the alchemical medicine onto base metal to transform it</note> which is intended for alteration, they saw that the substance burned away and vanished, failing to remain for any long duration within the bodies of the metals. Indeed, the metals were left by them more impure than they were before the projection. Because they saw this delusion at the

<column-break/>

completion of their work—and for a very long time it had revolved in their hearts that this science was to be found in sulphur alone—when they did not find it therein, they argued that it could not be found in anything else. Therefore, since it is found neither in this nor in another, they argue it is found nowhere at all.

---

### DISCOURSE ON THE RE-
### sponse to the afore-
### said.

#### Chapter xiv.

We respond to them briefly, and we say that they understand little in this matter and have very little wisdom, because they suppose that sulphur alone is our "matter" <term>materia nostra: the "Our Matter" or starting substance of the Alchemists, often distinguished from common store-bought chemicals</term>. And even if this supposition were true, they are nevertheless deceived in the method of preparation, because they believe sublimation alone to be sufficient. It is as if a boy, from the beginning of his birth until old age, were shut up in a house, not thinking the breadth of the world extended beyond the width of his house or beyond what he could perceive with his eye. For these men have not applied their labor to many stones; therefore, they could not perceive from what source our matter—

<summary>This page critiques alchemists who mistakenly believe that sulphur or arsenic alone, purified only by sublimation, can achieve the Great Work. The author argues that their failure leads them to falsely conclude that alchemy itself is impossible.</summary>
<keywords>sulphur, arsenic, sublimation, projection, alchemical preparation, critique of alchemists, materia nostra</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's systematic rebuttal of those who deny the possibility of alchemy based on their own failed experiments with specific minerals.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<margin>Signature: B iiij</margin>
<meta>catchword: for</meta>

...elicits our [results], and from what it does not; since they have even excused their own hands from an abundance of labors, they have deservedly remained ignorant of what truly perfects the work and what does not. But as to why their work was diminished, we say it is because they left behind the "burning quality" <note>original: "adurentiam" - referring to the corrosive or combustible properties of impure sulfur</note> in the sulfur and the "flight" <note>original: "fugam" - volatility; the tendency of a substance to evaporate or escape when heated</note> which not only fail to complete the work, but actually scatter and destroy it.

#### Chapter 15.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'A' containing a geometric diagram of a circle inscribed with a triangle, with a horizontal line passing through the apex of the triangle.</image-desc>
Others, however, believing that this stone is to be found in arsenic and its companion <note>likely referring to orpiment or realgar, minerals often found with arsenic</note>, and attending more deeply to the consummation of the work, did not only cleanse the burning sulfurousness through <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then collecting the purified solid as it cools</note>. They also attempted to remove the "earthiness," yet left the "flight" <insert>volatility</insert> within it; and upon the <term>projection</term> <note>The final stage of alchemy where the elixir is cast upon base metal to transform it</note> of these substances, a similar delusion occurred. This is because it did not adhere stable-

<column-break/>

-ly in those very bodies, but vanished successively or little by little, leaving the body in its prior state. These people argued against the art just as the first group did; and responding to them as we did the first, we affirm the art, and we know it to exist, because we have seen it and touched the truth of it.

#### Chapter 16.

Certain others looked more deeply into this matter; they cleansed it and took away both the "flight" and the "burning," and it became for them fixed and earthy, giving no good fusion by the heat of fire, but only a <term>vitrifying</term> <note>Turning into a glass-like substance rather than a metallic melt</note> effect. Therefore, they were unable to mix it into the bodies during projection. Thus they argue like the first group, and we respond to them as we did the first: because they left the work diminished and did not know how to complete it. For the "Ingression" <note>The ability of the alchemical agent to penetrate and permeate a metal</note> which is the perfection—

<summary>The author critiques failed alchemists who worked with arsenic and sulfur, explaining that their failure to balance volatility and fixation led them to wrongly conclude that alchemy is impossible.</summary>
<keywords>arsenic, sulfur, sublimation, projection, volatility, fixation, vitrification, alchemical theory</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the general principles of preparing mineral spirits; this page continues by comparing the specific qualities of different substances and addressing those who doubt the alchemical art due to failed experiments.</meta>

# BOOK

In all other <term>spirits</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are volatile substances that vaporize when heated, such as mercury or sulfur.</note> the method of preparation is similar, except that in the case of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argento vivo"; mercury</note> and <term>tutty</term> <note>original: "tutiis"; zinc oxide collected from the flues of furnaces</note> we are spared the greater labor of removing "combustibility." These substances do not possess a combustible or flammable sulfurous nature, but only the quality of "flight" <note>volatility; the tendency to evaporate</note>. Every kind of <term>magnesia</term> <note>likely manganese ore or a similar dark mineral</note> and <term>marcasite</term> <note>iron pyrites</note> possesses a sulfurous nature—marcasite more so, and magnesia less—yet all of them possess flight. Quicksilver and <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>ammonium chloride</note> have the most flight; sulfur has less; even less than that is its companion <insert>arsenic</insert> and sal ammoniac; fourthly, marcasite has still less; fifthly, magnesia has less than that; and sixthly and finally, tutty has the least of all. Each participates in flight, some more and some less. Therefore, because of this flight, a powerful delusion overcomes certain experimenters in their operations of preparation and likewise in their attempts to protect these substances. Consequently, they argue against the art and perish <note>or "fail"</note> as they do when assuming things about sulfur; we respond to them just as we do to those making assumptions about sulfur.

<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish resembling a horizontal figure-eight or infinity symbol with extended loops.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

### DISCOURSE ON THOSE WHO DENY
->the art by making assumptions regarding the administration of all spirits with bodies.<-

### Chapter 17.

There are others who strive in their experiments to "fix" <note>to make a volatile substance stable under heat</note> the spirits into the <term>bodies</term> <note>the solid metals</note> without any other preparation preceding this. But likewise, delusion has brought upon them a distressing fear and desperation, and they are forced by this to believe that this science does not exist and to argue against it. The cause of their confusion here is well-known: during the melting of the bodies, those spirits will leave them and not adhere to them; rather, they flee the harshness of the fire, leaving only the bodies remaining in it. This happens because they cannot endure the pressure of the fire's "cruelty" due to their flight, which was not removed from them. Similarly, delusion sometimes occurs because the spirits carry the bodies away from the fire with them; this happens when the spirits, not being fixed, have not adhered inseparably to the depths of the bodies, for the total amount of the volatile overcomes the total amount of the fixed. Hence, they argue just as the first group did, and we respond to them just as we did to the first. Their entire error is this: If you wish to transform the bodies, O sons of doctrine, then if it be possible for this to be done by any material, it must necessarily be done by the spirits themselves.

<summary>This page discusses the varying degrees of volatility (flight) in alchemical spirits like mercury and sulfur, warning that failure to "fix" these spirits leads to failed experiments and skepticism toward alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>volatility, fixation, quicksilver, tutty, marcasite, magnesia, sal ammoniac, alchemical spirits, transformation of metals</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and chemical debate regarding the possibility of transmutation, specifically addressing those who fail to achieve the "art" due to a lack of complete knowledge or improper methods.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

But it is not possible for those [spirits] that are not fixed to adhere usefully to bodies; rather, they flee and leave those bodies impure. Furthermore, it is not possible for those that are fixed to enter into them, since they have become like earth, which does not melt. And when they appear fixed within the clothed bodies, they are not truly so; either they withdraw from the bodies while the bodies remain, or both flee together. Since it is in no way possible to find this art in a more closely related matter <note>original: "materia affini"</note>, it will certainly not be found in one more remote; therefore, it is found nowhere. Our response to this is that they do not know the whole of what is knowable concerning this matter, and therefore the work resulting from it is not found. Consequently, they place the strength of their argument on a flaw arising from their own insufficiency.

### A GENERAL DISCOURSE
### Concerning those who deny the art
### based on its application
### within bodies:

#### Chapter 18.

<note>Decorative woodcut initial Q featuring a bow and arrow.</note>
Certain people, however, have placed that art within bodies. Yet when they came to the work, they were deceived; for instance, they thought that both types of tin <note>The author uses "stagnum" to refer to tin or alloys; "vtrunq̃" likely refers to lead and tin as "two types of tin" in the archaic sense</note>—namely the bluish-gray one, which is lead, and the white one of impure whiteness—were very similar and close to the nature of the sun <note>gold</note> and the moon <note>silver</note>. They believed the bluish-gray lead was very close to gold but little to silver, while the white tin was very close to silver but little to gold.

<column-break/>

### A SPECIFIC DISCOURSE
### Concerning those who
### deny the art based
### on its application
### in tin.

->❦<-

#### Chapter 19.

<note>Decorative woodcut initial E featuring a seated figure and foliage.</note>
Because of these things, some—supposing that tin (or Jupiter <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal tin</term>) was very similar to the moon <note>silver</note>, differing only in its "cry" <note>The "tin cry" is the characteristic cracking sound made when bending a bar of tin</note>, its softness, and the speed of its melting—believed it was easily melted and soft solely due to an excess of its humidity. They thought its "cry" was caused by parts of fugitive <term>fugitive: volatile or easily evaporated</term> quicksilver <note>original: "ar. vi." for argentum vivum</note> trapped within it. They exposed it to fire, and by calcining <term>calcining: heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile parts or oxidize it</term> it, they kept it in the fire as long as it could endure until it turned into a white powder <note>original: "calce," literally lime or ash</note>. When they later wished to reduce <term>reduce: to return a metal from its powdered/oxidized state back to a solid metallic form</term> this powder, they were unable to do so, and thus judged it to be impossible. And some of them did reduce a small portion of it, but they found the same "cry," softness, and speed of melting as before.

Therefore, they believed this path to be impossible and were led into unbelief, thinking that the art of its hardening <note>original: "induratiõis," referring to making the metal harder and more like silver</note> could never be found.

<summary>This page discusses the failures of alchemists who attempt to transmute lead and tin into gold and silver without full knowledge. It specifically describes the failed attempt to "harden" tin by calcining it to remove its "cry" and softness.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, tin, lead, calcination, transmutation, Jupiter, silver, tin cry, reduction, chemical properties</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of alchemy against skeptics and failed practitioners, specifically addressing those who failed to transform lead and other metals due to improper techniques.</meta>

# BOOK
A
<meta>Catchword at bottom right: "pora". Faint bleed-through from reverse page visible.</meta>

Indeed, some of these men have calcined <note>term: calcination; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often reducing it to a powder or 'calx'</note> and reduced <note>term: reduction; the chemical process of returning a calcined powder back into a solid metal state</note> the materials, and by drawing away the dross <note>original: "scoriam"</note> with the application of a greater fire, they calcined and reduced them again. By repeating this work often, they saw the material had become hardened and without its "shriek" <note>The "shriek" or "cry" of tin (stridor) was a characteristic sound made when bending the metal; its absence suggested a change in the metal's nature.</note>. However, because they did not entirely remove the speed of its melting <note>original: "liquefactionis"</note>, their minds erred, and they judged that the goal could not be reached. Others among them, wishing to grant the same material hardness and a delay in melting by adding "hard bodies" <note>likely referring to metals like iron or copper</note>, fell into a delusion; for whatever hard bodies were mixed into it were shattered, and no preparation helped them in this. Therefore, since they could prepare it neither with hard things nor with fire, they excused themselves for the long delay in discovering the art, saying and believing it to be impossible. Through this, they argued urgently against the art, positing that it does not exist. From this point, adding many "medicines" <note>term: medicine; in alchemy, a substance or elixir added to a base metal to trigger transformation</note>, they saw that these caused no change and were not suitable, but rather corrupted the work and acted against their purpose. Therefore, they cast away their books, turned their heads away, and said the art was frivolous. To these men, we shall respond with our first answer.

### DISCOURSE CONCERNING THOSE WHO DENY
->the art based on their assumptions<-
->regarding lead.<-

#### Chapter 20.

<column-break/>

The same mode of delusion is found regarding lead <note>original: "plumbo"</note>, with only this added: it does not shatter other bodies, and it returns from its calcined state more quickly than Jupiter <note>note: "Jupiter" is the alchemical name for Tin</note>. Yet they cannot remove its lividity <note>the dull, bluish-gray color of lead</note> because they are ignorant; therefore, they cannot whiten it with a good whitening <note>original: "dealbatione"</note>, nor could they, through their own imagination, stably associate it with stable bodies. Instead, it happens that through a strong application of fire, it withdraws from the mixture. And that which most deceives them in this preparation is the assumption that the science can be found in nothing except that which, after two reductions from its calcined state, receives no hardness, but rather a greater softness than it had before. Similarly, they did not see it improved by other differences. And so, while thinking they would find the goal more closely and better in this material, and yet failing to find it, they are compelled by this to believe and argue that the science is not real, but a delusion; and thus they sin just as the former men did.

Others, indeed, by composing hard things with hard, and soft with soft, wished for the bodies to be transmuted and trans- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "transmutari," likely continuing as "transformed" on the next page.</note>

# BOOK ONE

to change them, and they were unable to do so because of their ignorance. For example, when mixing Sun <note>Gold</note> or Moon <note>Silver</note> with Venus <note>Copper</note> or any other of the metals, they do not transform them into Sun or Moon with a firm transmutation; rather, it is necessary that each of them be separated from the mixture by the strong expression of fire, and be burned away or return to its former nature. Some of these indeed last longer in the mixture, while others last less, as has been sufficiently determined by us. Therefore, these delusions arising from ignorance cause such people to distrust this art and to argue that it does not exist.

### A SPECIFIC DISCOURSE Concerning those who deny the art based on the assumption of the further management of hard metals with soft ones.

#### Chapter 22.

Others, inquiring into this more closely and deeply, have thought and devised, and wished to find a way so that hard things united with soft things might subtly harden them, and that perfect things might lead the imperfect back to perfection, and generally that they might be mutually transformed and achieve a firm transmutation. And for this reason, they wished to find the similarity and affinity between them, sometimes indeed through medicines <note>Chemical reagents or "elixirs"</note>

<column-break/>

and at other times through the administration of fire, by thinning the coarse metals—such as Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars <note>Iron</note>—and by thickening the subtle ones, such as Jupiter <note>Tin</note> and those similar to it. And some of them, believing they could perfect this administration, were deceived in the mixing of these hard bodies, either because they made them entirely brittle, or entirely too soft (not altered by the hard), or too hard (not altered by the soft). And thus they did not find the proper agreement, and therefore they denied that the art exists.

### A DISCOURSE CONCERNING THOSE WHO DENY the art by the assumption of its final administration of hard metals with soft ones.

#### Chapter 23.

<note>The decorative initial 'A' contains a circle with a horizontal line, a symbol often representing Salt or the horizon in alchemical geometry.</note>

Others indeed, looking even more deeply, wished to alter the bodies <note>Metals</note> by the extraction of their souls <note>Volatile spirits or essential properties</note>, and with the soul extracted, to alter all things likewise. And their experience could not reach this goal, but they were deceived in their intention, estimating by this that the art could not be found. Others indeed, attempting to perfect these things by fire alone, were deceived in their opinion because they did not know how to arrive at that result. And these people, for this reason, thought the art did not exist. To all of whom we respond as we did to the first.

### DISCOURSE ON THOSE WHO DENY the art based on their assumptions regarding lead.

#### Chapter 20.

<column-break/>

The same mode of deception is found regarding lead <note>original: plumbum</note>, with this addition: it does not shatter other bodies, and it returns from its ash <note>original: calce; literally "lime," referring to the metallic powder produced by calcination.</note> more quickly than Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note>. And so, when they thought they would find the goal more closely and easily in this metal and did not find it, they were forced to believe and argue that the science is not real, but a delusion; and thus they err as the previous men did.
