Others, inquiring into this more closely and deeply, thought and devised—and wished to find a way—so that hard things united with soft things might subtly harden them, and that perfect things might lead the imperfect back to perfection, and generally that they might be mutually transformed and achieve a stable transmutation. And for this reason, they wished to find the similarity and affinity between them, sometimes indeed through "medicines" <note>Chemical reagents or elixirs</note>

<column-break/>

and sometimes through the administration of fire, by thinning the coarse metals—such as "Venus" <note>Copper</note> and "Mars" <note>Iron</note>—and by thickening the subtle ones, such as "Jupiter" <note>Tin</note> and those similar to it. And some of them, believing they could perfect this processing, were deceived in the mixing of these hard bodies; either because they made them entirely brittle, or entirely too soft (not altered by the hard), or too hard (not altered by the soft). And thus they did not find the proper harmony, and for that reason, they denied that the art exists.

### A DISCOURSE CONCERNING THOSE WHO DENY the art by the assumption of its final processing of hard metals with soft ones.

#### Chapter 23.

<note>The decorative initial 'A' contains a circle with a horizontal line, a symbol often representing Salt or the horizon in alchemical diagrams.</note>

Others indeed, looking even more deeply, wished to alter the "bodies" <note>Metals</note> by the extraction of their "souls" <note>Volatile spirits or essential properties</note>, and with the soul extracted, to alter all things likewise. And their experience was not able to reach that goal, but they were deceived in their intention, estimating by this that the art could not be found. Others indeed, attempting to perfect these things by fire alone, were deceived in their opinion because they did not know how to arrive at that result. And these men, for this reason, thought the art did not exist. To all of whom we respond as we did to the first group.

<note>Technical Vocabulary: Specific Discourse (Sermo particularis), Transmutation (transmutatio), Gold (sol), Silver (luna), Copper (venus), Iron (mars), Tin (jupiter), Metallic bodies (corpora), Spirits/Essences (animae), Alchemy (alchimia)</note>

<summary>This page discusses the failures of amateur alchemists who attempt to transmute metals without understanding their chemical affinities or the proper balance between hard and soft elements. The author argues that their failure to achieve a "stable transmutation" is due to their own ignorance rather than the impossibility of the art itself.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, metallurgy, transmutation, gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, chemical spirits, philosophical failure</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's systematic defense of alchemy by refuting those who fail to achieve the "Great Work" because they use incorrect starting materials.</meta>

### DISCOURSE CONCERNING THOSE
### who deny the art based on the
### assumption of using gems, glass,
### and similar things.

#### Chapter 24.

Those who have placed their hopes for the art in glass and gems have learned through experience that alterations cannot be made in [metallic] bodies by means of glass and gems. This is because a substance does not alter what it does not penetrate, and neither glass nor gems penetrate [metals]; therefore, they do not alter them. Even if they have attempted to unite glass with the metal—though this can scarcely be done—they still do not achieve their purpose, because glass lacks the essential nature of the metallic bodies. For this reason, they mistakenly believe that their own failure applies to the entire art, and thus they argue that the art does not exist. To these people we respond: they are working with improper <term>matter<note>Latin: materia; the raw physical substance used at the start of the alchemical process.</note></term>, and therefore, by drawing improper conclusions, they cannot abolish this art based on their own errors.

---

### DISCOURSE CONCERNING THOSE
### who deny the art based on its
### assumption in salts
### and alums.

#### Chapter 25.

<column-break/>

There are others who suppose this art is to be found in salts, alums, nitres, and boraxes. They may indeed experiment with these, but we do not think the art is to be found in them at all. Therefore, if after their experimentation they find little utility for <term>transmutation<note>Latin: transmutatio; the changing of one base metal into gold or silver.</note></term>—namely through dissolving, coagulating, and roasting—they should not, however, abolish this divine art, since it is both necessary and known. It is possible, nonetheless, for some alteration to be found in all these things, but it is very remote and exceedingly laborious. Others, supposing the art lies in all other growing things, prove it to be even more laborious; they even place it in all <term>vegetables<note>Latin: vegetabilibus; in medieval alchemy, this refers to all plant life or organic matter.</note></term>. They posit it as possible, but not for themselves, since they would fail in the labor before it were possible to complete what was worked upon. Therefore, if such people do not find the art through their labors, it is not permitted to argue that the art cannot be found by any labors. All those previously mentioned, being in error, established a single matter of their own and assumed no other besides it; and all of these have now been refuted. Many others, indeed almost infinite in number, who make compositions of all these things or of certain ones under diverse proportions, proceed ignorantly and unknowingly. Their error extends to infinity, because the diversity of the <term>proportion<note>Latin: proportio; the specific ratio of ingredients in a chemical mixture.</note></term> of mixable things is infinite, and the diversity of the number of mixable things is infinite. In both of these infinities, they happen to err infinitely—sometimes from an excess, sometimes from...

<summary>The author refutes critics of alchemy who use incorrect materials like glass, gems, salts, or plants, arguing that their personal failures do not disprove the validity of the "divine art." He notes that the infinite possible combinations of wrong ingredients lead many seekers into endless, fruitless labor.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, glass, gemstones, salts, alums, vegetable matter, alchemical error, proportions, matter</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the correction of errors in the alchemical art, transitioning into a formal treatise on the natural principles of bodies.</meta>

diminution, and for these people, correction is impossible. But we, sparing ourselves the length and prolixity of long speeches, do not wish to dwell on infinite details, since we shall hand down universal knowledge in brief statements, by which they will evidently be able to amend the infinity of their errors and correct them. Now, however, let us first discuss the natural principles according to their causes, as we have made you attentive to from the beginning and by the recollection of them.

### A UNIVERSAL DISCOURSE
### on the natural principles
### of bodies according to
### the opinion of the
### ancients.

#### Chapter XXVI.

We suggest to you, therefore, according to the opinion of the ancients who were of our sect of those imitating the art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, which seeks to imitate the creative processes of nature</note>, that the natural principles in the work of nature are the "fetid spirit"—that is, sulfur—and "living water," which we also allow to be called "dry water." Moreover, we have divided the fetid spirit, for it is white in its hidden state, and red and black, each being used in the mastery of this work. In its manifest state, however, each tends toward redness. We speak, therefore, in a brief discourse, as well as one that is similarly complete and sufficient, regarding the generation of each of these, and likewise the manner of their generation. It is expedient, therefore, for us to broaden our discourse and expand it, and to provide a

<column-break/>

single chapter concerning each individual natural principle. In general, however, we say that each of them is of the strongest composition and of uniform substance; and this is so because in them the smallest parts of earth are united with the aerial, aqueous, and fiery parts in such a way that none of them can leave the other during resolution <note>The process of breaking a substance down into its component parts</note>. On the contrary, each is resolved with the other because of the strong union they had with one another through their smallest parts—that is, little by little—by a heat in the minerals equal to the bowels of the earth, trodden down and leveled according to the due course of nature, as required by their essence, according to the opinion of certain ancient philosophers.

### A UNIVERSAL DISCOURSE
### on the natural principles
### according to the opinion
### of the author.

#### Chapter XXVII.

Others, however, have spoken differently, since the principle was not "living water" <note>original: "aqua viva"</note> in its own nature, but altered and converted into its own earth; and sulfur similarly altered and changed into earth. Whence they said that the principle was something else: a fetid spirit and a fugitive spirit <note>A "fugitive spirit" usually refers to a volatile substance like mercury that evaporates or escapes when heated</note> in the intention of nature. And the reason by which...

<summary>This page introduces the primary alchemical principles—sulfur (the fetid spirit) and mercury (living water)—explaining their composition and how they are unified by natural heat within the earth. It contrasts the views of ancient philosophers with the author's own developing theory on the transformation of these elements.</summary>
<keywords>natural principles, sulfur, living water, fetid spirit, mercury, alchemy, mineral generation, ancient philosophers, four elements</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the general theories of metallic composition; this page continues the debate regarding the specific origins of quicksilver and sulfur within the earth.</meta>

# BOOK

They are <note>The author refers to certain natural philosophers or alchemists</note>, and he knows, because they do not find in the mines of silver or other metals anything that is <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "ar. vi." or argentum vivum; mercury</note> in its own nature, nor anything that is similarly <term>sulfur</term>; rather, they find each of these separately in its own proper mine and nature. And they affirm this by another line of reasoning: there is no passage from one contrary to another except through a middle disposition. Therefore, since this is the case, they are forced to confess and believe that there is no passage from the softness of quicksilver to the hardness of any metal, except through a disposition that lies between their hardness and softness. But in the mines, they do not find anything in which this middle disposition is preserved. For this reason, they are compelled to believe that quicksilver and sulfur in their own nature are not the principle intended by nature, but rather something else which follows from the alteration of those essences at the root of nature into an earthy substance. And the method is this: first, each of them is converted to an earthy nature, and from these two earthy natures, the thinnest parts are resolved by the multiplied heat in the bowels of the earth, and this is a double smoke <note>The "dual vapor" theory of Aristotle, involving moist and dry exhalations</note>. This smoke is the immediate matter of metals; when this smoke has been cooked by the tempered heat of the mine, it is converted into the nature of a certain earth. Therefore, it undergoes a certain fixation, which flowing water—as it passes through the bowels of the mine and the sponginess of the earth—dissolves and unites with it uniformly in a natural and firm union. Thus, those who hold this opinion have said that the water flowing through the passages of the earth finds a dissolvable substance from the substance

<column-break/>

of the earth in its bowels and dissolves it, and is uniformly united with it, until both the dissolved substance of the earth in the bowels and the flowing water that dissolves it become one by a natural union. To such a mixture, all the elements come according to their due natural proportion and are mixed in their smallest parts <note>original: "per minima"; an early concept of atomic or molecular mixing</note> until they create a uniform mixture. And this mixture, through successive <term>decoction</term> <note>a process of heating or "cooking" within the earth</note> in the mine, is thickened and hardened and becomes metal. And these people are indeed close to the truth, yet they do not sing the pure truth.

### A SPECIFIC DISCOURSE
### on the natural principles
### of bodies.

#### Chapter 28

In a universal discourse, we have determined the natural principles of metals. It remains, therefore, that we now set down a chapter for each principle. Since there are three—sulfur, arsenic, and quicksilver—we will first assign a chapter to sulfur. Second, to arsenic. Third, to quicksilver. Then, to each of the metals which are their effects. Next, we shall set down a specific chapter according to the work of nature. From there, passing to those things which are the foundations of this <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterium"; the alchemical art or Great Work</note> and their operations, assigning the causes of all these things.

### A SINGULAR DISCOURSE
### on the principle which
### is sulfur.

### Chapter 29.

We say, therefore, that sulfur is the fatness of the earth, thickened in the mine of the earth by a temperate boiling <note>original: "decoctionem," a slow heating process</note> until it becomes hardened and dry; and when it has been hardened, it is called sulfur. Indeed, sulfur possesses a very strong composition and is of a uniform substance, entirely pure in its parts because it is homogeneous. For this reason, its oil is not taken from it by distillation, as oil is taken from other things that possess it. Those, therefore, who seek to calcine <note>to reduce to powder through heat</note> it without losing any of its substance worth caring about labor in vain, since it is not calcined except through great industry and by wasting much of its substance; for out of a hundred parts, you will scarcely reserve three sufficiently for yourself. Similarly, after calcination, it cannot be fixed <note>made resistant to heat</note> unless it is calcined first; nevertheless, it can be mixed, and its flight <note>volatility or evaporation</note> can be somewhat retarded, and its burning can be repressed, and it calcines more easily when mixed. He, therefore, who seeks to draw out a work from it should not draw it out by preparing it by itself, since it is perfected with a mixture, and without that, the mastery <note>term: "magisterium" - the successful completion of the alchemical process</note> is prolonged even unto despair. And when it is with its partner, it is a tinc-

<column-break/>

-ture, and it gives full weight to each of the metals, and it purifies and brightens them from foulness. It is perfected with the mastery, without which it provides none of these things but rather corrupts and blackens; therefore, do not use it without the mastery itself. He who also knows how to mix and befriend it with the bodies <note>the metals</note> during preparation shall know one of the greatest secrets of nature and one way of perfection, though there are many ways to one effect and one intent. And whatever body is calcined by it undoubtedly acquires weight. Copper <note>original: "Aes"</note> also takes from it the likeness of the Sun <note>gold</note>. It is also associated with Mercury <note>quicksilver</note>, and through sublimation, it becomes visible. Finally, all bodies are easily calcined by it except for the Sun <note>gold</note> and Jupiter <note>tin</note>. The Sun, indeed, most difficultly. And Mercury is not coagulated by it into gold or silver <note>original: "solem vel lunam"</note> in a way that is useful through weak artifice, as certain foolish philosophers have thought. And so we say that whichever bodies have less moisture are more easily calcined by sulfur than those which have much. By God Most High, it illuminates every body, because it is an alum and a tincture. It is also dissolved with great difficulty, because it does not have salty parts, but oily ones, which are not easily dissolved into water. However, which things dissolve easily or with difficulty into water, we shall show clearly enough in the chapter on solution. It is sublimated, truly, because it is a spirit. And if it is mixed with Venus <note>copper</note> and united with it, a wonderful violet color is made. It is likewise mixed with mercury in the same way, and from them, through boiling, a celestial and delightful color is made. Let no one think

<summary>This chapter defines sulfur as the "fatness of the earth" and explains its role as a transformative agent that can color and purify metals when handled with specific alchemical mastery. It warns of sulfur's volatility and the difficulty of fixing it without proper mixing.</summary>

<keywords>sulfur, calcination, magisterium, alchemy, metals, gold, copper, mercury, tincture, sublimation</keywords>

BOOK

that sulfur by itself completes the work of alchemy <note>original: "chimię"</note>, for this would be nothing but a vain belief; however, we shall prove this clearly enough in what follows. One should choose sulfur that is thick and bright. Let these words suffice regarding sulfur.

### DISCOURSE ON THE PRINCIPLE
->which is arsenic.<-

#### Chapter 30.

It remains for us to conclude for the present regarding arsenic. We say, therefore, since it is of a subtle matter and similar to sulfur, it ought not to be defined differently than sulfur. However, it differs from sulfur in this: that it is easily a <term>tincture</term> <note>A substance used to change the color or quality of a metal</note> of whiteness, but only with great difficulty of redness; whereas sulfur is easily a tincture of redness, but only with great difficulty of whiteness. Furthermore, there are two kinds of sulfur and arsenic: the yellow and the red, which are useful to this art, while many other kinds are not. Arsenic is "fixed" <note>rendered non-volatile or solid through heat</note> just like sulfur. Indeed, the <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note> of both is better when performed from the <term>calxes</term> <note>The powdery metallic oxide remaining after a metal has been burned or roasted</note> of metals. However, sulfur and arsenic are not the perfecting matter of this work. For they are not complete unto perfection, though they may provide aid to perfection in certain cases. One should choose that which is bright, scaly, and easily split.

### DISCOURSE ON THE PRINCIPLE
->which is quicksilver.<-

<column-break/>

#### Chapter 31.

Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum vivum"</note>, which is also called Mercury by the custom of the ancients, is a viscous water in the bowels of the earth, consisting of a subtle substance of white earth united with a most temperate heat in a total union of the smallest parts, until the moist is tempered by the dry and the dry by the moist equally. Therefore, it runs easily over a flat surface because of the moistness of its water. Yet it does not stick—even though it has a viscous moisture—because of its dryness, which tempers it and does not permit it to adhere. It is also (as some say) the matter of metals along with sulfur. It adheres easily to three minerals: namely <term>Saturn</term> <note>Lead</note>, the <term>Sun</term> <note>Gold</note>, and <term>Jupiter</term> <note>Tin</note>; and to the <term>Moon</term> <note>Silver</note> more difficultly. To <term>Venus</term> <note>Copper</note> it adheres more difficultly than to the Moon. To <term>Mars</term> <note>Iron</note>, however, it adheres in no way except by artifice. From this, surely, you may draw out a very great secret. For it is friendly and pleasing to metals, and the medium for joining tinctures. Nothing is submerged in quicksilver except the Sun <note>Gold</note>. Nevertheless, Jupiter, Saturn, the Moon, and Venus are dissolved by it and mixed with it; and without it, none of the metals can be gilded. It can be fixed and is a tincture of most exuberant redness, of restoration, and of shining splendor; and it does not depart from that which it is mixed with, so long as it is in its own nature. However, it is not our "medicine" in its own nature, but it can sometimes assist in certain cases.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical properties of arsenic and mercury, detailing their ability to color metals and their varying affinities for different planetary metals like gold, lead, and iron.</summary>
<keywords>arsenic, quicksilver, mercury, sulfur, tincture, sublimation, gold, silver, lead, tin, copper, iron, alchemy</keywords>

### GENERAL DISCOURSE
on the effects of nature
which are metallic
bodies.

#### Chapter 32.

We shall speak of metallic bodies, which are the effects of those natural principles. There are, moreover, six in number: Gold, Silver, Lead, Tin, Copper <note>original: "Es"; while often translated as brass, in this medieval context it refers to copper or its primary alloys</note>, and Iron. We say, therefore, that a metal is a mineral body capable of being melted, and extendable under the hammer in every dimension. Metal is also (as we say) of a dense substance, and of a very strong and firm composition. Furthermore, metals have a great affinity for one another; however, a perfect metal does not perfect a diminished one simply through its mixing. For if gold is mixed with lead by melting, gold does not result from this lead, but rather the lead vanishes from the mixture and is burnt away, while the gold remains in the testing. It happens similarly in the rest of the metals following the common course of nature; however, according to our Mastery <note>The "Mastery" or "Magisterium" refers to the specialized knowledge and practice of alchemy</note>, the perfect aids the imperfect, and the imperfect is perfected in our Mastery without the addition of any foreign thing. And by God, they alter and are altered by one another, and they perfect and are perfected by one another, and each one is perfected by itself alone, without the aid of anything else.

<column-break/>

### SPECIFIC DISCOURSE
on the Sun <note>The "Sun" (Sol) is the alchemical name for Gold</note>.

#### Chapter 33.

<margin>A</margin>
We have brought to you in the general chapter a summary of the intentions of the metals, yet we shall make for you a specific discourse concerning each one. And first, let us speak of gold. We say, therefore, that gold is a yellow metallic body, heavy and shining, which has been equally digested in the belly of the earth, washed for a very long time by mineral water, extendable under the hammer, meltable, and able to endure the test of the ash-cup <note>original: "cinericium"; refers to cupellation, a refining process using a porous bowl made of bone ash</note> and of the cement <note>original: "cementi"; a refining process where gold is heated with salts to remove impurities</note>. From this you may certainly gather that something is not gold unless it possesses all the causes of the definition and the characteristics of gold. Nevertheless, whatever metal it yellows to the root, and brings to a state of balance and cleanses, it makes gold out of every kind of metal. Therefore, we weigh this by the work of nature, and that by craftsmanship copper can be changed into gold. For we have seen in copper mines from which water flowed, bringing with it very thin scales of copper. And by a long and continuous flow, the water washed and cleansed them. Then, when the water ceased to flow, we saw these scales cooked in the heat of the sun within dry sand for a period of three years, among which was found the truest gold. We estimated, therefore, that those scales had been cleansed by the benefit of the water, and by the heat of the sun in the dryness of the sand, they were equally digested and reached a state of balance.

<summary>This page defines the six primary metals and introduces the alchemical theory that imperfect metals can be perfected into gold through specific processes, citing a natural observation of copper transforming into gold in a mine as evidence.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, metals, gold, copper, transmutation, cupellation, mineralogy, digestion of metals</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the general principles of metallic transformation or the nature of the Sun (Gold), as this page begins by concluding the section on Gold and transitioning to Silver and Lead.</meta>

# BOOK
<note>original: "LIBER"</note>

By imitating nature as much as we are able, we can similarly alter substances, yet we cannot fully equal nature in this regard. Gold is the most precious of metals and is the tincture of redness <note>In alchemy, "redness" refers to the highest stage of transformation, associated with the Sun and perfection</note>, because it colors and transforms every body. It can be calcined <term>calcination: the process of reducing a metal to powder through heat</term> and dissolved, though without practical use <note>likely meaning it does not improve the gold itself</note>, and it is a medicine that brings joy and preserves the body in youth. It is broken most easily with mercury and is worn down by the odor of lead. There is no other actual substance that agrees more with its substance than Jupiter <note>Tin</note> and the Moon <note>Silver</note>. In weight, dullness, and tendency to decay, it is like Saturn <note>Lead</note>; in color it is like Venus <note>Copper</note>. In power, it is most like Venus, then the Moon, then Jupiter, then Saturn, and finally Mars <note>Iron</note>. This is one of the secrets of nature. Spirits are similarly mixed with it and are fixed by it through great skill, which cannot be achieved by a craftsman of stubborn neck and hard heart.

### DISCOURSE
->on the Moon.<-

#### Chapter 34.

Having finished the chapter on the Sun, let us direct our discourse to the Moon, commonly called silver. We say that silver is a metallic body, white with a pure whiteness, clean, hard, resonant, enduring in the ash-cup <term>cinericium: a cupel or vessel used for refining metals in a furnace</term>, extendable under the hammer, and meltable by fire. It is therefore a tincture of whiteness and hardens Jupiter <insert>Tin</insert> through artifice, converting it to itself; it mixes with the Sun <insert>Gold</insert> and does not break it, though in a test without artifice, it does not remain with it. He who knows how to further refine it, and after refinement to thicken and fix it in association, will find it remains with the gold in the "battle" <note>the trial of the furnace</note> and does not leave it entirely. When placed over the fumes of sharp substances, such as vinegar, sal ammoniac, and verjuice <note>acidic juice of unripened grapes</note>, a wonderful celestial blue color is produced. It is a noble body, but diminished from the nobility of gold. Its ore is found in specific veins, though sometimes it has an indistinct ore mixed with other bodies, and that is not as noble. It is likewise calcined and dissolved with great labor but no practical utility.

<column-break/>

### DISCOURSE ON
->Saturn.<-

#### Chapter 35.

Let us likewise provide a chapter on lead, and say that lead is a metallic body, livid, very earthy and heavy, participating little in whiteness but much in lividity. It flees from the ash-cup and the cement <note>refining processes</note>; it is soft, easily extended in every dimension by small pressure, and easily melted without reaching a red heat. Certain fools think and say that lead, in its nature, closely approaches gold. But because they are stubborn and void of all reason, unable to discern any truth, they try to extract what they can from the most subtle things; but concerning them, according to...

...judging by the senses, because they see that it is heavy and silent and does not rot, they believe it to be very close [to perfection], but all of this is also erroneous, and will be more broadly and openly proven by us in the following business. Lead <note>original: "Plumbum"</note> also has much of an earthy substance, therefore it is washed, and is turned into tin <note>original: "stagnum"; the author suggests tin is a purified form of lead</note> through washing. Through this, it is clear that tin is more like perfection. And lead is similarly burned and becomes minium <note>red lead or lead tetroxide</note>, and is placed over the vapors of vinegar and becomes ceruse <note>white lead, used as a pigment</note>. And although it does not approach perfection much, we nevertheless easily form silver from it through our craft <note>original: "artificium"</note>, and it does not keep its own weight in the transformation, but is changed into a new weight. And it acquires all of this in the Mastery <note>the "Magisterium" or the successful completion of the alchemical process</note>. Lead is also the test of silver in the cupel <note>original: "cinericio"; a vessel used in cupellation to separate precious metals from lead</note>, the reasons for which we shall state.

### DISCOURSE ON
### JUPITER <note>Tin</note>.

#### Chapter 36.

The tradition of Jupiter not being omitted, we signify to the sons of doctrine that it is a white metallic body, not purely livid, sounding little, participating in few earthy qualities, possessing a shrieking noise <note>the "tin cry," a crackling sound made when bending tin</note>, softness, and liquefaction without glowing, with speed in its root; not awaiting the cupel or the cement <note>refining processes</note>, but extendable under the hammer. Jupiter is therefore, among the bodies diminished from perfection, more akin in the root of its nature to the perfect—namely to the Sun <note>Gold</note> and the Moon <note>Silver</note>. More to the Moon, but less to the Sun. This will certainly be clearly narrated in what follows. Because Jupiter received much whiteness from the root of its generation, it therefore whitens all non-white bodies. Yet it has a vice, because it breaks every body except Saturn and the purest Sun. And Jupiter adheres much to the Sun and Moon, and therefore does not easily recede from them through tests. It receives the tincture of redness, and shines in it with inestimable brightness, and acquires weight in the Mastery of this art. It is hardened and cleansed more easily than Saturn. And he who knows how to take away its vice of breaking <note>brittleness when alloyed</note> will suddenly rejoice to enjoy its profit. For it agrees with the Sun and Moon, and will not always separate from them.

<column-break/>

### DISCOURSE ON
### VENUS <note>Copper</note>.

#### Chapter 37.

<margin>D</margin> Let our discourse at present be concerning Venus. It is a livid metallic body participating in a dark redness, ignitable, fusible, extendable under the hammer, but fleeing the cupel and the cement. Venus therefore (as has been narrated) in the depth of its substance extends the color and essence of gold, and is hammered and ignited like silver and gold. Therefore, you should take the secret from it, because it is a medium between the Sun and the Moon, and it happens that its nature is easily converted to either, and it is of good conversion and little labor. It agrees with tutty <note>original: "tutia"; zinc oxide sublimate from smelting</note>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of metallic properties, transitioning from the qualities of Tin (Jupiter) to the specific alchemical nature of Iron (Mars) and a comparison of the seven metals.</meta>

->BOOK<-

vehemently, and it colors it with a good lemon-yellow hue <note>original: "citrinat citrinitate bona"; citrination was a specific stage in alchemy where the metal turned yellow</note>, so that you may draw profit from this. We are thus excused by these means from the labors of hardening and that particular firing. You should therefore choose it <note>Tin/Jupiter</note> above the other imperfect metals for the "minor" and "middle" works, but for the "great work" <note>the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note> not at all. In this respect, however, it possesses a flaw from Jupiter, because it tarnishes easily and takes on corruption from sharp and acidic substances; to eradicate that is no easy feat, but rather a profound mystery.

### DISCOURSE ON
### Mars <note>Iron</note>.

### Chapter 38.

The account of Mars and its entire secret is a work of nature, because it is a metallic body that is very leaden-colored, yet slightly reddish, participating in a non-pure whiteness; it is hard, capable of being heated, but not meltable by direct fusion <note>Iron has a much higher melting point than other ancient metals</note>, though it is extensible under the hammer and very resonant. Mars is of difficult handling because of the powerlessness of its melting; if it is melted without a medicine that imitates its nature, it joins with the Sun <note>Gold</note> and Moon <note>Silver</note> and cannot be separated by testing without great industry. But if it is prepared and joined, it cannot be separated by any artifice if its nature of fixation is not changed, provided only its impurity is removed. It is therefore a tincture of redness easily, but of whiteness with difficulty. And when it is joined

<column-break/>

without being altered, it does not change the color of what it is mixed with, but increases it in quantity. Among all bodies, therefore, Jupiter <note>Tin</note> is transformed into a more splendid, clear, shining, and perfect solar or lunar body. But in it, the work is of easy handling but long labor. After that, Venus <note>Copper</note> is chosen, which is of less perfect mutation and more difficult handling, but of shorter labor than Jupiter. Saturn <note>Lead</note>, after Venus, has a perfection in transmutation diminished from hers, being of easy handling but the longest labor. Mars, however, stands among the rest as having the least perfection in transmutation, being of the most difficult handling and the longest labor. Therefore, whichever bodies are furthest from speed of liquefaction <note>melting</note> are found to be of more difficult handling in the work of transmutation. Such are Venus and Mars—those which are more so, are more difficult, and those which are most so, are most difficult. Whichever bodies participate in greater leadenness and corruption in the earth are found to be of greater labor and lesser perfection. However, these diversities of perfection which we determined a little earlier are found in the artifice of the "minor" and "middle" work; in the "great work," all are of one perfection, though they are not of one handling or labor. It remains to be said that the ease and difficulty of handling, and the brevity and length of labor, are found at the root of the nature of the bodies. We have now ascribed the natural principles with a true treatment, which is according to the intention of the nature of the bodies themselves.

<summary>This page details the alchemical properties of Iron (Mars), noting its high melting point and difficulty in transmutation, while providing a comparative hierarchy of metals including Tin, Copper, and Lead.</summary>

XVII
->SECOND<-
<meta>catchword: confi- (confidence/confirmation)</meta>

And we have truly explained the specific discourses of those very bodies in individual chapters, both according to the opinion of those who were able to see the inner secrets of nature, and according to our own view, who have arrived at these things with persistent labor. But now, according to what we have discovered, it is expedient to supply what is lacking in this art, and to hand down all the principles of this mastery <note>original: "magisterij" - the mastery or the Great Work of alchemy</note> in the final part of our discourse, and to show the perfection which we have seen according to its requirements, along with its causes.

### BOOK SECOND
#### Concerning the principles of this mastery and its perfection.

#### Chapter 39.

There are two things which must be determined: namely, the principles of this mastery, and its perfection. Therefore, the principles of this art are the modes of its operations, by which the craftsman applies himself to this mastery. These are indeed diverse from one another. For one mode is sublimation <note>vaporizing a solid to purify it</note>, and another is descension <note>a method of distillation by heat from above, forcing liquid downward</note>, and one also is distillation, and one of the modes is calcination <note>reducing a substance to powder by heat</note>, and another is solution <note>dissolving</note>, and another is coagulation. The seventh mode is fixation <note>making a volatile substance stable under heat</note>, and the eighth is ceration <note>softening a substance to a wax-like state</note>. We shall give an individual account of each of these. Perfection consists of the consideration of the things by which one

<column-break/>

arrives at it, and from the condition of the helping things, and from the consideration of the thing itself which is finally perfected, and by which it is known whether the mastery was in perfection or not. The consideration of those things by which one arrives at the completion of the work is the consideration of the manifest substance, and the manifest colors, and the weight in each of the bodies to be changed, and those which are not to be changed from the root of their nature without that artifice; and likewise the consideration of those in their root with artifice, and the consideration of the principles of bodies according to their hidden depth and manifest surface, according to their nature, without artifice, and likewise those with artifice. For if the bodies and their principles were not known in the depth of their nature and in the manifest, with artifice and without, it would not be known what is superfluous in them and what is diminished; for which reason it would be necessary that we never arrive at the perfection of their transmutation. Moreover, the consideration of the things helping perfection is the consideration of the natures of those things which we see adhere to bodies without artifice and cause a change. And these are Marchesite <note>Iron pyrites</note>, Magnesia <note>likely manganese ore or a similar mineral</note>, Tutia <note>Zinc oxide</note>, Antimony, and Lapis Lazuli; and the consideration of those things which cleanse bodies without adhering, which are namely salt and alum, nitre and borax, and those which are of their nature; and also the consideration of glass-making <note>original: "vitrificationis"</note> which cleanses by a similar nature.

### GENERAL DISCOURSE
### on the perfection
### of the Mastery.

### Chapter 40.

The consideration of the thing which brings perfection is the consideration of choosing the pure substance of quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; the term for mercury</note>, and it is the material which took its origin from the matter of that substance and was created from it. However, this material is not quicksilver in its own nature, nor in its entire substance, but is a part of it; it does not exist now, but only when our Stone has been made. For the Stone itself illuminates and preserves it from burning <note>original: "adustione"; refers to oxidation or chemical degradation by fire</note>, which is the sign of perfection.

### GENERAL DISCOURSE
### on the consideration of the examination of the perfection of the Mastery, and on the principles of the Mastery, and the perfection of the same.

### Chapter 41.

The consideration of the thing by which it is known whether the Mastery <term>Magisterium: the alchemical process or the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term> is in a state of perfection or not, is the consideration of the cupel <note>original: "cinericium"; a vessel made of bone ash used to refine precious metals</note>, of cementation, of ignition, of exposure to the vapors of sharp substances, of quenching, of the mixing of sulfur that burns bodies, of reduction after calcination, and of the reception of quicksilver. We shall describe all these things with their causes and with easy experiments, by which you will be able to clearly recognize that our discourses have not erred. And these experiments shall be thoroughly known to you.

<column-break/>

### GENERAL DISCOURSE
### on sublimation.

### Chapter 42.

Following our purpose, therefore, we speak of sublimation <term>Sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify "spirits"</term>. And the reason for its invention was because our ancestors did not find—nor have we, nor those who will come after us—anything that could be united with the bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to solid metals</note> except for the spirits alone, or something that contains the nature of the body or spirit within itself. And they saw that if these were cast upon the bodies without their cleansing, they either gave imperfect colors or entirely corrupted, burned, blackened, and defiled them. And this occurred according to the diversity of the spirits themselves. For some are burning, such as sulfur, arsenic, and marcasite <note>original: "marchesita"; likely iron pyrites or similar metallic sulfides</note>, and these indeed corrupt entirely. Others, however, do not burn, such as every kind of tutty <note>original: "tutia"; zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</note>, and these indeed give imperfect colors. This happens for two reasons: first, because the burning oiliness of sulfur—whose property is to be easily inflamed and to be blackened by inflammation, and consequently to blacken—has not been removed from them. The second cause is the earthy <unclear>fecititas</unclear> <note>likely "faecitas" or dregs/impurities</note>...

<summary>This page discusses the essential role of purified mercury in the alchemical "Mastery" and outlines various laboratory tests, such as cupellation and calcination, to verify the perfection of the work. It also introduces the necessity of sublimation to remove "burning oiliness" and impurities from volatile substances like sulfur and arsenic.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, quicksilver, sulfur, sublimation, calcination, cupellation, arsenic, tutty, purification of spirits, Magisterium</keywords>

# SECOND BOOK

...was not similarly taken away from them. However, in those substances where a perfect color is not granted, the cause is an earthiness <note>original: "terreitas"; referring to the impure, solid matter that contaminates a substance</note> that makes the color livid. Scorching can also create a livid color; therefore, we have devised a way to cleanse these from the oiliness that burns them, as well as from all earthy superfluity. We could arrive at this through no other mastery except through **sublimation** alone. For when fire elevates a substance, it always elevates the finer parts; therefore, it leaves behind the coarser parts. From this, it is clear that through sublimation, the spirit is cleansed of the earthiness which both hindered its entry <note>original: "ingressionem"; the ability of a chemical agent to penetrate a metal</note> and gave it an unclean color.

Experience indeed makes it well enough known to you through your own sight that they have been freed from that impurity. For you see them more splendid and more transparent, and you see them enter and penetrate the density of bodies more easily, and not leave a stain as they did before. That scorching is removed by sublimation is clear by experiment, since arsenic—which before its sublimation was impure and prone to scorching—does not allow itself to be inflamed after its sublimation, but only withdraws without inflammation; and you will find this same thing in sulfur if you test it. Because we have seen the adherence to bodies with alteration in no things other than in the spirits <note>term: "spirits" (spiritus) — volatile substances like sulfur, arsenic, and mercury that can be vaporized by heat</note>, we were by no means excused from the necessity of preparing them through their cleansing, which is done by sublimation; therefore, this was the necessary cause of the invention. Let us therefore relate the entire order of sublimation without omission, and its very essence.

<column-break/>

### PARTICULAR DISCOURSE on the sublimation of sulfur and arsenic.

### Chapter 42.

We say, therefore, that **Sublimation** is the elevation of a dry thing by fire, with adherence to its vessel. Sublimation is diversified because of the diversity of the spirits to be sublimated. For some is done with strong ignition, some with moderate, and some with a gentle fire. If, therefore, arsenic or sulfur is sublimated, it is necessary that they be sublimated by a low fire; since they have very fine parts joined uniformly to coarse ones, their entire substance would otherwise ascend without any purification—indeed, blackened and even burnt.

So that one may separate the unclean earthy substance, it is necessary to find two types of ingenuity: namely, the proportion of the fire, and the purification by mixing substances with it. For mixing with dregs <note>original: "fecibus"; solid additives used to hold back impurities</note> catches the coarse parts and keeps them pressed down in the bottom of the **aludel** <note>term: "aludel" — a pear-shaped chemical pot used as a condenser in the sublimation process</note>, and does not permit them to climb. Whence it is also necessary for the craftsman to apply a triple degree of fire to the sublimation: to proportion one in such a way that through it only the altered and cleaner parts ascend, until he clearly sees them by this means cleansed of earthy dregs.

<summary>This page explains the theory and necessity of sublimation, arguing that heat separates "fine" volatile spirits from "coarse" earthy impurities. It introduces the practical requirements for sublimating sulfur and arsenic, emphasizing the need for controlled fire and the use of an aludel.</summary>

<keywords>sublimation, sulfur, arsenic, purification, aludel, spirits, alchemy, chemical fire, volatility</keywords>

# BOOK

The second stage <note>original: "Aster" likely a typo for "Alter," meaning the second step</note> is that whatever remains in the dregs <term>feces: the solid residue or waste left after a chemical process</term> of their pure essence should be sublimated <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here for purification</term> with a greater intensity of fire. This involves the ignition of the aludel <term>aludel: a pear-shaped laboratory vessel used in sublimation</term> and the dregs themselves, which the operator will be able to see with their own eyes. The third stage, however, is to apply a very weak fire, without dregs, to that which has already been sublimated and purified. In this stage, hardly anything should ascend; whatever does rise must be the most subtle substance, which is of no value to this work, as it is the medium through which burning <note>original: "adustio"</note> occurs in sulfurs. Therefore, the entire intent of sublimation is this: that by removing the earthiness through the proper application of fire, and likewise by casting away the most subtle and smoky part which causes corruptive burning, we are left with that part which exists in balance. This part achieves a simple melting over the fire without any burning, and it escapes the fire without bursting into flame. That the most subtle part is what causes burning is proven by the clearest arguments: fire converts everything to its own nature, starting with whatever is most similar to it. In any combustible thing, the subtle part is most similar to fire, and the even more subtle part is more similar still. Experience confirms this, for raw sulfur or arsenic <note>original: "arsenicum"</note> ignites very quickly—sulfur even more easily. However, once sublimated, it does not ignite directly, but instead evaporates and thins out without flaming, provided melting has occurred first. Thus, it is clear that our discourse

<column-break/>

is truthful. The proof for the application of dregs, in their proper proportion, is that a material should be chosen with which the spirits to be sublimated are most compatible and with which they can mix most deeply. This is because a material to which they are more closely united is more powerful in retaining the impurities of the substances being sublimated than one to which they are not. The reason for this is a sufficiently manifest proof. The proof that the administration of dregs is necessary is this: if the sulfur or arsenic to be sublimated were not joined with a residue of some fixed thing, then it would necessarily ascend with its entire substance, uncleaned. Those experienced in sublimations know through practice that we speak the truth. The proof that it is necessary to use dregs with which the substances can unite in their depths is that if the dregs were not mixed down to the smallest particles, then the same thing would happen as if they had no dregs at all—namely, their entire essence would ascend without any purification. Just as they ascend with their whole substance when without dregs, they must also ascend when sublimated from dregs to which they are not united. Experience knows this to be true for anyone who has seen and understood this; for when one sublimates from a substance foreign to the nature of metallic bodies, he sublimates in vain, finding the substances in no way purified after their ascent. If, however, he sublimates with the lime <term>calx: a powder produced by heating a metal or mineral; an oxide</term> of one of the metallic bodies, then he has sublimated well and can achieve perfect cleansing with ease. Therefore, the intent of the dregs is that they should be provided from the limes of metals. For in them, the operation of sublimation is easy.

# BOOK TWO

-> XVIII <-

mation <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing "sublimation" from the previous page.</note>, but with other materials it is most difficult. Therefore, there is nothing else that can take their place. We do not say, however, that sublimation is impossible without the calces <note>term: calx (plural: calces). A powder produced by heating a metal or mineral to high temperatures; an oxide.</note> of bodies, but rather that it is extremely difficult and involves such long labors and delays as to lead to despair. But it has this much benefit: that which is sublimated without dregs <note>original: "fecibus" (feces/dregs). The solid residue left after a process.</note>, or without the calces of bodies, is of great quantity; whereas that with dregs is of less. Furthermore, that which is done with the calces of bodies is of very small quantity, but of the easiest and shortest labor. 

That which best excuses us from using dregs from [metallic] bodies is every kind of prepared salt, and things of a similar nature; with these, sublimation of a great quantity is also possible for us. This is because the separation of the dregs from the substances to be sublimated is easily achieved by dissolving the salt, which happens in no other materials. The proportion of dregs is such that the quantity of dregs should equal the quantity of the substances to be sublimated. In this, even an unrefined craftsman <note>original: "artifex". The alchemist or skilled operator.</note> cannot err; for a mediocre craftsman, it happens that setting the dregs at half the weight of the substances to be sublimated is sufficient, and in this he ought not to err. To the practiced and highly experienced man, however, the smallest portion of them suffices. For the smaller the quantities of dregs are, the greater the abundance of the sublimation must necessarily be—provided that the reduction of the fire is made proportional to the reduction of the dregs. For in a small quantity, a small fire is drawn out for perfection; in a large quantity, a large fire; in a greater, a greater. But since

<column-break/>

fire is not a thing that can be measured, it happens that one errs in it very often when one is not practiced, as much from the diversity of furnaces and the wood being burned as from the vessels and their capacity. Concerning all these things, it is expedient for the attentive craftsman to be diligent. 

But we provide a common rule: first, it is expedient to use a small fire to press out only the wateriness from the substances to be sublimated. Once this is removed, if anything ascends by that heat, then the fire is not increased at the start, so that by the weakest fire the most subtle part—which is the cause of burning—may be taken away and set aside. When, however, nothing or very little ascends (which you can test by placing a wick <note>original: "licinij". A probe or wick used to test the vapors.</note> into the upper hole of the aludel <note>term: aludel. A pear-shaped pot or condenser used in the sublimation process.</note>), let the fire be strengthened beneath it. 

Experience with the wick will show you how the strength of the fire ought to be. For if a little of the substance to be sublimated comes out, or if it comes out clean, the fire is still small; therefore, let it be increased. If, however, it comes out in great quantity and is impure, then the fire is excessive; therefore, let it be reduced. But if it is clean and in great quantity, the [correct] proportion has now been found. The craftsman will be able to judge the purity or impurity by pulling out the wick that was placed in the hole of the aludel; according to the quantity and quality of what he sees adhering to the wick, he can reason out the necessary proportional fire for the whole sublimation. From this, he will find the correct regulation of the fire without deception. The best kind of dregs is to take scales of iron or burnt copper. And these indeed, because of their private...

### DISCOURSE ON THE SUBLIMA-
### tion and rectification
### of sulfur and arse-
### nic.

#### Chapter 43.

It is therefore necessary for us to guide the craftsman, as everyone happens to err through ignorance in the <term>sublimation</term><note>The process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note> of these two spirits. Let us first explain that if he uses too much dross <note>original: "feces"</note>, and does not increase the fire proportionally, nothing of the substance to be sublimated will rise. How he may find that proportion has already been sufficiently explained to him. And if he uses a small quantity, or does not use the <term>calx</term><note>A powder produced by roasting a metal or mineral.</note> of bodies, then even if he does not find the right proportion, the substance to be sublimated rises with its entire substance. I have likewise provided a sufficient discovery of this. Errors also happen regarding the furnace. For a large furnace gives a large fire, and a small one a small fire, provided the wood and the "ear-holes" <note>ventilation holes or draft ports</note> are proportioned to them. If, therefore, he places a large quantity of material to be sublimated into a small furnace, he will not be able to provide a fire sufficient for its elevation. If, however, he puts a small quantity into a large furnace, he will destroy the sublimation because of the excess of fire. Similarly,

<column-break/>

a thick-walled furnace gives a concentrated and strong fire, while a thin one gives a sparse and weak one, in which matters it is likewise possible to err. Similarly, a furnace with spacious "ears" <note>vents</note> gives a bright and large fire, while one with narrow ones gives a weak one. Furthermore, if the distance from the furnace supports <note>original: "spondilibus"</note> is greater than the fitting of the vessel to the furnace, it will give a large fire; if smaller, a smaller fire. In all these things, it is very easy to err. The <term>rectification</term><note>The process of refining or purifying a substance.</note> of these matters is that the furnace should be constructed according to the intensity of the fire he seeks: namely, thick-walled, with free-flowing vents, and with a large distance between the <term>aludel</term><note>A pear-shaped pot or condenser used in sublimation.</note> vessel and the furnace supports, if he seeks a large fire. If he seeks a medium fire, let him find a medium proportion in all these things; if a weak one, let him find the corresponding proportion. We shall teach you to find all these proportions with true measurement and determined experience. If, therefore, you wish to elevate a large quantity of sublimation, find an aludel vessel of such capacity that it receives the material on its bottom to the height of one palm's breadth. Fit the furnace to it so that it receives the aludel in its center, with a distance of two fingers from its walls. Once the furnace is made, give it ten equidistant vents of the same size, so that there is an equality of fire on all its parts. Then, with an iron bar fixed across the middle of the furnace on its supports—which should be one palm and a thumb's width from the bottom of the furnace, and one finger thick—let the aludel vessel be fixed upon it and sealed with clay to the fur-

### THE DESCRIPTION OF WHICH IS AS FOLLOWS.

Then indeed, observe whether it has freed itself well and easily from the smoke, and if the flame has passed freely through the whole furnace in the circuit around the <term>aludel</term> <note>A pear-shaped earthenware bottle used in sublimation, often stacked in series.</note>. If so, then it is well-proportioned; if not, it is not. In that case, widen its <term>vents</term> <note>original: "auriculas," literally "little ears," referring to the small openings or handles that allow air to circulate.</note>, and if it is improved, all is well. If it is not improved, then it must be concluded that the distance of the aludel from the walls is too small; therefore, let the walls be scraped down and the distance increased, and afterwards let it be tested again. Let the widening of the vents and the scraping of the <term>internal supports</term> <note>original: "spondilium," referring to the structural rings or vertebrae of the furnace.</note> be repeated until it freely

<column-break/>

clears itself of smoke, and the flame around the circuit of the aludel is bright, and the smoke exits freely through the vents. # BOOK
### C

...should form a thickness of two fingers. Similarly, the proportion of the wood should be selected, since solid wood provides a strong fire, while spongy wood provides a weak one; dry wood provides a large fire that ends quickly. Therefore, by considering the distance of the **aludel** <note>A pear-shaped chemical vessel used for sublimation</note>, the size or smallness of the "ears" <note>likely the air vents or handles of the furnace</note>, the thickness or thinness of the furnace walls, and the variety of woods previously mentioned, one comes to investigate all the variations of fires through true experience. Indeed, by the greater or lesser closing of the vents, or the window of the furnace through which the wood is introduced, and by the variety of wood supplied, and by adding or subtracting from them, one comes to find the determined length of time the fire will last. That is, so that it may be known with certain knowledge how long each fire in its specific degree can endure in a steady state. And this investigation is especially useful and necessary for you, because through it you will be spared from a multitude of immense labor. Therefore, exercise yourself in this and in all things recently determined by us, for he who exercises himself will find the way, but he who does not, will not.

The purpose of the **aludel** vessel is that it should be fashioned from thick glass; for it is not effective if made of another material, unless perhaps it were of a substance similar to thick glass. For only glass and its likeness, since it lacks pores, is able to hold the **spirits** <note>Volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that vaporize when heated</note> so that they do not flee and be destroyed by the fire. No other material will do, because through their pores the spirits are gradually diminished and vanish. Nor are metals effective in this, because the spirits, on account of

<column-break/>

their friendship and affinity for them, penetrate the metals and unite with them; for which reason, passing through them, they vanish—which is clearly proven by those things determined by us. By necessity and experience it has been found that we have spoken the truth; therefore, we are not excused from the use of glass in the composition of the aludel.

Let there be fashioned, therefore, a round glass bowl, the bottom of which should have a slight curve, and in the middle of its joints let a glass "belt" be formed surrounding it. Above that belt, let a round wall be cast, equidistant from the finished wall to the thickness of the lid of the bowl itself, so that the wall of the lid may fall into this space loosely without pressure. Let the height of this wall be according to the measure of the height of the bowl's wall, or a little more, or a little less. Let two lids be fashioned equal to the measure of this cavity between the two walls, whose length should be equal—specifically one span <note>about 9 inches</note>—and let their shape be a single shape, namely **pyramidal**. In their heads let there be two equal holes, one in one and the other in the other, into both of which a large hen's feather could fit, as will be seen more clearly here and a little later. The purpose of the bowl of the vessel, therefore, is so that its assembly can be moved according to the will of the craftsman, and that the joint be ingenious, through which no exit is open to the spirits even without any **luting** <note>Sealing with clay or paste</note>. Therefore, he who can be more ingenious in this should not excuse himself from doing so because of our tradition. And there is a special...

<summary>This page details the regulation of furnace heat using different types of wood and air vents, and argues that the aludel (sublimation vessel) must be made of glass to prevent volatile "spirits" from escaping through pores or reacting with metal.</summary>
<keywords>aludel, glassmaking, furnace regulation, spirits, sublimation, alchemy, laboratory equipment, heat control</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of sublimation techniques, specifically focusing on the physical assembly of the apparatus and the quality control of the resulting substances.</meta>

->SECOND<-

The intention regarding this is namely that the shell <note>the lower vessel</note> should enter into its cover by its joints <note>original: "spondilibus," referring to the interlocking rims or "vertebrae" of the vessels</note> up to the middle. For since it is the nature of vapors to ascend and not to descend, we have found by this first discovery that the spirits have no exit for escape; and by this method, we have surpassed other ways which we sought through our own invention. And by experiencing this, they will see that we have judged truly concerning it. Indeed, there is also one intention: that the head of the aludel <term>aludel: a pear-shaped pot used in sublimation</term> be emptied very frequently, lest by too great a multiplication of the substances to be sublimated into it, they fall back to its bottom, and the multitude of repeated sublimations keep the work occupied for a long time. There is likewise another intention: that what ascends as a powder near the opening of the aludel's head should always be separated apart from that which is found settled, fused and pressed down in pieces, appearing clear at the bottom and adhering to the joints of the vessel,

<column-break/>

since this latter material has less scorching <note>original: "adustione," referring to the chemical "burning" or oxidation caused by excessive heat</note> than that which is found to have climbed near the opening; this is proven in the previous sections by both reason and experiment. The proof of the goodness and perfection of the sublimation has already been stated: it is that the substance be found clear and bright, and that it does not burn away when ignited. This, therefore, is the perfection of the intentions for the sublimation of sulfur and arsenic. And if it is not found to be so, let the work be repeated upon them, with consideration of all its requirements, until it is found to be thus.

### Sublimation of
### Mercury.

### Chapter 45.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of chemical purification, specifically focusing on the refinement of mercury (quicksilver) through sublimation, following the previous discussion on mineral properties.</meta>

# BOOK

Now, indeed, let us determine with all our focus the sublimation of **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti viui"; mercury</note>. Its complete perfection is therefore the purification of its earthiness and the removal of its wateriness. We are excused from the labor of removing its combustibility, since it possesses none. We say, therefore, that the method for separating its superfluous earth is to mix it with things for which it has no affinity, and to repeat its sublimation from them many times. The types of these substances are **talc**, and the **lime of eggshells** and of **white marble**. Similarly, very finely ground **glass**, and every kind of prepared **salt**. It is indeed cleansed by these, but it is instead corrupted by things having an affinity with it, because all such things possess a sulfurous quality which, ascending with it during sublimation, corrupts it. And you see the proof of this in experience: if you sublime it from **tin** or from **lead**, you will see it stained with blackness after the sublimation. Therefore, its sublimation is better through those things with which it does not agree. It would be better with those it agrees with, if they did not have a sulfurous nature. Thus, sublimation from **lime** is better than from all other things, because it has little affinity and no sulfurous nature. The method for removing its superfluous wateriness is this: when it is mixed with the limes from which it must be sublimed, let it be ground and mixed with them by **imbibition** <note>The process of soaking a solid in a liquid until it is saturated</note> until nothing of it appears, and then

<column-break/>

afterward, the wateriness of the imbibition should be removed over a very slow fire. As this recedes, the wateriness of the quicksilver recedes with it. However, let the fire be so gentle that the entire substance of the quicksilver does not ascend because of it. Therefore, by the multiple repetition of imbibition with grinding and gentle roasting, its greater wateriness is destroyed, the remainder of which is removed through the repetition of sublimation. And when you see it most white, surpassing snow in its whiteness, and adhering like a "dead" thing to the segments of the **aludel** <note>term: aludel; a pear-shaped chemical vessel used in sublimation</note>, then repeat its sublimation over itself without the dregs. For a fixed part of it adheres to the dregs, and could never be separated from them by any kind of ingenuity. Or, after that, you may **fix** <note>term: fixio; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> a part of it, and we will explicitly hand down to you the method of its fixation narrated in the following sections. And when you have fixed that part, then repeat the sublimation of the remaining part over it, so that it may similarly be fixed, and keep that which you will test over the fire. If it gives a good fusion, then you have administered sufficient sublimation for it. If not, add to it some part of sublimated quicksilver, and repeat the sublimation until it is done. If it has a bright, most white, and transparent color, then you have cleansed it well; if not, you have not. Therefore, do not be negligent in its preparation which is done through sublimation, because as the cleansing is, so the perfection follows through it in the **projection** <note>The act of casting a medicinal or transformative substance onto a base metal</note> upon each of the imperfect bodies, and upon the unprepared quicksilver itself. Whence it happens to some...

...produces iron, while for some it happens to form lead, and for others through that it forms copper <note>original: "venerem" (Venus), the alchemical symbol for copper</note>, and for others tin. This happens because of negligence in purification, sometimes of that substance alone, and sometimes of the sulfur mixed with it and its companion. If, therefore, you cleanse it by direct sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it becomes vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</note> and perfect it, there will be a firm and perfect white tincture, to which there is no equal.

### DISCOURSE ON THE Sublimation of Marcasite.

#### Chapter 46.

The highest intention regarding the sublimation of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> having been sufficiently handed down, we now proceed to the sublimation of Marcasite <note>A term used historically for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrites or "fool's gold"</note> itself. There are, however, two ways to sublimate it. The first is performed without ignition <note>heating to the point of glowing</note>, the other with ignition. And this is why: it possesses a twofold substance. One is pure sulfur in its own nature; the other is "mortified" <note>chemically "killed" or fixed, meaning it has lost its fluid, metallic form</note> quicksilver. The first is useful just like sulfur. The second is useful as quicksilver that has been mortified and moderately prepared. Let us therefore take up this latter one, since through it we are excused from the previous [work with] quicksilver and from the labor of mortifying it. The entire method of its sublimation, therefore, is that it be ground and placed in an aludel <note>A pear-shaped chemical vessel used in sublimation</note>, and its sulfur should be sublimated without ignition, always removing what is sublimated from it, very frequently for the reason stated, and increasing the strength of the fire until the ignition of the aludel. And the first sublimation of marcasite ought to take place in the sublimation vessel until the sulfur has departed, and afterwards proceed in that vessel successively and in an orderly fashion until everything consisting of sulfur within it has gone out. You will be able to prove this by these manifest experiments: for when all its sulfur has been raised, you will see a white color. That which is sublimated after that is changed to the brightest white, with a heavenly blue <note>original: "cœleſtino"</note> mixed into that same most white and pleasant color. Otherwise, [you will know] because that which is of the nature of sulfur will be consumed and give a flame like sulfur. But that which is sublimated second after that will neither be set on fire nor show any properties of sulfur, but rather those of mortified quicksilver in the repetition of the sublimation.

<column-break/>

Let us therefore collect that by its own method of sublimation, which is: let a very solid and well-baked earthen vessel be made, to the length of half a man's height. As for the width, let it be such that a hand can enter it. And let its bottom be made so that it can be separated and joined, in the likeness of a single, very deep flat dish. And measure...

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing an alchemical distillation apparatus. [The substance] is drawn from the opening of that vessel down near to the bottom, to the measure of the length of one hand including the fingers. And from that place up to its head, let the vessel be glazed <term>vitrificetur: coated with a glass-like glaze to prevent porosity</term> on the inside with a very thick glazing. And upon its head let an alembic <note>the cooling cap of a still</note> with a wide nose be placed. For in such a vessel, that substance is to be sublimated <term>sublimation: the process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then condenses back into a solid on a cooler surface</term>. Therefore, let the bottom be joined in its own vessel by a clay of firm tenacity, and upon that bottom let marcasite <note>original: "marschesita"; likely iron pyrite or a similar metallic mineral</note> be scattered. Upon the head of the vessel, place the wide-nosed alembic, and let it be put into a furnace, the property of which must be to provide strong ignition—namely, for the melting of silver <note>original: "argenti"</note> or copper <note>original: "veneris"; literally 'of Venus', the alchemical symbol for copper</note>, if the craftsman should require it, which we

<column-break/>

have placed in the summary of our work, where we shall sufficiently describe to you the varieties of all instruments. Surround the head of the furnace with a wheel <note>a circular plate or ring</note> having a hole in its center the size of the vessel, through which that vessel may enter. Seal the joints around the vessel and the furnace with clay, lest the escaping fire hinder you and the adhesion of the sublimation. Leave only four small windows in the wheel which can be closed and opened, through which coals may be put into the furnace; and in the sides of the furnace, likewise, another four beneath those intermediate ones, through which coals may also be sent. There should be six or eight holes the size of a little finger, which are never to be closed, so that through them the furnace may freely rid itself of smoke. Let those holes be at the junction of the furnace with its wheel. The furnace is indeed one of great ignition, the supports <note>original: "spondilia"; structural ribs or bars</note> of which should be at a height of two cubits. In the middle let there be a wheel, perforated with very small and very frequent holes, strongly attached with clay. The tops of the holes should be narrow above, but wide below, so that ash or coals may fall more freely from them; leave them continually open for the freer intake of air. For the free and abundant intake of air through the lower holes is one cause of great ignition throughout the furnace. Exercise yourself in this, therefore, and you shall find it. The reason for the length of the vessel is so that a great quantity of it may extend outside the fire and be cooled, so that the vapors of the sublimation may find a place of cooling and adhere, and not find [an exit...]

flee the path of its own destruction. This was discovered by the one who, while raising the <term>aludel</term><note>A pear-shaped chemical vessel used for sublimation, often stacked in series.</note> only a short distance, produced nothing from the sublimated material. Because of the shortness of the vessel, the heat was equal throughout its entire body. Therefore, the substance to be sublimated remained constantly converted into the form of smoke and did not adhere to the sides, but instead vanished gradually through the pores of the vessel. Thus, in all things to be sublimated, the vessel should be extended for the greater part of its length into a place of cooling. The reason for the <term>vitrification</term><note>The process of coating the vessel in glass or a glaze to make it non-porous.</note> of the vessel is so that the rising vapors do not find the wall of the aludel porous at the point of their ascent and escape by penetrating through it. Therefore, the place of their ascent is glazed so that the path of escape is blocked. The bottom of the aludel, however, is not glazed, because its base sits in the fire, which would melt the glazing; if that melted, both the base and the material to be sublimated would melt and turn into glass. For it is the property of glass to conquer all things and convert them into itself. Having considered all these things and their causes, let a fire be made under the aludel until you are certain by true experience that the whole has ascended. The "experience" <note>In this context, "experience" refers to a practical test or proof.</note> consists of inserting a well-baked earthen rod—which should have a small hole in its tip reaching to its middle, about the size of a pinky finger—near the substance from which the sublimation occurs. If any of the sublimated material adheres to the hole, it is not yet fully sublimated; if not, then it is finished. By this same practice, you can be certain in all sublimations. ### DISCOURSE ON THE SUBLIMATION of magnesia and tutty.

### Chapter 47.

The method for the sublimation of <term>magnesia</term><note>Likely referring to manganese ore or a similar mineral, not modern magnesium.</note> and <term>tutty</term><note>Zinc oxide, often collected as a crust from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note> is the same as the method for the final sublimation of marcasite. For all these things cannot be sublimated without "ignition" <note>The application of intense heat or fire.</note>; therefore, they all share one method with the same causes and tests. They follow one general order: whatever must be sublimated with ignition ought to be sublimated without added dregs <note>original: "fecibus"</note>, because they contain sufficient—even superfluous—dregs within themselves. The sign of this is the difficulty of their sublimation. All "diminished bodies" <note>A term for base metals that are considered "imperfect" compared to gold or silver.</note> are likewise sublimated in this same order; no difference occurs except that the fire of sublimation is more vehement for the metals than for magnesia, marcasite, and tutty.

<summary>This page details the technical requirements for sublimating minerals like marcasite and tutty, emphasizing the need for glazed vessels to prevent vapor loss and a cooling zone for condensation. It also describes a practical test using a ceramic rod to determine when the process is complete.</summary>

<keywords>sublimation, aludel, vitrification, marcasite, magnesia, tutty, chemical apparatus, metallurgy</keywords>
