<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of alchemical operations, specifically focusing on the transition from sublimation (vaporization) to descension (downward filtration/melting).</meta>

->BOOK<-

And bodies are not diversified in their sublimations <note>Sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify materials.</note> in the same way, except that some require the admixture of a substance that helps lift them, while others do not, so that their elevation may be made easier. But only one specific sublimation in the consideration of bodies has been found good by experience: namely, that there should not be a large quantity of the body to be sublimated at the bottom of the *aludel* <note>original: "aludel"; a pear-shaped chemical flask used in sublimation.</note>, because a great mass hinders the sublimation. And let there be a flat surface at the bottom of the sublimation *aludel*, and not a concavity, so that the body spread evenly and thinly over the bottom can be elevated equally and greatly in all its parts. Indeed, the bodies requiring the admixture of an elevating substance are Venus <note>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper.</note> and Mars <note>Mars: the alchemical name for Iron.</note>, because of the slowness of their melting. Thus, Venus is elevated with *tutia* <note>original: "tutia"; zinc oxide, often found as a crust in furnaces.</note> and Mars with arsenic, and with these they are easily elevated, because they agree with them most closely. Therefore, let the sublimation be performed after considering these things, as with *tutia* and similar substances in sublimation, and their sublimation shall be arranged in this same order with its causes and experiences.

<column-break/>

### DISCOURSE
#### On Descension.

### Chapter 47.

Having thus related the intentions of sublimation with all their causes, it remains for us to show the method of descension <note>Descension: a process where a substance is melted so it flows downward through a filter or into a lower vessel, often to separate it from impurities.</note>, likewise with its causes and in its determined and complete order. The cause of its invention was threefold: namely, so that when some matter is enclosed in that vessel which is called a *descensory* or *thimia* <note>original: "thimia"; likely a corruption of the Greek 'thimeia', referring to a specialized melting pot or furnace.</note>, after its melting it may descend through its opening, and by its descension we may be certain that it has undergone fusion. Another cause is so that weak bodies may be preserved from combustion by it after their reduction from their *calxes* <note>original: "calcibus"; a calx is the powdery oxide left after a metal has been roasted or "burnt".</note>. For when we attempt to reduce weak bodies from their calxes <note>Reduction: the process of turning a metallic powder (calx) back into a solid metal.</note>, we cannot reduce every part of them at one time. If, therefore, that part which was first reduced into a body were to wait for the reduction of the whole, the greater part of it would vanish through the fire. It was necessary, therefore, to devise a way so that as soon as a part is reduced, it may be withdrawn from the fire. This, however, is done through the descensory. There is also a third cause for its invention: the purification of bodies from every foreign thing. For the melted body descends pure...

<summary>This page concludes the instructions on sublimating metals like copper and iron using additives, then introduces 'descension' as a method for melting metals away from heat to prevent them from burning away.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, aludel, copper, iron, tutia, arsenic, descension, reduction, calx, purification</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of alchemical apparatus and processes, specifically focusing on the methods of "descensory" filtration and the principles of distillation.</meta>

->**BOOK TWO**<-

...while it leaves all foreign matter behind in its cavity. Therefore, having established these three necessities for the invention of the **descensory** <note>A "descensory" is a vessel used for downward filtration or melting, where the purified substance drips through a hole in the bottom.</note>, the method for using it—along with its previously noted instrument and its causes—must be determined. We say, therefore, that its form shall be such that its bottom is pointed, and its walls should be smooth and without any ledge, tapering equally toward the sharpness of the bottom, so that every fusible substance may descend freely without sticking to the hole at the bottom. Its cover (if necessary) should be like a flat dish and fit it well; these should be made of good, firm earth <note>Refractory clay capable of withstanding high heat.</note> that does not easily melt under the pressure of the fire. Therefore, let the material intended for descent be placed in it upon round sticks made of earth, so that it may be closer to the upper bottom; let it be covered and the joint sealed, and placed in a charcoal fire. Let the fire be blown upon until the entire substance descends into the vessel placed beneath it. However, if the substance is difficult to melt, it can be placed upon a flat plate or one of slight concavity, from which it can easily descend by tilting the head of the descensory once it has melted. For by this means, bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the seven known metals.</note> are purified. But they are better purified by means of a **pastille** <note>A small cake or lump of material used in refining.</note>, the method of which is the same as the descensory; for this reason, we are excused from further describing the latter. For the pastille holds the dross of the bodies just like the descensory, but better, and therefore we shall describe its method. We say, then, that we take the body intended for cleansing and reduce it into very small grains, or filings,

<column-break/>

or into a **calx** <note>term: calx (a powder produced by heating a metal or mineral to remove its volatile parts)</note> (which is more perfect). We mix with it a high-grade lime <note>original: "calcem altē"</note> which is not intended to melt, and afterwards we melt the mixture. We find that by this method, bodies are cleansed through many repetitions—not with the "perfect" purification which we know exists, but with a "useful" purification, so that once the medicine of perfection is received, the bodies may be transformed better and more perfectly by it. For this is the administration that precedes the cause; we shall sufficiently describe every administration to you in the following sections. Now, however, let us set down the description of the descensory. It is fitting, therefore, as we follow our purpose, that we deliver a discourse on **distillation** along with its causes. Distillation is the elevation of aqueous vapors in their vessel. Distillation is certainly diversified: for some is done by fire, and some without fire. That which is done by fire is found to be of two kinds: one is by elevation into an **alembic** <note>term: alembic (the cap or "head" of a still used to collect and condense vapor)</note>, and the other is by the descent of **chemistry** <note>original: "descensum chimię"; likely referring to "distillation by descent" used for extracting oils.</note>, by which means oil is drawn out from vegetables. The reason why distillation was invented, and the general cause for the invention of any distillation, is the purification of a liquid from the dross of its turbulence and its preservation from putrefaction. For we see that a thing distilled by any kind of distillation is made purer and is better guarded against rotting. The specific reason for the invention of that distillation which occurs by ascent into an alembic is the search for pure water without earth. The proof of this is that you see water distilled twice has no dross.

<summary>This page describes the construction and use of the descensory for melting metals and introduces the theory of distillation, explaining its role in purifying liquids and preventing decay.</summary>
<keywords>descensory, distillation, purification of metals, alembic, calx, alchemy apparatus, chemical preservation</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of alchemical processes, specifically focusing on the theory and practice of distillation as a means of purification and elemental separation.</meta>

# BOOK

The reason for the invention of pure water <note>distilled water</note> was for the soaking of clean medicinal powders; specifically, so that whenever we require a soaking, we might have a pure water that leaves no dregs behind after its evaporation, by which dregs our medicines and cleansed spirits might be stained or corrupted. However, the reason for the invention of that distillation which is done by descent <note>distillatio per descensum: a method where heat is applied from above and the liquid flows downward into a receiver</note> was the extraction of oil that is pure in its nature. This is because oil, due to its combustible nature, could not be obtained through ascent <note>standard upward distillation</note>; the goal of this inquiry was to ensure that the oil retains its color, which is mixed within its substance. This can indeed be helpful in certain cases. Furthermore, the reason for the invention of distillation performed through a filter without fire <note>capillary action using a wick or cloth</note> was solely for the clarity of the water. Let us now, therefore, describe the methods of all distillations along with their causes.

Of that distillation which is performed by ascent, there are two methods. One is performed with an earthenware pot filled with ashes. The other is performed with water in its own vessel, packed with grass or woolly material arranged in its proper order, so that the gourd <note>cucurbita: the lower boiling vessel</note> or the distilling <term>alembic</term> <note>the "head" or cap of the still</note> does not break before the process is brought to completion. That which is done with ashes is performed with a greater, stronger, and sharper fire; however, that which is done with water does not sustain a gentle, soothing heat in the same way as the ashes. Therefore, through the method using ashes, colors and coarser earthy parts are usually raised. But through the method using water, the parts raised are more subtle, colorless, and approach the nature of simple wateriness.

Thus, a much finer separation is achieved through the water method than through the one elicited by ashes. Experience has shown this to be true: for one who had distilled oil through ashes received the oil into the receiver scarcely altered. Wishing to separate its parts, he found it necessary to distill it through water. Then, through the repetition of this process, he separated the oil into its elemental parts, such that he extracted the whitest and clearest water from the deepest red oil, while the redness remained entirely at the bottom of the alembic. Therefore, through this <term>mastery</term> <note>magisterium: the highest level of alchemical skill or the resulting "Great Work"</note>, it is necessary to arrive at the determined separation of all elements for every vegetable thing, and for that which proceeds from the vegetable state, and for all similar things.

Through the method of descent, one can specifically obtain the oil of any thing—namely of all vegetables and their likenesses. Through the method of the filter, one can achieve the clarity of any liquid. These things are manifest and known even to those with little or no knowledge; indeed, he who is ignorant of these things knows nothing of this mastery. Let him, therefore, practice, and he will investigate them with ease.

The arrangement for the distillation by ashes is as follows: take a strong pot made of earth and fit it into a furnace similar to the aforementioned sublimation furnace, with the same distance from the furnace ledges, following the same investigation and with similar handles. Upon its bottom, let sifted ashes be placed to the thickness of a finger, and upon these ashes place the distillation vessel. Let it be covered all around with the same ashes up to near the neck of the alembic; afterwards, pour into it the substance intended to be distilled in this manner.

<column-break/>

# BOOK TWO
<note>original: "SECVNDVS"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a brick furnace or kiln. Inside the top of the furnace, several vessels (cucurbits or alembics) are visible, with flames or heat rising around them. The furnace is constructed of rectangular stone or brick blocks with an arched opening at the base for the fire.</image-desc>

Finally, let it be covered with the **alembic** <note>a cap-like apparatus used in distilling</note>, the neck of which receives the neck of the lower **cucurbit** <note>a gourd-shaped chemical vessel</note> within itself, right up to the curve of the alembic's own channel, so that the substance to be distilled finds no way of escape. Afterward, let the alembic be sealed with clay to its cucurbit, and let their joint be made firm; then let the fire be applied beneath until it distills. Indeed, the alembic and its cucurbit should be made of glass. Let the fire be increased according to the requirements of the distillation, until it is seen that everything to be distilled has been drawn off by the great force of the fire.

The arrangement for the second method of **sublimation** <note>the process of heating a substance to a vapor and then condensing it back to a solid</note>, which is done by means of water <note>likely referring to a 'balneum Mariae' or water bath</note>, is similar to this one regarding the vessel and the alembic. It differs from it, however, in this: that in this method an iron or bronze pot is taken and fitted to the furnace, as has been described. Afterward, a layer of grass, or wool, or a similar material should be built up on the bottom of that pot to a thickness of three fingers, so that the cucurbit is not broken. The cucurbit should then be covered all around with the same grass or similar materials, up to near the neck of the alembic. Thin rods should be scattered over these layers, and heavy stones placed upon the rods, which by their weight press down the cucurbit, the alembic, and the bedding itself, holding them firmly and steadily against the bottom of the pot so they do not float when lightened by the water. Such floating might cause the breaking of the vessel and the loss of the substance being distilled. Afterward, water should be poured among the bedding until the pot is full, and the fire applied beneath until the whole has distilled.

The arrangement for that distillation which is done by **descent** <note>a method where the distillate flows downward into a receiver, often used for oils</note> is as follows: a glass **descensory** <note>a specialized vessel for downward distillation</note> is made with its cover, and both are sealed with clay, and the substance intended for distillation is placed inside. Let the fire be made above the head of the vessel. Its distillation will then descend.

The arrangement for that which is done by means of a **filter** is as follows: the liquid to be distilled is placed in a stone basin, and the wider part of a well-washed and moistened filter is placed into the said liquid down to the bottom of the basin. Let the thinner part of it hang outside from the opening of the basin. Under the end of that filter, let a vessel be placed to receive the distillation. Therefore, when the filter begins to drip, first—

<note>original catchword: "stillare" (to drip/distill)</note>

### Technical Vocabulary
**Alembic**: The top part of a still.
**Cucurbit**: The bottom part of a still, containing the liquid to be heated.
**Distillation**: The process of purifying a liquid by heating and cooling.
**Sublimation**: Heating a solid until it becomes a gas and then a solid again.
**Furnace**: The heat source.
**Descensory**: A vessel used for distilling downwards.
**Filter**: A piece of fabric or fiber used to draw liquid out of a container via capillary action.

<summary>This page provides detailed instructions for three methods of distillation: direct heat using glass vessels, a water bath (bain-marie) to prevent breakage, and distillation by descent or filtration. It emphasizes the practical setup, including sealing joints with clay and securing vessels with weights.</summary>

# BOOK

The water with which the material was moistened will distill first. When that ceases, the liquid intended for distillation follows it. If this is not yet clear, it should be returned to the basin <note>original: "concham," referring to the wide-mouthed vessel shown in the illustration</note> as many times as necessary until it distills perfectly clear. Because these matters are all simple, they do not require great proof; therefore, we have remained silent regarding their demonstration.

The description of all vessels for any kind of distillation, as now delivered by us, will be handed down more perfectly in our *Summa of the Perfect Magistery* <note>A reference to the author's more advanced theoretical work</note>.

### DISCOURSE ON CALCINATION

#### Chapter 49

FOLLOWING the account of distillation, let us hand down our discourse on **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of reducing a solid substance to a powder by high heat, often to remove impurities</term>. Calcination is, therefore, the pulverization of a thing by fire through the deprivation of the moisture that holds its parts together. The reason for its invention is so that the burning "sulfurity" <note>the combustible, corrupting principle believed to exist within metals</note> which corrupts and defiles may be destroyed by fire. It is varied according to the diversity of the things to be calcined.

Therefore, bodies <note>metals</note> are calcined, and spirits <note>volatile substances like mercury or arsenic</note> are calcined, as well as other things foreign to their nature, though with different intentions. Because there are imperfect bodies of two kinds—namely the Hard, such as **Venus** <note>Copper</note> and **Mars** <note>Iron</note>, and the Soft, such as **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> and **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, all of which are calcined—it was necessary for them to be calcined with different intentions, both specific and general.

<column-break/>

Nevertheless, they are calcined with one primary intention: that the corrupting and defiling sulfurity may be destroyed by fire. For in this way, every burning sulfurity is consumed from whatever thing it resides in, which cannot be destroyed without calcination. Since the body itself is solid, the sulfurity is protected from burning because of that solidity and its hiding within the continuity of the substance of **quicksilver** <note>mercury, considered the "mother" or base of all metals</note>. Therefore, it was necessary to break that continuity so that the fire, reaching more freely to every tiny part, might burn the sulfurity out of it, and the continuity of the quicksilver within it might not defend it.

There is likewise a common intention in this: the purification of "earthiness." For it has been found that bodies are cleansed through the repetition of calcination and reduction <note>returning the powder back to a metallic state</note>, as we shall show in what follows. The specific calcination of soft bodies, however, is so that along with these two intentions, there may be the intention of... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "duabus" indicates the next page continues with "two intentions"</note>

<summary>This page concludes the instructions on distillation and introduces the theory of calcination, explaining that metals must be reduced to powder so that fire can burn away corrupting "sulfur" trapped within their solid structure.</summary>

...that it may harden and ignite them—which is achieved through the ingenious repetition of calcination upon them. Regarding this, it is expedient for us to define it in the following tradition. For we have found that by this ingenuity, they are manifestly hardened. But **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iuppiter"; the alchemical name for Tin</note> is hardened more clearly and more quickly. Truly, the reason for the discovery of the calcination of spirits is so that they may be better fixed and more easily dissolved into water. Since every kind of calcined material is more fixed than that which is not calcined, and of easier solution. Because the parts of the calcined material, having been made more subtle through fire, are more easily mixed with waters and converted into them. And if you have experienced this, you will find it to be so. Indeed, the calcination of other things serves the requirements for preparing spirits and bodies. Concerning this, we shall define it more extensively in the following sections. However, none of these things belong to the final perfection itself. Therefore, the method of calcination itself is diverse, due to the diversity of the things to be calcined. For bodies are calcined differently than spirits or other things. And bodies, being diverse from one another, are similarly calcined in different ways. For soft bodies have one general method for the purpose of calcination: namely, that both can be calcined by fire alone, and both similarly by the sharpness of salt, whether prepared or unprepared. Therefore, the first method is completed thus: let an iron or earthen vessel be taken, shaped like a shallow dish <note>original: "parapsidis"</note>, whose structure is firm, and let it be fitted to the furnace for their calcination in such a way that beneath it coals can be

<column-break/>

thrown and blown with bellows. Let the **lead** or **tin** be thrown into the bottom of that vessel, which should sit firmly upon an iron tripod or three stone pillars, and be similarly secured to the walls of its furnace by four or three stones wedging it against them, so that the vessel cannot be moved. Indeed, let the shape of the furnace be the same as the form of the furnace for great ignition, which has already been described, and will be described similarly in a more complete discourse. Therefore, let a fire be kindled in it beneath the calcining vessel, powerful enough for the fusion of the body to be calcined. And when that body has created a black skin over itself by the heat of the fire, let that be drawn away from it with an iron or stone shovel which does not allow itself to be burned so as to contaminate the <term>calx</term> <note>A powder produced by heating a metal or mineral; the oxide</note>. Moreover, let this "skinning" be continued until the body itself is turned into powder. If it be **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturnus"; the alchemical name for Lead</note>, let it be placed in a greater fire until its calx is changed into a very bright yellow color. If, however, it be **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, let it be similarly exposed and left until it is changed into a complete whiteness. In this, however, we make the craftsman careful: since Saturn easily returns from its calx back to a metal body, but Jupiter does so with great difficulty. Therefore, lest he happen to err in the exposure of Saturn after its first pulverization to a greater fire, let it not return to a body before its calx is perfected. For it requires a tempering of the fire and a gradual succession of its increase, with caution, until it is fixed in its calx, so that it does not easily return to a body, so that a greater fire can be applied to it.

administered, until its calcination is perfected. Let it not happen that one errs similarly in the case of Jupiter <note>original: "Ioue"; the alchemical name for Tin</note>, because of the difficulty of its reduction <note>The process of restoring a calcined powder back into a solid metal</note>. For if one places its ash for reduction, it may happen that it does not reduce, but instead remains in its prior state or is turned into glass, leading the worker to estimate that its reduction is impossible. We say this because if a great fire is not applied during the reduction of Jupiter, it will not reduce. If, however, the fire is too great, it does not necessarily reduce, but it is possible for it to turn into glass <note>vitrification</note>. This is because Jupiter has a fugitive substance of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> enclosed in the depths of its nature. If this substance remains too long in the fire, it will flee, leaving the body deprived of its moisture; thus, it is found to be more suited for turning into glass than for the melting of a metallic body. For everything deprived of its own moisture gives no fusion except that of glass. Therefore, it is necessarily required that one hastens to reduce it quickly with a very swift impetus of fire. Otherwise, it is not reduced. Let the worker practice this, and he shall know.

The method of calcination for these two bodies <note>Tin and Lead</note>, which is perfected by the sharpness of salt, is as follows: a quantity of salt should be thrown upon their surface. After this quantity is added, they should be frequently infused and mixed with much stirring using an iron rod over the fire, until through the mixture of salt they are turned into ash. Afterward, their lime <note>calx/powder</note> should be perfected by the same method of perfection, according to its specific considerations.

<column-break/>

But there is also a difference in the calcination of these two bodies. Lead <note>Saturn</note> is converted to powder more easily than Jupiter <note>Tin</note> during the first labors of calcination; however, its lime is not perfected more easily than that of Jupiter. The cause of this depends on the fact that Saturn has a more fixed moisture than Jupiter.

For Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars <note>Iron</note>, the method is the same, yet different from the first two. This is due to the difficulty of their melting. The method is that they are hammered into plates and placed in a strong fire, the intention of which is not to melt them. Because of the great quantity of earthiness in them, and the large measure of burning and fugitive sulfurousness, they are easily brought into lime by this method. This is because the proper continuity of the quicksilver is disturbed by the great amount of earthiness mixed into its substance. Therefore, porosity is caused within them, through which the passing sulfurousness can fly away; the fire reaching it for that reason can burn it and lift it away. Through this, the parts are left more rare <note>less dense</note>, and are converted into ash through the discontinuity of that rareness. The manifest experience of this is that when a plate of Venus is exposed to fire, it will give off a sulfurous flame and cause a crushable scale to form on its surface. This is because the combustion of sulfur must necessarily happen more easily from the parts nearest the surface. The style of the furnace for this calcination is the same as the furnace for distillation, except that outside...

...however, it ought to have only one large opening above its head, through which it may freely release the smoke and vapors. And the placement of the materials to be <term>calcined</term><note>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize it or reduce it to powder.</note> should be in the middle of the furnace, so that they may receive the fire freely all around. Indeed, its vessel should be made of earthenware in the shape of a shallow bowl.

The method, therefore, for the calcination of <term>spirits</term><note>Spirits: volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "flee" the fire.</note> is that as they approach <term>fixation</term><note>Fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it no longer evaporates.</note>, the fire should be administered gradually and little by little, increasing it so that they do not flee, until it happens that they can endure the greatest heat. Their vessel should be round and closed on all sides, and the furnace the same as the one last described. Moreover, with a similar furnace and a similar vessel, every substance is likewise calcined. We are, however, spared from the greater labor which is applied in guarding against "flight" <note>original: "fugę" — referring to the evaporation of volatile materials.</note>. For other things do not flee, unless they are spirits or that which approaches the nature of spirits.

### DISCOURSE ON Solution.

#### Chapter L.

Our expanded discourse on solution indicates that the solution of a dry thing is its reduction into water. We say, therefore, that every perfection of solution is brought about with subtle waters, especially those that are sharp, acidic, pungent, and astringent, having no dregs—such as distilled vinegar, sour grapes,

<column-break/>

bitter pears, and likewise distilled pomegranates, and things similar to these. Now, the reason for its invention was the refinement of those things which possess neither <term>fusion</term><note>Fusion: the ability to melt or flow like a liquid.</note> nor <term>ingression</term><note>Ingression: the ability of an alchemical agent to penetrate and transform a base metal.</note>. Because of this lack, the great utility of the "fixed spirits" and those things of their nature was being lost. For everything that is dissolved must necessarily have the nature of salt, or alum, or something similar. It is their nature to grant fusion before they undergo <term>vitrification</term><note>Vitrification: the process of turning into glass through heat, which alchemists often tried to avoid as it "locked" the material.</note>. Therefore, dissolved spirits will likewise provide a similar fusion. Since they agree greatly by their nature with the bodies <note>Metals</note> and with each other, once fusion is achieved, it is necessary for them to penetrate the bodies through that fusion, and by penetrating, to transmute them. This is not achieved without the <term>mastery</term><note>original: "magisterio" — the specialized alchemical technique or "The Work".</note>, which is to say: that after its solution and coagulation, one of the purified, non-fixed spirits should be administered to it. Let it be <term>sublimated</term><note>Sublimation: heating a solid until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note> from it so many times until it remains with it, providing it with a swifter fusion and preserving it from vitrification during that fusion. For it is in the nature of spirits and bodies not to be turned into glass, and to save a mixture from vitrification as long as they are within it. Therefore, the spirit which has better preserved its "spirit-nature" will better defend against vitrification. Furthermore, a spirit that is merely purified preserves its nature better than one that is purified, fixed, calcined, and dissolved. Therefore, it is necessary to mix it in. For from these things results a good fusion, ingression, and a stable fixation. From the works of nature, we can prove that only those things preserving the nature of salts, alums, and the like,

...are soluble. For in considering all his works, we have found that nothing is dissolved except those things. Therefore, whatever things are dissolved, it is necessary that they be dissolved through their own nature, by the repetition of **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or "calx."</note> and **solution** <note>Dissolving a solid into a liquid state.</note>. And for that reason, we prove by this that all calcined things approach the nature of salts or alums; therefore, it is necessary that they accompany one another in their properties.

The method of solution is twofold: namely, by **warm dung** <note>A common alchemical heat source providing gentle, steady warmth through fermentation.</note> and by **boiling water**. These have one intention and one effect. The method by dung is as follows: let the calcined material be placed in a glass **flask** <note>original: "ampulla vitrea"</note>, and over it let there be poured a quantity of **distilled vinegar** <note>original: "aceti distillati"</note>, or something similar, in double the amount. Let its head be well sealed so that it does not breathe <note>i.e., so that no vapors escape</note>, and let it be left under warm dung for three days; afterwards, let the dissolved portion be removed by filtering. Let the part that is not dissolved be calcined again, and after calcination, let it be dissolved again in the same way, until through the repetition of the work upon it, the whole is dissolved.

The method which is done by boiling water is faster. It is done by arranging the calcined material in a flask with vinegar in the same way, with the opening sealed so it does not breathe. This is buried in a cauldron full of water and straw, as we have prescribed in the order for the method of distillation by water. Afterwards, let a fire be lit beneath it until the water boils for an hour. After this, let the dissolved portion be distilled and kept aside. Let the part not dissolved be calcined again, and dissolved again in the same order, until through repetition the whole is dissolved. The description of what has just been said is this:

<column-break/>

### DISCOURSE on Coagulation.
### Chapter 51.

**Coagulation** is the reduction of a liquid thing to a solid substance by the removal of its moisture. There are two reasons for its discovery. One is the hardening of **quicksilver** <note>Mercury; original: "argenti viui"</note>. The other reason for its discovery is the freeing of dissolved medicines from the wateriness mixed into them. It is therefore diversified according to the variety of things to be coagulated. For some things require the coagulation of quicksilver, while others require the coagulation of dissolved things—that is, dissolved medicines.

However, there is a twofold coagulation of quicksilver itself. One is by the removal of all **innate moisture** <note>The essential fluid thought to give mercury its liquid metal properties.</note> from it. The other is by the thickening of that moisture until it hardens. Nevertheless, to congeal it happens with the greatest difficulty and labor, requiring a depth of clear-sighted industry; and we shall relate every ingenious method for its coagulation.

Some have thought the method of its coagulation would be through its preservation in a tempered fire. But when they thought they had coagulated it, after its removal from the fire, they found it to flow just as before. Because of this, they were led into stupor and wonder, arguing vehemently that this could not be achieved. Others, however, supposing by necessity from natural principles that any moisture is converted into dryness by the heat of fire, attempted with the insistence of perseverance to continue its preservation in the fire; and through this continuation...

->XXVIII<-

They reached such a point that some of them converted it into a white stone, others into a red stone, and others into a yellow stone, which possessed neither the ability to melt nor the power to penetrate <note>original: "ingressio" - the alchemical ability of a substance to permeate and transform a base metal</note>. They could not determine the cause of these variations, and therefore they cast it aside. Others, attempting to coagulate it with medicinal preparations <note>term: "medicinis" - in alchemy, 'medicines' refer to chemical elixirs or reagents used to transform substances</note>, did not succeed, but were deceived; either because it did not solidify at all, or because it was imperceptibly thinned away, or because its solidification did not take the form of any known body. They remained ignorant of the cause of these differences. Others, composing ingenious medicines, did coagulate it through projection <note>term: "proiectione" - the act of casting a small amount of a transformative powder or elixir onto a molten metal</note>. But their solidification was not useful, since they converted it into an imperfect body <note>likely a base metal like lead or tin rather than gold or silver</note>. Likewise, they could not see the cause of this. It is therefore necessary for us to explain these causes, so that the craftsman may reach the mastery of its solidification. As we have already sufficiently explained, quicksilver <note>original: "argentum viuum" - literally 'living silver'</note> is of a uniform substance. For this reason, it is not possible to remove its wateriness in a short space of time by keeping it in the fire. Excessive haste, therefore, was the cause of the first error. For since its substance is subtle, it flees from the fire. Thus, an excess of fire is the cause of error for those from whom it escapes. It mixes easily with sulfur, arsenic, and marcasite <note>term: "marchesita" - a term used for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrites</note> because of their common nature. Therefore, through these, it appears solidified, but not in the form of a metal; rather, it is quicksilver mixed with lead. For since these substances are themselves volatile <note>original: "fugitiua" - substances that evaporate or sublimate when heated</note>, they cannot hold it in the struggle of the fire until

<column-break/>

it reaches the nature of a solid body. Instead, they flee with it through the striking of the fire, and this is the cause of error for those who coagulate it in this way. Furthermore, quicksilver possesses a great deal of moisture united to it, which cannot be separated from it except by the cautious violence of fire and by keeping it in its own proper heat. Its "own fire" is that which, by increasing it according to the requirements of what the substance can endure, removes its moisture without leaving behind enough for metallic melting; once that moisture is removed, it will not melt. This is the cause of error for those who coagulate it into a stone that cannot be fused. Similarly, it has parts of sulfur naturally mixed with it—some more, some less—which it is possible to remove through craftsmanship. Since it is the property of sulfur to create a red or yellow color with quicksilver (according to the measure of its quantity), by its removal, it will be the property of quicksilver to give whiteness through the fire. This, then, is the cause of the variety of its colors after its solidification into a stone. It likewise has the earthiness of sulfur mixed in, by which all its solidifications are necessarily stained. This is the cause of error for those who coagulate it into an imperfect body. Therefore, from the diversity of the medicines used for its solidification, it happens that different bodies are created during its coagulation; and similarly from the diversity of the material that is to be solidified. For if the medicine or the material itself contained unfixed sulfur <note>term: "non fixum" - 'unfixed' or volatile sulfur that has not been stabilized to withstand heat</note>, the resulting body must necessarily be

...softness is created from it. If, however, it is fixed <note>Fixed: rendered solid or non-volatile through chemical processes</note>, it must necessarily be hard. And if it is white, it produces white. If indeed it is red, it produces red. And if it is a shade derived from white or red, it must similarly be made so. If it is earthy, it is imperfect. If not, then it is not. And everything that is similarly not fixed creates a leaden <note>original: "lividum," likely referring to a dull, bluish-gray color associated with lead</note> color; but if fixed, it does not, as far as its nature allows. Its pure substance creates a pure body, while an impure substance does the opposite. This same diversity also occurs in mercury alone, without the admixture of sulfur, due to the differences in its cleansing and preparation within medicines <note>Medicines: in alchemy, these are the "elixirs" or agents used to transform metals</note>. Therefore, a deceptive variety arises from the medicine, so that sometimes in its coagulation <note>Coagulation: the process of turning a liquid or vaporous substance into a solid</note> it becomes lead, sometimes Jupiter <note>Jupiter: the alchemical name for tin</note>, sometimes Venus <note>Venus: the alchemical name for copper</note>, and sometimes Mars <note>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</note>, which happens because of impurity. Sometimes, however, it becomes the Sun <note>The Sun: the alchemical name for gold</note>, and sometimes the Moon <note>The Moon: the alchemical name for silver</note>, which must necessarily come from purity and the careful consideration of colors. It is therefore coagulated by its frequent precipitation with violence through the harshness of fire. For the harshness of fire easily removes its wateriness. This is done by means of a vessel whose shape is

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a chemical vessel, specifically a long-necked flask or cucurbit with a rounded base, resting on a textured or tiled surface within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

of great length, in which [the substance] finds a place of cooling for adherence and rest in its sides <note>original: "spondilibus," referring to the joints or vertebrae-like sections of the vessel's neck</note>, because of its length, and finds no way of escape, until by repeated turns it is precipitated to the bottom by the great heat of the fire, with many repetitions, until it becomes fixed. It is similarly coagulated by long-lasting retention in its own fire, using a glass vessel whose neck is of great length and which has the shape of a flask in its belly, with the neck kept continually open so that its humidity can vanish through it. It is also coagulated by a medicine suitable to it. And we shall tell you of that more clearly in what follows, namely in chapter 77. Here, however, we shall likewise describe our complete intention regarding it, according to what we have found through our own experiment. And its medicine is that which adheres most deeply to it and is mixed with it down to its smallest parts before its escape. Therefore, it is necessary to gather it from things suitable to it. All such bodies are of this kind, as are sulfur or arsenic. But because we do not see any of the bodies in their own nature coagulate it, but rather it flees from them however much they may be suited to it, we have therefore considered that no body adheres to it in its depths. Therefore, that medicine must be of a more subtle substance and more liquid fusion than the bodies themselves. Also, among the spirits <note>Spirits: volatile substances like sulfur, arsenic, or mercury that can be vaporized</note>, we do not see a firm and stable coagulation of it occurring while they remain in their own nature, but rather one that is fleeting and causes much infection <note>Infection: in this context, a staining or corruption of the metal's purity</note>.

<meta>The catchword "ali-" indicates the next page begins with a word like "aliquid" or "aliter."</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

This indeed happens, partly due to the flight of the spirits <note>original: "spirituum"; in alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances like sulfur or mercury that vaporize when heated</note>, and partly from the mixture of their earthy and combustible substance. Therefore, it clearly follows from this that from whatever thing that medicine is drawn, it must necessarily be of the most subtle and pure substance, adhering to it by its own nature, and of the easiest liquefaction, and as thin as water, and fixed against the struggle of the fire. For this will coagulate it, and convert it into a solar <note>gold-like</note> or lunar <note>silver-like</note> nature. We have given you the methods of the ingenuity of the medicine, by which you can reach it through them, and we have defined it in its own proper discourse. Therefore, exercise yourself solicitously toward it, and you shall find it. But so that you cannot rebuke us for its insufficiency, we say that this medicine is drawn from the metallic bodies themselves prepared with their sulfur or arsenic; it can likewise be drawn from sulfur or arsenic alone prepared, and similarly from bodies alone. But it is found more easily, more closely, and more perfectly from quicksilver <note>original: "viuo argento"; mercury</note> alone. For nature embraces its own nature more amicably, and rejoices in it more than in a foreign one. And in it is the ease of extracting that subtle substance, since it already possesses a subtle substance in actuality. The methods of finding this medicine are through sublimation <note>the process of heating a solid until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note>, as has been sufficiently narrated by us. The method of its fixation <note>making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> has likewise been narrated in its own chapter. The method for the coagulation of dissolved things is by means of a flask in ashes up to the neck

<column-break/>

with a tempered fire, until the wateriness vanishes.

<margin>This is the description of the long vessels, and of those in which every coagulation is made.</margin>

### DISCOURSE ON FIXATION.
### Chapter 52.

Fixation <note>original: "fixio"</note> is the suitable adaptation of a thing fleeing from the fire. The cause for the invention of fixation itself is so that every tincture and every alteration may be made perpetual in the thing altered, and not be changed. It is likewise diversified according to the multiplicity of things to be fixed, which are namely certain bodies diminished from perfection, such as Saturn <note>lead</note>, Jupiter <note>tin</note>, and Venus <note>copper</note>. And according to the diversity of the spirits themselves, which are namely sulfur and arsenic in their own grade, and quicksilver in another; and in a third, marcasite <note>iron pyrites</note>, magnesia, and tutty <note>zinc oxide</note>, and things similar to these. Therefore, these bodies diminished from perfection are fixed through their calcination <note>heating to high temperatures to reduce to powder</note>, because they are freed by it from volatile and corrupting sulfurousness. And this [is] suffici—

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "sufficiently" (sufficienter), matching the catchword "tiua" (from "corruptiua" or similar) intended for the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the extraction of the "medicine" (the elixir) from metals and spirits, emphasizing mercury as the ideal source, and introduces the theory of "fixation" to stabilize volatile elements like lead, tin, and copper.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, mercury, sulfur, arsenic, fixation, coagulation, calcination, planetary metals, laboratory apparatus</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of chemical processes, specifically focusing on the "fixation" of volatile substances. The catchword "Horum" links to the final sentence of the second column.</meta>

# BOOK

We have already described this in its own discourse, namely in the chapter on **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical properties, often to reduce it to a powder.</note>. Therefore, **sulfur** and **arsenic** are "fixed" <note>Fixation refers to making a volatile substance stable under heat so it no longer evaporates or sublimes.</note> in two ways. The first is by repeating their **sublimation** <note>The process where a solid turns directly into vapor and then back into a solid on a cooler surface.</note> inside a vessel called an **aludel** <note>A pear-shaped earthenware or glass vessel used in alchemy for sublimation.</note> until they remain stable. The goal for speeding up their fixation is to devise a way to repeat the sublimation many times in a short period. This is achieved using two aludels with their double covers in this order: you must never stop the act of sublimation until they are fixed. Therefore, when the substances have ascended in one vessel, they should be cast into the other aludel, and so it is done by turns. Never allow the substances to sit idle, clinging to the sides <note>original: "spondilibus"</note> of the aludel; rather, they should be under the continuous rising of the fire until they cease to rise. For the more you can multiply these repeated sublimations in a shorter time, the more quickly and effectively you will shorten the time required for fixation.

For this reason, a second method of fixation was invented. This is done by casting the substance to be sublimated back down toward the heat, so that it remains in the heat continuously until it is fixed. This is performed using a long glass vessel with a base made of earth rather than glass (since glass would shatter into pieces), joined to the vessel skillfully with a good seal. Using an iron or stone spatula, when the substance has stuck to the sides

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a long-necked glass vessel (aludel) with a rounded base and a stoppered top, used for sublimation or fixation processes.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

of the vessel, it should be continuously cast down to the bottom of the heat, alternating turns, until it is fixed.

The method for the fixation of **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti viui" (mercury)</note> is the same as the method for sulfur and arsenic. They do not differ from one another except in this: sulfur and arsenic cannot be fixed unless their highly flammable parts are first separated from them by a very fine artifice of division through this last method of fixation. Quicksilver does not require this consideration. Similarly, sulfur and arsenic require a more moderate heat than quicksilver does in this method. They also differ because they take a longer time to fix than quicksilver. Furthermore, they rise higher because of their slowness <note>likely referring to the density or weight of the vapors</note> compared to quicksilver. Therefore, they require a longer vessel than quicksilver does for their fixation.

The fixation of **marcasite**, **magnesia**, and **tutia** <note>Technical terms for various minerals: Marcasite (iron pyrite), Magnesia (likely manganese or magnesium ores), and Tutia (zinc oxide or "tutty").</note> is as follows: after the first sublimation, once we have obtained what we want from them and discarded their dregs, we repeat the sublimation upon them. We keep returning whatever remained on top from each of them until they are fixed.

The description of these vessels has already been provided.

->❦<-

<note>Technical vocabulary used in this text: calcination (heating to powder), sulfur, arsenic, fixation (stabilizing a substance), sublimation (vaporizing and re-solidifying), aludel (sublimation pot), quicksilver (mercury), marcasite (iron sulfide), magnesia (mineral ore), tutia (zinc oxide).</note>

### DISCOURSE ON WAXING,
->or the method.<-

### Chapter 53.

Waxing <note>Latin: *Ceratio*; the process of softening a hard substance until it has the consistency of wax</note> is the softening of a hard, non-fusible thing to the point of liquefaction. From this, it is clear that the reason for this invention was so that a substance—which previously could not penetrate a body to alter it because it lacked the ability to melt—might be softened so that it flows and gains the power of penetration. Some have therefore thought that waxing ought to be done with liquid oils and waters. But that is erroneous and entirely removed from the principles of this natural mastery, and is rejected by the manifest works of nature. For we do not see that nature has placed a moisture in metallic bodies that is quickly ended; rather, it is long-lasting (as is clear from the following text) to meet the necessity of their melting and softening. For if nature had placed such [fleeting] moisture in them, it would necessarily follow that by a single firing, the bodies would happen to be totally deprived of moisture. Wherefore it would follow that after one firing, any body could neither be hammered nor melted. Therefore, insofar as we can imitate the works of nature, it is necessary for us to follow nature’s method in waxing. Nature herself waxes at the root of the softening of fusible things with a moisture that, above all other moistures, awaits

<column-break/>

the heat of the fire. Therefore, it is also necessary and expedient for us to wax with a similar moisture. In no other things is this "waxing moisture" found better or more possibly than in these: namely, in sulfur and arsenic closely, but more closely and better in quicksilver <note>mercury</note>. We do not see the moisture of these things leave their earth because of the strong union they achieved in nature's work of mixing. However, in all other things possessing moisture, you will find by experiment that the moisture is separated from its earthy substance during dissolution, and after that separation, the substance is deprived of all moisture. In the aforementioned spirits <note>volatile substances like sulfur and mercury</note>, this happens least of all. Therefore, there is nothing else by which we can be excused from accepting their use in waxing. The method of waxing, then, is through them: let their sublimation be multiplied so many times over the thing to be waxed until, remaining in it with their moisture, they provide a good melting. This, however, will not happen before their perfect cleansing from every corrupting thing. But it seems better to me that their oils should first be fixed by "oil of tartar" <note>a concentrated solution of potassium carbonate</note>, and with those, every competent and necessary waxing for this art should be performed.

<image-desc>A small decorative printer's ornament consisting of a stylized floral or scrollwork design.</image-desc>

<summary>This chapter defines 'ceration' as the process of softening hard minerals to a wax-like state to allow them to penetrate and transform other metals. The author argues that only 'spirits' like sulfur and mercury provide the permanent moisture necessary for this, rejecting the use of common oils or water.</summary>
<keywords>ceration, softening, liquefaction, alchemy, mercury, sulfur, arsenic, oil of tartar, sublimation, metallurgy</keywords>

# BOOK THREE, DI-
### vided from the second.

### A GENERAL DISCOURSE
### on perfection, and on
### those things which pertain
### to perfection.

### Chapter 54.

The distinction of the principles of this mastery <note>original: "magisterium," referring to the highest level of alchemical skill or the production of the Philosopher's Stone</note> having thus been handed down, it is necessary, according to our promise, to pursue the intent of this art completely, in language appropriate to it. This involves the consideration of every thing through which the perfection of this work may be more clearly shown. It also includes the consideration of the necessity of the perfection of the medicine <note>In alchemy, the "medicine" refers to the elixir or tincture capable of curing "sick" or base metals</note>—namely, that it be discussed from what substance that medicine may be better or more closely elicited for the perfection of every imperfect thing. And likewise, the consideration of the techniques by which we may know whether the perfection is complete. Once these things are handed down, the entire knowledge of perfection will be delivered, according to the requirements of our art.

### A GENERAL DISCOURSE
### on the knowledge of the
### principles of bodies.

### Chapter 55.

<column-break/>

Truly, it is not possible to know the transmutation of bodies or of quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum," literally "living silver"</note> itself, unless a true knowledge of their nature, according to their roots, comes upon the mind of the craftsman. Therefore, we shall first make known the principles of bodies: namely, what they are according to their causes, and what good or evil they contain within themselves. Furthermore, we shall show the natures of all those bodies with all their properties—specifically, what the causes of their corruption are—proving them with their own experiments.

### A PARTICULAR DISCOURSE
### on the knowledge of sulfur
### and arsenic.

### Chapter 56.

Therefore, in the first place, affirming the nature of the spirits which are the principles of those bodies—which are sulfur, quicksilver, and its companion <note>likely referring to arsenic, often paired with sulfur in alchemical theory</note>. We say, therefore, that sulfur and arsenic are the fatness of the earth, as has been narrated by us. You may elicit the experience and manifest proof of this through its easy inflammation and its easy liquefaction by heat. For nothing is inflamed unless it is oily.

<summary>This page introduces Book Three of the treatise, outlining the requirements for achieving "perfection" in alchemy and beginning a detailed study of the "spirits" (sulfur and arsenic) that compose metallic bodies.</summary>
<keywords>perfection, medicine, transmutation, quicksilver, sulfur, arsenic, principles of bodies, alchemical mastery</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the chemical principles of minerals, specifically focusing on the internal composition of sulfur and its role in the transformation of metals.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

It does not melt easily through heat, unless it possesses the nature of that heat. Therefore, sulfur and its counterparts contain the cause of corruption: an inflammable substance and an earthy dross. However, they possess a perfecting cause that is intermediate between these two extremes. The cause of corruption in it is thus its "earthiness," because it allows for neither melting nor penetration. Similarly, the inflammable substance is a cause of corruption because it is sometimes unstable and sometimes causes instability, and because it produces blackness in every kind of its genus. Therefore, the cause of perfection in these substances is their intermediate substance; because of its earthiness, it is not disturbed by the penetration which is perfected through a good melting. And because of its subtlety, its impression is not easily removed by flight <note>original: "fugam" - referring to volatility or evaporation</note>. However, their intermediate substance is not a cause of perfection for bodies or for quicksilver <note>original: "argenti viui"</note> unless it is fixed. For when it is not fixed, although its impression is not easily removed, it is nevertheless not permanently stabilized. From these points, it is concluded that it is necessary to divide the intermediate substance. Some have thought it impossible to divide it because of its strong mixture. These people are contradicted by their own manifest works. For they have calcined <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state</term> sulfur itself, and although not to a great extent, it resulted in something that gave neither melting nor inflammation. But this must have happened through division, since sulfur remaining in its natural mixture must necessarily be inflamed and burned. Therefore, through the division of the different substances within it, it remains for the part

<column-break/>

that is more inflammable in it to be separated by artifice from the non-inflammable parts. For if it is possible through calcination to reach the removal of all inflammable parts in it, they must confess, based on their natural works, that a complete division of parts is achieved. But because this depends upon a most subtle artifice, they thought it would be impossible. It is clear, therefore, from what has been set forth in this chapter, that sulfur is not the "truth" of our art <note>The "art" refers to Alchemy</note>, but rather a part of it. And we have already led you to the knowledge of the artifice through which it is possible to arrive at its division. In arsenic, however, because its inflammable parts are loosely mixed in the root of its ore by the action of nature, the artifice of its separation is easy. But arsenic is a tincture <term>tincture: a substance used to change the color or quality of a metal</term> of whiteness, while sulfur is a tincture of redness. In the division of sulfur, therefore, it is necessary to apply great caution.

### DISCOURSE ON THE KNOWLEDGE of the nature of Mercury.

#### Chapter 57.

In quicksilver <note>Mercury</note> it is likewise necessary to remove the superfluities. For it has causes of corruption, namely an earthy substance and a substance of watery nature that is combustible without inflammation. Some have thought, however, that it does not have superfluous earth or impurity. But what they have thought is in vain...

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical purification of sulfur and arsenic, arguing that their "corruptive" parts (earthiness and flammability) must be separated from their "perfecting" parts to be useful in transmutation. It introduces the nature of Mercury as the next subject of refinement.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur, arsenic, mercury, quicksilver, calcination, alchemy, tincture, purification, metallurgy</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the initial properties of quicksilver (mercury) and its role as a foundational substance in the alchemical Great Work. The catchword "mentis" (of the mind/intellect) suggests a transition from theoretical understanding to practical application.</meta>

# BOOK

...by the same. For we see that it possesses much lividness <note>original: "liuiditatis"; referring to a leaden, bluish-gray discoloration</note> and not whiteness. And similarly, through a simple artifice, we see black and foul earth emanate from it—namely through washing, the method of which we shall describe. But because it is a twofold process to elicit perfection from it and to perfect it, it is therefore necessary to prepare it by a twofold grade of cleansing. Furthermore, two cleansings of Mercury <note>The author uses "argentum vivum" and "Mercurius" interchangeably for quicksilver.</note> are necessary. One is by **sublimation** for the <term>medicine</term> <note>The "medicine" or "elixir" is the substance used to transmute base metals into gold or silver.</note>, and this is hinted at here. The other is by **washing** for **coagulation**, and that will be hinted at later. For these are found in the paragraph beginning, "The second indeed." If we wish to create the medicine from it, then it is necessary to cleanse it from the foulness of its earthiness through sublimation, lest in the <term>projection</term> <note>The act of casting the alchemical catalyst onto molten metal.</note> it creates a livid color; and we must destroy its fugitive wateriness, lest it makes the entire medicine fugitive during projection; and we must save its moderate substance through the medicine. It is the property of this substance not to be burned, to defend against burning, and it is that which does not flee and makes things fixed. We prove, therefore, by manifest experiences, that it is perfective. For we see that quicksilver adheres more to quicksilver and is more friendly to the same; after that, to gold, and after these, to silver. Therefore, it follows from this that it is more of their own nature. We see that other bodies do not have such great conformity to these, and therefore we truly find that they participate less in its nature. And whatever things we see are saved more from burning, those we consider to possess more of its nature. And so it follows,

<column-break/>

that quicksilver itself is most perfect and a savior against burning, which is the ultimate state of perfection. The second grade of cleansing, however, is for its **coagulation**. And it is such that a single day's washing of its earthiness suffices for it. The method is this: Let an earthen pan be taken, and in it let the quicksilver be placed, over which is poured a small quantity of the strongest vinegar, or some similar thing sufficient to cover it. Afterward, let it be placed over a slow fire—lest it boil—and let it be stirred continually with the fingers against the bottom of the pan, so that the quicksilver is divided into the likeness of a most subtle white powder, until all the vinegar has evaporated and the quicksilver returns to itself. Afterward, wash away and cast aside that foul and black matter which you see has emanated from it. Repeat this many times until you see the color of its earthiness change, and the clear mixture perfectly transformed into a white and heavenly <note>original: "cœlestino"; likely a bright, sky-blue or clear hue indicating purity.</note> color, which is the sign of perfect washing. When, therefore, it has reached that state, let one cast upon it the medicine of its coagulation, and it will be coagulated into a <term>solific</term> <note>Gold-making (from Sol/Sun).</note> or <term>lunific</term> <note>Silver-making (from Luna/Moon).</note> substance, according to how they have been prepared. We shall place the description of this in the following sections. From what has been said, it is clear that quicksilver is not perfective in its own nature, but rather that which is produced from it by our artifice. And a similar case can be made for sulfur and its partner. It is not possible, therefore, to follow nature naturally in these things, but rather through our **natural artifice**.

<summary>This page describes the dual purification of mercury—sublimation for creating the alchemical elixir and washing for solidifying it into gold or silver. The author emphasizes that while mercury is naturally "friendly" to precious metals, it requires human "artifice" to reach its perfect, non-combustible state.</summary>

### DISCOURSE ON THE KNOW-
### ledge of true marcasite, ma-
### gnesia, and tutty.

#### Chapter 58.

Regarding the other spirits, it is necessary that we provide the traditional knowledge of their nature; these are namely marcasite <note>likely iron pyrites or similar glittering ores</note>, magnesia <note>not modern magnesium, but likely manganese ore or a similar dark mineral</note>, and tutty <note>zinc oxide collected from furnace flues</note>, which produce a great impression upon the bodies of metals. Therefore, let us declare what they are through their tests in this chapter. We say, then, that marcasite possesses a twofold substance in its creation: namely, of mortified quicksilver <note>original: "argenti vivi," meaning mercury</note> approaching fixation, and of burning sulfur. We have discovered by clear experience that it possesses a sulfurous quality. For when it is sublimated, a manifest, burning sulfurous substance emanates from it. Even without sublimation, its sulfurous nature is similarly perceived. For if it is placed in the fire, it does not endure the heat before it is ignited and burns by the inflammation of the sulfur. Truly, it is shown by the senses to possess the substance of quicksilver. For it grants to Venus <note>the alchemical name for copper</note> the whiteness of pure silver, just as quicksilver itself does; and we see that in its sublimation it grants a celestial color and possesses a manifest metallic luster. These things make the craftsman certain that it contains these substances in its root. Magnesia, however, contains a more turbid sulfur and a quicksilver

<column-break/>

that is more earthy and dross-filled; and you will be able to prove clearly by the same experiments that its sulfur is likewise more fixed and less flammable, and that it approaches more closely to the nature of Mars <note>the alchemical name for iron</note>. Tutty, truly, is the smoke of those white bodies, and this is perceived by clear proof. For when the smoke of a mixture of Jupiter <note>tin</note> and Venus <note>copper</note> is cast off, adhering to the rafters of the smiths' workshops, it produces the same impression as tutty. And because metallic smoke does not return to a solid state, neither does tutty, unless mixed with some body. Therefore, since it is the smoke of white bodies, it does not turn white bodies yellow, but rather red ones. For yellowness is nothing other than a determined proportion of red and white. Therefore, because of its subtlety, it penetrates more deeply into the body of the metal. Thus it alters the metal more than its own body does, and it adheres more firmly during the trial with little effort, as has already been told to you. Whatever things are altered, therefore, must be altered by the power of quicksilver, or sulfur, or things similar to them, since these alone share a nature common to the metallic bodies themselves.

### ON THE ESSENCE OF GOLD.

#### Chapter 59.

Having expanded our discourse on the bodies, let us now relate their intimate essence. First concerning the Sun <note>original: "Solis," the alchemical name for gold</note>, afterwards the Moon <note>original: "Luna," the alchemical name for silver</note>, and finally the others, according to what shall seem expedient, along with their proofs, which by...

<summary>This page describes the chemical composition and properties of marcasite, magnesia, and tutty, identifying them as combinations of mercury and sulfur. It explains how these substances interact with base metals like copper and tin to alter their color and properties.</summary>
<keywords>marcasite, magnesia, tutty, mercury, sulfur, copper, tin, gold, silver, alchemy, sublimation, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the elemental composition of metals, specifically focusing on the generation of gold from mercury and sulfur.</meta>

# BOOK

->The Experiment<-

Gold is understood through experiment. Therefore, the sun <note>original: "sol"; in alchemy, Sol refers to Gold</note> is created from the most subtle substance of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti vivi"; mercury</note> and a most clear fixation; and from a small substance of the world's sulfur, of pure redness, fixed and clear, and changed from its own nature, which tints that same mercury. And because diversity occurs in the colors of that sulfur, it is necessary that the yellowness <note>original: "citrinitatem"</note> of gold likewise possesses diversity. Indeed, some gold is more intense in its yellowness, while some is less. That it is composed of the most subtle substance of quicksilver is weighed and judged by the fact that quicksilver receives it easily. For quicksilver receives nothing except that which is of its own nature. That it possesses a clear and clean substance is manifested by its shining and radiating brilliance, revealing itself not only by day, but even in the night. That it possesses a fixed substance without burning sulfurousness is evident through every operation of it in the fire; for it is neither diminished nor inflamed. That it is itself a tinting sulfur is evident by this: that when mixed with quicksilver, it transforms it into a red color; and when it is sublimated from bodies by a strong fire so that their subtlety ascends, it creates with it a most yellow color. It is evident, therefore, that when pure generates with pure, it produces a pure color; with the impure, it produces the impure. He who requires a demonstration to prove its yellowness lacks sense, for this is perfected through sight. Therefore, the most subtle substance of quicksilver brought to fixation, and its purity, and the most subtle matter of sulfur, fixed and non-burning, is the entire essential matter of gold itself.

<column-break/>

Because it contains a greater amount of quicksilver, it allows for a greater ingress of quicksilver into itself. Therefore, whatever you wish to alter, alter it according to this exemplar, so that you may lead those things to its equality. And we have already given the method for this. And because it possessed subtle and fixed parts, its parts were able to be greatly condensed. This was the cause of its great weight. Through much decoction <note>term: decoctio; a process of "cooking" or maturing substances through steady heat</note> worked by nature, there was made from it a gradual resolution, a good thickening, and a final mixture, so that it melts when fired. From the preceding points, it is evident that a large quantity of quicksilver is the cause of perfection, while a large quantity of sulfur is the cause of corruption. Uniformity in substance is the cause of perfection; the opposite is the cause of corruption. Therefore, if sulfur falls upon that quicksilver improperly, it is necessary that various corruptions be introduced according to that diversity. For the sulfur that falls upon it can be entirely fixed and non-combustible <note>original: "non adustibile"</note>, or entirely combustible and fleeting in the nature of sulfur; or fleeting and not in the nature of sulfur; or it may hold part of the fleeting and part of the fixed; or hold the nature of sulfur in part, and in part not; or be entirely clean, or moderately unclean; or of great quantity, overpowering the mixture; or of small quantity, overpowered within it; or neither overpowering nor overpowered; or white, or red, or mediocre. From all these diversities, it was necessary for diverse bodies to be created in nature, and things similar to these; all of which we must recount with manifest proofs.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical composition of gold, asserting it is formed from highly purified mercury and a specific "tinting" sulfur. It details how the proportions and purity of these two elements determine the quality, weight, and color of the resulting metal.</summary>
<keywords>gold, quicksilver, sulfur, alchemy, fixation, decoction, elemental purity, metallic transformation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of metallic composition, specifically focusing on the "Sulphur-Mercury" theory where different grades of these two principles create different metals.</meta>

### DISCOURSE ON THE UNDER-
### standing of the nature of the Moon <note>original: "Lunae"; in alchemy, this refers to Silver</note>.

#### Chapter 60.

IT is now therefore clear from what has been said before, that if pure, fixed, clear red sulfur <note>The "fixed" quality refers to a substance's ability to withstand fire without evaporating</note> falls upon a pure substance of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti viui"; Mercury</note>, not overcoming it, but rather being overcome by it in a small quantity, pure gold is created from this. If, however, it is pure, fixed, clear white sulfur that falls upon the substance of quicksilver, let it be pure silver. If it does not exceed in quantity, it nevertheless has a diminished purity compared to the purity of gold, and a thicker density than gold. The proof of this is that its parts are not thickened to such an extent that it matches the weight of gold, nor does it have as fixed a substance as that. The proof is its loss through fire; and its sulfur, which is neither fixed nor incombustible, is the cause of that. And that the sulfur in it is of this disposition is proven by its slight inflammation. Moreover, it must not be thought impossible for the same thing to be "fixed" in relation to one thing and not to another. For the sulfurousness of Silver, when compared to the sulfurousness of gold, is not fixed and is combustible. But compared to the sulfur of other bodies, it is fixed and non-combustible.

<column-break/>

### DISCOURSE ON THE UNDER-
### standing of the nature of Mars <note>original: "Martis"; in alchemy, this refers to Iron</note>.

#### Chapter 61.

IF, however, there is fixed earthy sulfur mixed with fixed earthy quicksilver, and both of these are not pure but of a leaden whiteness, and the quantity of sulfur is in the highest excess, iron is made from these. For the excess of fixed sulfur prevents melting <note>original: "fusionem"</note>. Therefore, what remains from this is that sulfur loses its speed of melting through the work of fixation more quickly than quicksilver does. We see, however, that non-fixed sulfur melts more quickly than quicksilver. From these things, the cause of the speed or slowness of melting in each of the bodies is made manifest. For that which has more fixed sulfur receives melting more slowly, but that which has more burning sulfur receives melting more easily and quickly. This, we believe, has been demonstrated clearly enough by us. Therefore, that the fixed substance itself makes melting slower is manifested by this: that it is never fixed unless it is calcined <note>term: calcinatio (calcination); the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often reducing it to a powder</note>, and once calcined, it allows no melting. Therefore, it must impede melting in all things. That it is not fixed unless it is calcined is clear by the experiment of one who tries to fix it without calcining, for he finds that it always flees <note>evaporates or escapes</note> until it is turned into earth. Its likeness is that of the nature of lime. This, however, happens very little in quicksilver, because it can be fixed without being turned into earth, and be fixed at the same time...

<summary>This page explains the alchemical composition of Silver (Moon) and Iron (Mars), attributing their specific properties like density and melting point to the ratio and purity of their constituent Sulfur and Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>Silver, Iron, Alchemy, Sulfur-Mercury theory, Calcination, Fixation, Metallurgy, Quicksilver</keywords>
