# BOOK

Similarly, it is possible for it to be fixed <note>fixio: the process of making a volatile substance solid or resistant to fire</note> by turning it toward the earth. It is not fixed by haste in its solidification, which is achieved through precipitation <note>sudden chemical falling out of solution</note>, for then it is changed into earth <note>meaning it becomes a dry, non-metallic powder</note>. However, by its successive and repeated sublimation <note>the process of heating a substance until it vapors and then solidifies again</note>, it is similarly fixed, yet it is not turned into earth; rather, it yields a metallic fusion. This is evident and proven to anyone who has experienced both methods of fixing it—until its completion—both the hasty precipitation and the slow method through successive sublimations. For he saw and found it to be exactly as he received it in our writings. And this is because it possesses a viscous and dense substance: the sign of which is its grinding, along with its imbibition <note>absorption of liquid</note> and mixture with other things. For its viscosity is clearly felt through its great adherence. Moreover, that it possesses a dense substance is clearly seen by the one-eyed man <note>original: "monoculus"; likely a metaphorical reference to a focused observer or a specific alchemical authority</note> through its appearance and its heavy weight. For it outweighs gold when it is in its own nature; and it is likewise of a very strong composition, as has been told. From these things, therefore, it remains that it can be fixed without the consumption of its moisture, and without conversion into earth. For on account of the good adherence of its parts and the strength of its mixture, if its parts are thickened by fire in any way, it no longer allows itself to be corrupted, nor does it allow itself to be further raised into smoke by the entry of a smoky flame into it: because it does not suffer rarefaction <note>becoming less dense</note> due to its density and its lack of "combustibility," which is brought about by sulfurousness <note>original: "sulphureitas"; the presence of sulfur, believed to cause metals to burn or tarnish</note>. Through this, therefore, there has been found by a true discovery

<column-break/>

a wonderful double genus of two secrets. One, namely, is the cause of the corruption of each of the metals by fire: of which one cause is the inclusion of burning sulfurousness in their substance, which through inflammation diminishes them and exterminates them into smoke with ultimate consumption, no matter how much Quicksilver <note>original: "arg. vi." (argentum vivum)</note> of good fusion exists within them. Another cause is the multiplication of the exterior flame penetrating over them, and resolving them into smoke along with itself, whatever degree of fixation may be in them. The third cause is the rarefaction of them through calcination <note>reducing a metal to powder by heat</note>. For then the flame or fire can penetrate into it without hindrance and exterminate it. If, therefore, all the causes of corruption meet, it is necessary that the bodies be corrupted to the highest degree. But if not all meet, the speed of corruption of each body is lessened according to the lessening of those causes. The second genus is the goodness which is considered in bodies through it. Because Quicksilver allows itself to be divided into the parts of its composition for none of the causes of extermination (because it either withdraws from the fire with its whole substance, or remains standing in it with its whole substance), the cause of perfection is necessarily noted in it. Therefore, let the blessed and glorious Most High be praised, who created it and gave it a substance and properties of substance which it does not happen that any other thing in nature possesses; so that in it can be found this degree of perfection, through some artifice, which we find in it as a "near power" <note>a potentiality ready to be acted upon</note>. It is that which overcomes the fire, and is not overcome by the fire: but rests in it amicably, rejoicing in it.

### DISCOURSE ON the knowledge of the nature of copper.
### Chapter 62.

Returning therefore to our purpose, we say that if there should be an impure sulfur—thick and fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating</note> in its greater part, but in its lesser part unfixed, red, and leaden-colored, neither overcoming nor being overcome in its entirety—and if it should fall upon thick quicksilver <note>original: "argentum viuum" (mercury)</note>, it is necessary that copper <note>term: "aes" (copper or bronze)</note> be created from it. It is possible to bring forth proof of all these things through the data of its own nature. For when it is set down for inflammation, you will be able to discern a sulfurous flame from it, which is a sign of unfixed sulfur. And by the loss of its quantity through exhalation, it is signified to have fixed sulfur through its frequent combustion. For from this comes the slowing of its melting <note>term: "fusionis" (the process of melting or liquefying a metal)</note> and the hardening of its substance, which are signs of its great quantity of fixed sulfur. That it is, moreover, red and impure, joined to impure quicksilver, is signified by the senses; therefore it requires no other proof. Thus, you may draw out the secret by experiment. For you see every thing changed into earth by the action of heat, being dissolved with ease and reduced to the nature of water. This happens because of the thinning of the parts by fire. Therefore, a thing more subtle in its own nature, when reduced to this earthy nature, thins more. Therefore, it is dissolved more; and so that which is most subtle is dissolved most. From these things, therefore, the cause of corruption is clear

<column-break/>

and the infection of these two bodies, namely Mars <note>The alchemical name for Iron</note> and Venus <note>The alchemical name for Copper</note>: since it is through sulfur of a great quantity, both fixed and unfixed: a little in Venus, but very little in Mars. Therefore, when fixed sulfur reaches fixation through the heat of fire, its parts are thinned: but that part which is in a state of readiness for the dissolution of its substance is dissolved: the sign of which is the exposure of these two bodies to the vapor of vinegar. For through that, the alum-like quality <note>term: "aluminositas" (the astringent or salt-like property of alum)</note> of their sulfur blooms on its surface, created by that thinning heat. And if you place these two bodies in a sharp <note>term: "ponticum" (a sour, astringent, or acidic liquid)</note> liquor, many of their parts will be easily dissolved by boiling. And if you look into the mines of these two, you will clearly see the dissolved substance of the alum-like quality dripping from them and adhering to them; which alum-like quality is changed into water through its sharpness and easy dissolution. For nothing is found to be sharp and easily soluble other than alum, and that which is of its nature. Truly, the blackness in each of these two bodies is created at the fire because of the unfixed sulfur which is enclosed in them: much indeed in Venus, but a little in Mars, and approaching the nature of the fixed. And therefore it is not possible for such an impression to be easily removed from Mars. Now, therefore, it has become clear that from unfixed sulfur, melting occurs and melting is aided; but from fixed sulfur, melting does not occur, and melting is hindered. From fixed quicksilver, however, it is not necessary that melting not occur, or that melting be hindered. It is necessary to know that this is true: that by no ingenuity of melting has anyone been able to preserve sulfur in the melting after its fixation.

Quick silver <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; mercury</note> can indeed be fixed through the frequent repetition of sublimation until it undergoes a good fusion. From this, therefore, it is manifest that those bodies containing more quicksilver are of greater perfection, while those containing less are of lesser perfection. You should strive, therefore, in all your works to let the quicksilver prevail in the mixture. And if you can achieve perfection through quicksilver alone, you will be an investigator of the most precious perfection, and of that perfection which surpasses the work of nature. For you will be able to cleanse it inwardly, a point which nature does not reach. The proof that those things containing a greater quantity of quicksilver are of greater perfection is, namely, their easy reception of quicksilver. For we see that the bodies of perfection <note>referring to noble metals like gold and silver</note> receive quicksilver in a friendly manner. From the preceding discourse, it follows that there is a double sulfurousness in bodies. One is indeed enclosed in the depths of the quicksilver at the beginning of its mixture, while the other is added later. One of these is removed with labor, but it is impossible to remove the other by any ingenuity of the arts. That which is perfected by fire, to which our operation can appropriately and usefully arrive, is already made one with it in the same wax-like fusion <note>term: "cerationem"; the process of softening a substance to a wax-like consistency</note>. And this is proven by experiment, for we see combustible sulfurousness destroyed by fire, but fixed sulfurousness not at all. If, therefore, we say that bodies are cleansed by calcination <note>term: "calcinatio"; heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to powder</note>, you should understand this to be from the earthy substance which is not united at the root of its nature. Since it is not possible to cleanse what is united through the ingenuity of fire, unless there comes the medicine of quicksilver, hiding and tempering it, or separating it from the mixture. Moreover, the separation of the earthy substance united at the root of the metal's nature from its mixture occurs either by elevation <note>sublimation</note> with things that elevate the substance of quicksilver and leave behind the sulfurousness—due to their affinity with them, such as tutty <note>term: "tutia"; zinc oxide collected from furnace flues</note> and marcasite <note>term: "marchesita"; iron pyrites or similar metallic ores</note>—since they are vapors, the greater part of which is a quantity of quicksilver rather than sulfur. And you see the experience of this, because if you join these with bodies by a strong and sudden fusion, the spirits will lead the bodies with them in their flight. Therefore, you can elevate with them, or by washing through the mixture of quicksilver which we have described. For quicksilver holds what is of its own nature, but rejects the other.

### ledge of the nature of tin.

With this investigation proposed, following our purpose, we indicate that if sulfur is in the root of the mixture, participating little in fixation, white with a non-pure whiteness, not prevailing but prevailed upon, mixed with quicksilver that is partly fixed and partly not fixed, and of a non-pure white: tin will follow from it. And you will find the proof of these things through preparation. For if you calcine tin, you will perceive from it the stench of sulfur; which is a sign of non-fixed sulfur. And because

It does not give off a flame, nor should you think it is fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" means a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to fire.</note>. For it does not give off a flame because of fixation, but because of the dominance of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti viui" (Mercury)</note> in the mixture, which protects it from burning. Thus, a double sulfurousness is proven in tin <note>original: "stagno"</note>, and a double substance of quicksilver. One sulfurousness is this: that when it is calcined <note>reduced to powder by heat</note>, it stinks like sulfur. Through the first, the experience of the mixture is proven. The other is proven through its continuation in its ash <note>original: "calce"</note> over the fire; because it is more fixed, it does not stink. Indeed, a double substance of quicksilver is proven in it. One of these is not fixed, the other is fixed. For before its calcination it "shrieks" <note>The "cry of tin" is a distinct crackling sound made when bending the metal</note>, but after a triple calcination it does not shriek; this is because the substance of the fugitive quicksilver that caused the shrieking has flown away. That the substance of quicksilver is the cause of the fugitive shrieking is proven by the washing of lead with quicksilver. For if lead is washed with quicksilver, and after its washing you melt it with a fire that does not exceed the heat of its melting point, a part of the quicksilver will remain with it, which brings a shriek to the lead and converts it into tin. Conversely, you can observe this through the mutation of tin into lead. For through the multiple repetition of calcination, and by applying a fire more suitable for the administration of reduction, it is converted into lead—especially when it is calcined with a great fire through the removal of its "skin" <note>likely referring to the dross or surface oxidation</note>. And you will be able to be certain of the diversities of these substances through the ingenious methods of

<column-break/>

preserving them, or by proper instruments and the method of fire dividing them; to which we have arrived with persistence, and we have seen with our own certainty that we have affirmed the truth through this. Therefore, it is necessary for us to make you certain of what remains after the removal of the two non-fixed elements from Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note>—namely, sulfur and quicksilver—so that you may completely understand the composition of Jupiter itself. It is, therefore, a leaden color: heavy, like lead, yet participating in a greater whiteness than lead. It is, therefore, the purest lead. And in it, there is an equality of fixation of the two components, namely quicksilver and sulfur. However, there is not an equality of quantity, because in the mixture, the quicksilver prevails; the sign of this is the ease with which quicksilver in its natural state enters into it. Therefore, if there were not a greater quantity of quicksilver in it, it would not easily adhere to it when taken in its natural state. For this reason, it does not adhere to Mars <note>Iron</note> except by the most subtle ingenuity, nor to Venus <note>Copper</note> because of the scarcity of quicksilver in their mixtures. From this, therefore, it is clear that it adheres to Mars with the greatest difficulty. To Venus, however, it adheres more easily because of the greater abundance of quicksilver in her; the sign of which is her easy melting, while that of Mars is most difficult. Indeed, the fixation of these two substances approaches a firm fixation, yet it is not a perpetual fixation because of that. The proof of this is the calcination of the body itself, and after calcination, its exposure to the strongest fire. For through this, no division will occur, but the whole substance will ascend <note>sublime or evaporate</note>, though it will be more purified.

We see, therefore, that the substance of sulfur in tin <note>original: "stagno"; the author uses the Latin term for tin</note> is more easily separated when burning than it is in lead. You can see the proof of this through the easy hardening of Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note>, its calcination <note>The process of heating a metal to reduce it to a powder or ash</note>, and the improvement of its brilliance. Therefore, we have considered that these corrupting elements were not in its original root, but came to it later. And because they were not strongly joined to it in the first mixing, they can therefore be easily separated. For this reason, the alterations in it are of quick work—namely, its purification, hardening, and its fixation <note>Making a volatile substance stable under heat</note>. You can clearly consider the causes of these things through what has already been handed down by us. 

And because after these operations—namely, calcination and reduction—we considered the expression in its smoke through a great elevation of fire, and we saw that it pertained to a lemon-yellow color <note>original: "citrinitatem"</note>; since this is a property of calcined sulfur, we estimated with a true estimation, of which we were certain, that it contains within itself much of the nature of fixed sulfur. Therefore, those who wish to see the truth in this science of ours should study the investigation of all these things with diligence of work, until through it they find the principles of the bodies themselves and the property of the spirits with a certain, not conjectural, discovery, which we have handed down in this volume of ours sufficiently for the requirements of the art.

### DISCOURSE ON THE KNOWLEDGE of the nature of Saturn.

#### Chapter 64.

<column-break/>

It remains for us, therefore, to set down a description of Saturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note>. And we say, it is not changed into the nature of Jupiter by the repetition of calcination and reduction, except because it has a more impure substance mixed from two coarser substances, namely sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argento vivo"; Mercury</note>. And because the burning sulfur in it is more adhesive to the substance of its quicksilver, and because it has more of the substance of fixed sulfur in its composition than Jupiter does. We shall bring forth the proofs of these things through manifest experiences. 

That it is of greater earthy dross <note>original: "feculentie"</note> than Jupiter is made manifest by sight, and by its washing from quicksilver, in that more dross flows from it through washing than from Jupiter; it undergoes the first degree of calcination more easily than Jupiter, which is a sign of much earthiness. For we find bodies of more earthiness are of easier calcination, and those of little earthiness are of more difficult calcination. The proof of this is the most difficult thing: the complete calcination of the Sun <note>The alchemical name for Gold</note>. And because its foulness is not rectified by the repetition of calcination as it is in Jupiter, this is a sign of greater foulness in its principles from its own nature than in those of Jupiter. Truly, that the quantity of combustible sulfur is more adhesive to the substance of the quicksilver in it than in Jupiter is signified by this: that it does not separate from it into smoke, although what remained of it in the lower depths must necessarily be a sign of one of three things...

it is left behind, either with no quantity of combustible sulfur in it, or very little, or sulfur that is closely joined in the proximity of the principles at the root of the mixture. But we are made certain by its odor that it contains some quantity, and not a small amount, but rather much, because the smell of sulfurousness does not leave it in a short time. Therefore, we have considered with a certainty that the burning sulfur is uniformly mixed within the substance of the quicksilver <note>original: "argenti viui"; Mercury</note> alongside that non-burning sulfur which approaches the nature of the fixed. Therefore, when its smoke ascends, it necessarily ascends with the non-burning sulfur, whose property it is to create a lemon-yellow color <note>original: "citrinitatem"</note>. Indeed, that a greater quantity of non-burning sulfur exists in <term>Jupiter</term> <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note> has been established by us as a truth, because we see its entire color change into yellow, whereas the color of <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note> changes to white during its calcinations <note>The process of heating a metal to reduce it to a powder or ash</note>. And thus, the way is opened for us to investigate the cause of this work: why, during calcination, Jupiter is changed into a hard state more easily than Saturn, yet not more quickly in the slowness of its melting than Saturn. And this is because the cause of a natural hardness is sulfur and fixed quicksilver. The cause of melting, however, is twofold: namely, quicksilver and combustible sulfur. One of these is sufficient for the perfection of fusion in every degree, whether with ignition <note>heating until glowing</note> or without ignition—namely, quicksilver. Because there is a great quantity of quicksilver in Jupiter that is not truly fixed, there remains in it

<column-break/>

a great speed of melting, and it is not easily removed from it. The cause of softness is also twofold: namely, quicksilver and combustible sulfur. Because the burning sulfurousness is removed from Jupiter more easily than from Saturn, and thus one of the causes of softness is removed, it must necessarily become hardened when calcined. Saturn, however, because it has both causes of softness strongly joined, is not easily hardened. Yet there is a difference between softness caused by quicksilver and softness caused by sulfur: for softness from sulfur is yielding <note>compressible</note>, while softness from quicksilver is extensible <note>malleable or ductile</note>. This is necessarily proven by the fact that we see bodies with much quicksilver have great extension, while bodies with little have little. Therefore, Jupiter is extended more easily and finely than Saturn, Saturn more easily than Venus <note>Copper</note>, Venus more than Mars <note>Iron</note>. <term>Luna</term> <note>Silver</note> is extended more finely than Jupiter, and <term>Sol</term> <note>Gold</note> more finely than Luna. It is clear, therefore, that the cause of hardening is fixed quicksilver or fixed sulfur. The cause of softness is the opposite. The cause of fusion <note>melting</note> is twofold: non-fixed sulfur and quicksilver of any kind. But non-fixed sulfur is necessarily the cause of fusion without ignition. You see the experience of this thing clearly through the projection of arsenic upon bodies that are difficult to melt; for it makes them easy to melt and without ignition. Likewise, quicksilver is also a cause of easy fusion. But the cause of fusion with ignition is fixed quicksilver. Therefore, the cause of the hindrance of any fusion is fixed sulfur.

Through this, therefore, you will be able to elicit the greatest secret. Since bodies containing a greater quantity of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti vivi"; mercury</note> are found to be of the highest perfection, it follows that bodies with a large quantity of quicksilver—though diminished from perfection—must necessarily be close to the perfect state. Likewise, it happens that bodies containing much sulfurousness <note>original: "sulphureitatis"; referring to the combustible, "burning" principle in alchemy</note> are of the greatest corruption. For this reason, it is clear from what has been said that Jupiter <note>The metal Tin</note> approaches the perfect most closely, as it participates more in perfection. Saturn <note>The metal Lead</note> participates less. Venus <note>The metal Copper</note> even less, and Mars <note>The metal Iron</note> the least of all, upon which perfection depends. On the other hand, regarding the medicine <term>Medicine: In alchemy, the "elixir" or substance used to transform base metals into gold or silver</term> that completes and supplies what is lacking, it happens that it more perfectly thins the thickness of the bodies to their depths, cloaking their darkness under the substance of a shining brightness. From this perspective, Venus is the most capable of being perfected, Mars less so, Jupiter even less, and Saturn the least of all. From these things, therefore, it has been discovered through truthful investigation of the diversity of bodies, that diverse materials are found through preparation. For one body requires a medicine that is hard and combustible <note>original: "ignibile"</note>, while another requires a body that is soft and non-combustible. This medicine softens and thins to the depths, leveling the substance; that medicine hardens and thickens what is hidden within. It is expedient for us, therefore, to pass from these things to the medicines, since manifest experiences provide us with the causes for the invention of diverse medicines, showing what they leave diminished and what they lead to completion.
->∴<-

<column-break/>

### Chapter 65.

We prove, therefore, that spirits <note>Volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic</note> are more assimilated to bodies because they unite with bodies more easily and more amicably than anything else in nature. By this reasoning, it was brought to us in our first discovery that these spirits are the true medicine for the alteration of bodies. Therefore, we have exercised ourselves in every kind of ingenuity we could, so that through them we might transform each of the imperfect bodies, by a firm mutation, into a perfect lunar <note>Silver</note> or solar <note>Gold</note> body. For this reason, it necessarily happens that diverse medicines are created from them, based on the diverse intention of the things to be altered. Since there are two kinds of medicine: the alteration of quicksilver, which can be coagulated into perfection, and the bodies diminished from perfection. These latter are manifold: some are hard and combustible, others are soft and non-combustible. The hard and combustible ones are Mars <insert>Iron</insert> and Venus <insert>Copper</insert>. The soft and non-combustible ones are Jupiter <insert>Tin</insert> and Saturn <insert>Lead</insert>. It is necessary that the perfecting material be similarly manifold. For quicksilver requires one perfecting material, while the bodies to be transformed require another. And even among those, the hard and combustible (Venus and Mars) require one kind, while the soft and non-combustible (Saturn and Jupiter) require another. And since both of these kinds differ from one another in their nature, it necessarily happens that they require diverse medicines. For Mars and Venus are of one genus, but they differ in a certain specific property.

# BOOK THREE

For indeed, this one <note>referring to Iron</note> is not meltable, while that one <note>Copper</note> is indeed meltable. Therefore, one medicine is perfected for **Mars** <note>Iron</note> and another for **Venus** <note>Copper</note>. And this one <note>Iron</note> is entirely impure, while that one <note>Copper</note> is not. Furthermore, this one participates in a certain dark whiteness, while that one participates in redness and greenness; these qualities similarly impose a necessity for diversity in the medicine. For bodies of another kind of softness—namely **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> and **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>—since they differ in a similar way, it is necessary that they likewise require diverse medicines. For this one <note>Tin</note> is indeed clean, while that one <note>Lead</note> is not. But truly, all these bodies are changeable, being made now into **Lunar** <note>Silver</note> and now into **Solar** <note>Gold</note> bodies; therefore, it is necessary that there be a twofold material for each one. One is yellow, changing the body into a yellow Solar body; the other is white, similarly changing it into a white Lunar body. Since, therefore, a twofold material—namely Solar and Lunar—is found for each of the four imperfect bodies, there will be eight medicines in total that perfect all bodies into a good form. **Quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum viuum*, Mercury</note> is similarly perfected into Solar and Lunar forms; therefore, it happens that there are two differences of the medicine that alters it. Thus, there will be ten medicines in all, which we have found in their totality for the complete alteration of any imperfect thing. Truly, however, because of the length of time, the urgency of labor, and the diligence of great investigation, we wish to be excused from the labor of finding these ten medicines through the benefit of a single medicine. And we have found through long and most labori-

<column-break/>

-ous inquiry, and with certain experience, **one medicine**: by which indeed the hard is softened, and the soft body is hardened, and the fugitive <note>volatile substances like Mercury</note> is fixed, and it is illuminated according to an unspeakable splendor, even that which exists above nature. Therefore, it is expedient to bring forth a single discourse on all these medicines, with their causes and manifest proofs of experience. First, therefore, the series of the ten medicines; and then indeed of all the bodies; then truly of Quicksilver; and then finally, we must pass to the medicine of the perfecting **Mastery** <note>original: *magisterij*, the Great Work or Philosopher's Stone</note>; although it requires an imperfect preparation. Therefore, lest we be bitten by the envious on account of the insufficiency of the art's tradition, let us first bring forward a narration concerning the preparations of the imperfect things, stating the necessity of the cause of their invention: by which they may be made suitable in our craft to receive the medicine of the **Whiteness** <note>original: *albedinis*, the stage of Silver</note> and **Redness** <note>original: *rubedinis*, the stage of Gold</note> in every degree, and to be perfected by the same. Then, truly, a sufficient and appropriate narration of all the medicines must be added.

### Chapter 66.

From the discourses already narrated by us, it appears what nature leaves behind as superfluous or diminished in its works in each of those which are imperfect bodies, according to a sufficient part; but according to the completion which in the higher—

<margin>Catchword: ro</margin>

...we have omitted, we shall here complete with sufficient discourse. First, therefore, since the imperfection of changeable bodies happens to be of two kinds: namely the soft and non-igniting <note>original: "ignibilia," meaning metals that melt easily without needing to be brought to a glowing heat</note>, such as Saturn <note>Lead</note> and Jupiter <note>Tin</note>. For the hard ones are either not fusible, or fusible only with ignition, such as Mars <note>Iron</note> and Venus <note>Copper</note>: the former indeed is not fusible <note>in the furnaces of the time</note>, while the latter melts with ignition. Nature, in its forming, has necessarily taught us that from the diversity of those essences, we must administer different preparations at the root of their nature according to their requirements. There are, therefore, two necessary bodies of one kind of imperfection to be transformed: namely lead, which is called *mela* <note>likely a corruption of the Greek 'molybdos'</note>, and by the art is called Saturn; and the "shrieking" lead, which is called white lead <note>Tin</note>. And in this science, it is called Jupiter. These are different from one another in the depths of their hidden parts, innate from the root of their nature, and similarly in their manifest appearance. For Saturn is dark, leaden-colored, heavy, and black, and being without a "shriek," it is totally mute. Jupiter, however, is white, slightly leaden, but shrieking much, and producing a tinkling with a slight sound. We shall show you the difference in their depths, along with manifest experiences, from their necessary causes. From these, it happens that the craftsman of good mind may gather the order of preparation according to the greater or lesser degree. First, therefore, let us narrate the preparation according to the order of the bodies; but afterwards also of the coagulable quicksilver <note>term: *argentum vivum* (Quicksilver/Mercury)</note> itself. But first of the one kind, namely of the soft; after this, indeed, of the other. Let the preparation of Saturn and Jupiter be placed first among the bodies of the first kind; but afterwards

<column-break/>

indeed of the others, according to their determined order. Because in the preparation of bodies, nothing superfluous is to be removed from their depth, but rather from their manifest <note>surface or outward</note> part.

### Chapter 67.

The preparation of Saturn is applied to a manifold essence, and similarly that of Jupiter, according to the degree of their manifold perfection, or their distance from it. Since, therefore, one thing comes from the corrupting elements in its depth from the innate root of its nature—namely the earthiness of sulfurousness and the earthy impurity of quicksilver, mixed by essential nature in the principles of their creation. But another thing comes upon them after their first mixture, bringing corruption: and these are the burning sulfurousness of the first kind, and its impurity, and the foul substance of quicksilver; all of which are things corrupting the substance of the perfection of Saturn and Jupiter. But it is impossible for one of these to be removed by a medicine of the first order of any industry. The other, however, happens to be removed with a little assistance. This indeed happens to be impossible to remove because, in the principles of the proper nature of this kind of bodies, they were mixed into the true essence, and became the true essence. And therefore, since it is not possible to remove the true essence of any thing in nature, there remains...

# BOOK THREE

Remaining, it was not possible for them to destroy these corrupting elements. For this reason, certain philosophers <note>The author refers to earlier alchemists or natural philosophers who gave up on the "Great Work."</note> thought that it was impossible to arrive at the Art through this path. But we, even in our own time, seeking this knowledge <note>original: "scientiam"</note>, arrived at this same conclusion: that by no method of preparing the spirits were we able to illuminate the bodies with the fullness of their bright splendor, without them happening to be entirely stained and rather turned black. Because of this, we were similarly led into a state of stupor, and for a long space of time, we ceased our work under the shadow of despair. 

However, returning to ourselves and tormenting ourselves with the immense afflictions of meditative thought, we observed that the bodies diminished from perfection exist as foul in the depths of their nature, and nothing bright is found in them, since it is not in them according to nature. For nothing is found in a thing which is not already within it. Since, therefore, nothing perfect is found in them, it necessarily follows that nothing superfluous is found in them either—neither in the separation of their diverse substances nor in the depths of their nature. And so, through this, we found that there was something diminished in them, which must necessarily be completed by a material suitable to it, which fills what is lacking. That which is diminished in them is, namely, a scarcity of quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: Mercury (argentum vivum), considered by alchemists to be the essential "spirit" or "seed" of all metals.</term>, and an improper thickening of the same. Therefore, the completion in them will be the multiplication of quicksilver, its good thickening, and its permanent fixation. This, moreover, is perfected through a medicine created from it. For since this medicine is brought into being from quicksilver, through its benefit

<column-break/>

it hides and covers the foulness of those bodies by cloaking them in the splendor of its clarity; it draws out the splendor and converts it into brightness. For when quicksilver, prepared in the medicine through our craftsmanship, has been cleansed and reduced into a most pure and shining substance: when cast upon those things diminished from perfection, it will illuminate them and perfect them by its fixation. We shall describe this medicine, however, in its own discourse. 

It remains, therefore, from what has been laid down, that the discovery of a twofold form of perfection is necessary. Either one through a material which separates the foul substance from the mixture; or the other through a medicine which covers that foulness by cloaking it with the splendor of its own brightness, and honors it by illuminating it. Since, therefore, nothing superfluous is found, but rather something diminished in the depths of the bodies: if anything superfluous needs to be removed, it is necessary that it be taken away and moved from the manifest part of its nature where it has been added, using various preparations. It is expedient for us to describe these in this discourse: first, in the same discourse, concerning Jupiter <note>Tin</note> and Saturn <note>Lead</note>, and finally the others according to their order.

### ON THE PREPARATION of Saturn and Jupiter.
### Chapter 68.

Saturn and Jupiter are therefore prepared by multiple preparations according to the necessity of their approximation to greater perfection, namely by a common and a special mode of preparation.

<summary>The author describes a breakthrough in alchemical theory, moving from removing impurities to "completing" metals using a mercury-based medicine. He introduces the specific preparations for Lead (Saturn) and Tin (Jupiter).</summary>
<keywords>quicksilver, mercury, alchemy, medicine, perfection, Saturn, Jupiter, lead, tin, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the purification of metals, specifically focusing on the "imperfect" metals and the chemical processes required to bring them toward perfection.</meta>

->BOOK<-

Indeed, there is a common path through multiple degrees of approach toward perfection. For there is one degree of approach, namely the brilliance <note>original: "fulgur," referring to the flash or luster of a pure metal</note> arising from a clean substance; another is hardness, achieved through the ignition of its fusion; and the third is fixation, through the removal of the fugitive <note>volatile or easily evaporated</note> substance. Therefore, they are cleansed and made brilliant in three ways: either by purifying agents, or by the method of calcination <note>heating to high temperatures to create an oxide or powder</note> and reduction <note>returning a substance to its metallic state</note>, or by solution.

By purifying agents, they are cleansed in two ways: either when reduced to a lime <note>powdered oxide</note>, or in their natural metallic state. When reduced to a lime, they are purified in this manner: either by salts, by alums, or by glass. And it is done thus: when the body—namely Jupiter <note>the alchemical name for Tin</note> and Saturn <note>the alchemical name for Lead</note>—has been heated, then water of alums or salts is poured over their lime, or glass is mixed with it, and it is reduced back into a metallic body. This process is repeated for these bodies in alternating turns until they show themselves to be completely clean. For since salts, alums, and glass melt with a different fusion than other bodies, they are separated from them, carrying away the earthy substance with them and leaving behind only the purity of the metals.

In their natural state, they are purified similarly and by the same method. This is done by filing these two bodies <note>Tin and Lead</note> very finely; after this, they are treated with the same alums, salts, and glass, and afterward reduced back into a body. This is repeated in alternating turns until they appear cleaner. They are also cleansed by a wash of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, the method of which we have already described. Similarly, both types of bodies are purified here by

<column-break/>

repeated turns of calcination and reduction, with a sufficient amount of fire, until they appear cleaner. For by this process, these diminished bodies are cleansed of perfection's two corrupting substances: one which is flammable and fugitive, and the other which is earthy dross. This is because fire elevates and consumes every fugitive substance. Likewise, in the method of reduction, the fire divides every substance of the earth according to its proportion.

We have written about this proportion in our other volume, titled *On the Investigation of Perfection* <note>original: "De investigatione perfectionis," a famous medieval alchemical text attributed to Geber</note>, which precedes this book in order. In that work, we wrote whatever we investigated according to the reasoning of our mind; but here, we have determined according to the order of science what we have completely seen and touched.

These metals are also similarly purified by the solution of their substance—the method of which we have already stated—and likewise by the reduction of that which has been dissolved from them. For that which is found by this same method of preparation is cleaner and more perfect than by any other kind of preparation. No method compares to this one, except that which is perfected through sublimation <note>turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it</note>; therefore, it is equal to it.

There is also a similar preparation in them: the hardening of their soft substance through the ignition of their fusion. This is done by contriving to mix into their depths the fixed substance of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, or of fixed sulfur, or its equivalent; or with hard and non-fusible things, such as lime, marcasite <note>iron pyrite or similar metallic minerals</note>, and tutty <note>zinc oxide collected from furnace chimneys</note>.

# THIRD BOOK

For these things are united and mingled with them: they harden those substances until they are melted, before they are set on fire. And this same thing is completed similarly through a perfecting medicine <note>The "medicine" or "elixir" refers to the alchemical agent used to transform or "heal" base metals into noble ones.</note>, the description of which we shall set down. There is likewise another method of preparation, by the removal of the fugitive substance <note>original: "fugitiuę substantię" – referring to volatile components like sulfur or mercury that escape when heated.</note>. And this indeed is perfected through preservation, after that stage of calcination <note>term: Calcination – the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or ash.</note> of those things, in a fire proportional to them. And because a specific order in the methods of preparation is necessarily required, we shall therefore set down the complete order of them. First, therefore, let every fugitive and burning, corrupting substance be cleansed from them. Then, indeed, let the earthy superfluity be removed. After this, let them be dissolved and reduced, or let them be completely washed through a bath of quicksilver <note>original: "argen. vi." (argentum vivum) – Mercury.</note>. And this order is both useful and necessary.

However, the special preparation of these bodies is manifold; first, indeed, that of Jupiter <note>term: Jupiter – the alchemical name for Tin.</note>. One way is through calcination: and through this its substance is hardened more than happens to Saturn <note>term: Saturn – the alchemical name for Lead.</note>. Also through alums <note>term: Alumina – mineral salts used as astringents or fixing agents.</note> similarly: for these specifically harden Jupiter. Another way is through its preservation in the fire of its calcination. For through this it loses its "creaking" <note>original: "itritidorem" (stridorem) – Tin was famously known for the "cry of tin," a crackling sound it makes when bent.</note> and likewise the brittleness of its bodies: which similarly does not happen to Saturn, because it has no creaking, nor does it break the bodies; and through the repetition of calcination, it similarly loses its creaking through the sharpness of salt. Secondly, there is a special preparation of Saturn, namely through calcination by the sharpness of salt: for through this it is hardened, and it is specifically whitened through talc, and through marcasite <note>term: Marcasite – an iron sulfide mineral, often confused with pyrite or used as a source of sulfur.</note> and tutty <note>term: Tutia – zinc oxide, often found as a crust in smelting furnaces.</note>. Indeed,

<column-break/>

we have determined all the methods of preparation more completely in the book entitled *On the Invention of Perfection*: since in this one we have summarized their main points.

### ON THE PREPARATION
### of Venus.
### Chapter 69.

Following the order of the preceding things, let us describe the preparations of the hard bodies. First, therefore, of Venus <note>term: Venus – the alchemical name for Copper.</note>: and then indeed of Mars <note>term: Mars – the alchemical name for Iron.</note>. There is, therefore, a manifold method of preparing Venus. One is indeed through elevation <note>term: Elevation – another word for sublimation, where a solid is turned into vapor and then condensed.</note>: while another is performed without elevation. The method by elevation is this: namely, that tutty be taken, with which Venus is most compatible, and let it be united with it through ingenious means. Then indeed let it be placed in its sublimation vessel to be sublimated, and through the highest degree of fire, let its more subtle part be elevated, which is found to be of the most shining splendor. Or let it be mixed with sulfur in the smallest parts, and afterwards let it be elevated by its aforementioned method of elevation. Without sublimation, however, it is prepared either by things that perform cleansing: or in its ash: or in a body, such as tutty, salts, and alums: or by a bath of quicksilver, the method of which we have brought forward: or through calcinations and reductions, as has been described in others: or through solutions and the reductions of that which is dissolved to the nature of a body: or it is cleansed by a bath of quicksilver, just like the remaining bodies diminished from perfection.

### ON THE PREPARA-
tion of Mars <note>original: "Martis"; in alchemy, Mars is the symbol and name for Iron</note>.
### Chapter 70.

The preparation of Iron is likewise manifold. For some is completed through sublimation <note>the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note>, while others are done without sublimation. That which is done through sublimation is performed with arsenic: the method of which is this. We must contrive as deeply as we can to unite non-fixed arsenic to the iron, so that it melts with it during fusion. Afterward, let it be sublimated in a vessel proper for sublimation. This preparation is found to be better and more perfect among the others. There is also another preparation of this same Iron, by means of sublimated arsenic, [passing it] from it many times until some quantity of the arsenic remains. For if this is reduced <note>returned to a metallic state</note>, there will emerge a white, fusible, clean, and prepared [metal]. There is similarly a third method of its preparation through its fusion with lead and tutty <note>zinc oxide, often found as a crust in furnaces</note>. For from these, it flows clean and white. But lest we seem to have spoken insufficiently, since we promised to determine the ingenious softening of the two [hard] bodies, and the hardening of the soft ones by means of calcination <note>reducing a metal to powder by heat</note>: therefore let us not omit that: but first of the soft, and afterward of the hard. And it is this: namely, that precipitated quicksilver <note>mercury</note> be dissolved, and the calcined body which is intended to be hardened be dissolved. And let both these solutions be mixed, and from these, by turns, let the calcined body be mixed, by grinding, and by soaking <note>original: "imbibendo"; a process of adding liquid to a dry substance</note>,

<column-break/>

and by calcining, and by reducing, until it becomes hard and fusible under fire. This same thing happens to be completely perfected with lime, and tutty, and calcined marcasite <note>iron pyrite or similar metallic minerals</note>, when they are dissolved and soaked. And indeed, the cleaner these things are, the more perfectly they transform. Hard bodies are likewise softened with a similar ingenuity. And it is this: namely, that they are joined and sublimated with arsenic so many times: and after the sublimation of the arsenic, they are roasted with the proportion of their fire: the method of which we have narrated in the *Book of Furnaces*. And finally, let them be reduced with the expression of their fire, in the speech of its saying <note>likely referring to the specific instructions or "formula" previously mentioned</note>, until they soften in fusion, according to the requirement of the body's hardness. And indeed, all these alterations are of the first order: without which the Mastery <note>original: "magisterium"; the successful completion of the alchemical Great Work</note> is not perfected.

Then let it be placed over a slow fire, and let it be allowed to be heated, so much so, that with the fingers

### THE FIRST
### Lesson.

### Chapter 72.

Let us, therefore, first bring forward a universal discourse on medicines, along with their causes and clear experiments. We suggest, then, that unless every superfluity is removed from imperfect bodies—whether by medicine or by the method of preparation—they will not be perfected. That is to say, all superfluous sulfurousness <term>sulphureitas: the active, combustible principle that alchemists believed caused impurities and "burning" in metals</term> and every unclean earthiness must be taken from them: specifically so that they are not separated from the mixture during melting <note>original: "in fusione"</note> after the projection <note>the act of casting the alchemical medicine onto the molten metal</note> of the altering medicine. When you have found this to be the case, you already possess one of the characteristics of perfection.

### THE SECOND
### Lesson.

<column-break/>

Similarly, unless the medicine also brightens and alters the body into a white or lemon-yellow color <note>representing the stages of silver and gold</note> according to the intention you seek—bringing about a clarity of pleasantness through the brilliance of its luster—the diminished bodies are not totally perfected in their completion.

### THE THIRD
### Lesson.

Furthermore, unless it specifically brings about a lunar or solar melting <note>original: "fusionem lunarem aut solarem"; referring to the melting points and characteristics of silver (Moon) or gold (Sun)</note>, it is not alterable in its completion: for it does not remain stable under testing, but is entirely separated from the mixture and withdraws. This has been determined by us more extensively in what follows, demonstrated in the chapter on cupellation <term>capitulo decineritio: the process of refining ores in a "cineritium" or cupel to separate precious metals from lead</term>. Moreover, unless the nature of the medicine is made perpetual through a firm alteration of its impression, its change is of no value: for the impression does not remain, but vanishes.

### THE FOURTH
### Lesson.

Furthermore, unless it brings about the weights of perfection, it does not change under a firm and true completion of nature, in which there is no deception through the error of belief. For the weight of nature <term>pondus naturæ: the specific gravity or characteristic density of a true metal</term> is one of the signs of perfection. It is clear, therefore, since the differences of perfection...

# BOOK

Since there are five <note>referring to the five classes of metals or conditions previously discussed</note>, it is necessary that the medicine of our mastery <note>original: "magisterij"; refers to the Great Work or the alchemical art</note> introduces these specific differences during the process of projection. Through this, it becomes clear from what sources our medicine is drawn. For it is drawn from those things which are most closely joined to the bodies, and which, being transformative, adhere to them in a friendly manner deep within. Therefore, searching among all other things, we have found in our discovery no other thing more friendly to the natures of the bodies than quicksilver <note>original: "argentum viuum"; mercury</note>. By applying our work to it, we find it to be the true medicine for things capable of change, leading to completion with a true and uniquely remarkable transformation.

### THE FIFTH
->Instruction<-

<margin>There remains yet one Instruction not yet found, nor to be found by me, as if from a Printer <note>This marginal note appears to be a later skeptical or frustrated addition regarding the missing text or the difficulty of the art.</note></margin> It remains, therefore, for us to describe specifically its substance and the differences in the properties of that substance. Since we have found that it does not change without the administration of its natural alteration, we have likewise found that it must necessarily be prepared; for it cannot be mixed in the depths of a metal without that method of preparation. That is to say, its substance must be made such that it is mixed in the depths, even to the very core of the changeable body, without ever being separated. This does not happen unless it is greatly refined through a certain and determined preparation in the chapter on sublimation <term>Sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the mercury</term>. Similarly, its impression does not remain unless it is fixed <term>Fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</term>; nor does it give luster unless the most brilliant substance is drawn from it

<column-break/>

by the ingenuity of its method, and by the manner of its operation through a suitable fire. Furthermore, it does not provide a perfect fusion <note>melting or blending</note> unless caution is applied during its fixation, using this method to soften hard things and harden soft things. It is such, however, that it is preserved with a sufficiency of its proportioned moisture, according to the requirements of the fusion being sought. Through this, therefore, it is clear that its preparation should be so readied and administered that the most brilliant and purest substance is created from it. Then, indeed, let it be fixed, and let caution be shown to the same—namely, that the craftsman be practiced in the administration of the fire and in the manner of its fixation. By this, the moisture can be erased from it to such an extent that it suffices to complete a perfect fusion. That is to say, if you seek by this to soften bodies that are hard to melt, a slow fire should be applied at the beginning of its creation. For a slow fire preserves moisture and perfects fusion. But if you seek to harden soft things, a violent fire should be composed for it. For such a fire consumes moisture and disturbs fusion. Indeed, it is expedient for a craftsman of good mind to necessarily consider all these rules in every medicine. And it is likewise necessary to bring many other considerations to the change of weight, along with their causes and proper order. The cause of great weight, therefore, is the fineness of the substance of the bodies and the uniformity in their essence. For by this alone can their parts be made dense, since nothing falls between them; and the densification of parts is the cause of weight and its perfection.

<summary>This page discusses the central role of mercury (quicksilver) in the alchemical "medicine" and details the importance of sublimation, fixation, and the careful regulation of fire to achieve the desired density and fusion in metals.</summary>
<keywords>mercury, quicksilver, sublimation, fixation, alchemy, fusion, slow fire, violent fire, density, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical preparations, specifically categorizing the different "orders" of medicines used to transform metals.</meta>

->**BOOK THREE**<-

It is therefore clear that it is necessary to seek out subtlety through the craftsmanship of the work, both in the preparation of the bodies <note>The "bodies" refer to the base metals being worked upon.</note> and in the method of the perfecting medicine itself. For the greater the weight of the transformed bodies, the greater the perfection found through artistic investigation. Therefore, a useful discourse on medicines is completed if we describe the differences between all medicines. We have indeed suggested that there must necessarily be a threefold difference in medicines. One is of the **first order**, another of the **second**, and another of the **third**.

I call a medicine of the **first order** any preparation of minerals which, when cast upon bodies diminished from perfection, impresses an alteration upon them; yet this does not bring about a sufficient completion, for the altered metal may still change and be corrupted when the impression of that medicine totally evaporates. An example of this is any whitening sublimation of Venus <note>Copper</note> or Mars <note>Iron</note> which has not undergone fixation. Of this kind is every addition of the color of the Sun <note>Gold</note> and Moon <note>Silver</note>, or of Venus, mixed over a furnace of cement, placed with *zimar* <note>original: "zimar"; likely referring to verdigris or a specific mineral salt</note> and the like. For this changes the metal with a mutation that does not last, but rather diminishes itself through exhalation.

We call a medicine of the **second order** any preparation which, when cast upon bodies diminished from perfection, alters them toward some degree of completion, while leaving behind some characteristics of corruption entirely: such as the calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to remove volatile impurities</note> of bodies, by which everything fleeting is destroyed. To this class belongs the medicine that perpetually turns the Moon <note>Silver</note> yellow, or perpetually whitens Venus <note>Copper</note>, while leaving other corrupt qualities within them.

I call a medicine of the **third order** any preparation which, when it reaches the bodies, removes all corruption by its casting, and perfects them with every characteristic of completion. This medicine is one and only one. And therefore, by it, we are excused from the labors of inventing the ten medicines of the second order. He calls the work of the first order "lesser," the second "middle," and the third "greater." And this is a sufficient distinction of all medicines.

<column-break/>

### ON THE MEDICINE
### of the first order.

### Chapter 72

Following the permission of the determined order: since one medicine is for the bodies <note>Metals</note> and another for Quicksilver <note>original: "argenti viui"; Mercury</note>. And of the bodies, one is of the first order, another of the second, and another of the third; and similarly for Quicksilver. Let us therefore describe the differences of all medicines of the first order, then the second, and finally the third. First regarding the bodies, and afterwards we shall deliver the account of the medicine for Quicksilver with a complete discourse and in the proper order.

We say, therefore, that of the medicine of the first order for bodies, one is for hard bodies and another for soft. Of the hard bodies, one is for Venus...

<summary>This page defines the three "orders" of alchemical medicines, ranging from temporary surface alterations (first order) to partial permanent changes (second order) and finally the perfect, universal transformation (third order).</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, first order medicine, second order medicine, third order medicine, transmutation, Venus, Mars, Sol, Luna, Quicksilver, calcination, sublimation</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical transmutations, specifically focusing on the "first order" of medicines used to alter the color and purity of base metals.</meta>

# BOOK
## Page LX

->**On the Medicines of the First Order**<-

<column-break/>

There are various medicines: some for Venus <note>original: "Veneris"; alchemical symbol for Copper</note>, others for Mars <note>original: "Martis"; alchemical symbol for Iron</note>, and others for the Moon <note>original: "Lunae"; alchemical symbol for Silver</note>. For Venus and Mars, the goal is the pure whitening of their substance. For the Moon, however, it is a reddening, accompanied by a yellowing of pleasant brightness. Because Venus and Mars are entirely impure, a first-order medicine does not bring about reddening with a bright appearance in them; they are not fit to receive the brilliance of redness until a preparation arrives that brings forth such brightness. Let us therefore describe first all the medicines of Venus, and afterwards those of Mars, which fall into the first order.

There is, then, a medicine for whitening Venus, one by means of quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; Mercury</note>, and another by means of arsenic. Indeed, the whitening medicine using quicksilver is completed in this way: first, precipitated quicksilver is dissolved, and similarly, the calcined form <note>term: calcinatio; the process of heating a metal until it turns into a fine powder or ash</note> of Venus is dissolved. These two solutions are mixed into one. Then they are coagulated, and their medicine is projected <note>term: projiciatur; "projection" refers to casting a medicinal powder or liquid onto a molten metal to transform it</note> upon the body of the metal itself; for this whitens and cleanses it. Furthermore, and in another way, precipitated quicksilver is dissolved, and litharge <note>term: litargirium; lead monoxide, often used as a flux</note> is dissolved, and these two solutions are joined into one. Then the calcined body of the metal intended for whitening is dissolved and joined with the aforementioned solutions, and then it is coagulated. Afterwards, these are projected upon its body, for it is whitened by this. In another way, a quantity of quicksilver is sublimated <note>term: sublimatio; heating a substance so it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</note> alternately from its body until a portion of the quicksilver remains with it through complete ignition. Then, it should be ground by frequently soaking it in distilled vinegar,

<column-break/>

so that it may be better mixed into its depths. After this, it should be roasted, and finally, in a similar way, it should be sublimated from quicksilver, and soaked and roasted again. This work should be repeated upon it so many times until a great quantity of quicksilver rests within it through complete ignition. This is a good whitening of the first order. In another way, quicksilver in its own nature should be sublimated over precipitated quicksilver so many times until it is fixed <note>term: figatur; "fixation" is the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> within it and allows for melting. Then it should be projected upon the substance of Venus, and it is whitened remarkably. In another way, the Moon <note>Silver</note> is dissolved, and litharge is dissolved, and the solutions are joined. By these, the substance of Venus is whitened. But it is indeed whitened better if quicksilver is made permanent in all the medicines. It is also whitened by sublimated arsenic: as when the calcination of Venus is taken, and the sublimation of the same is repeated over it until it remains with it and whitens it. But unless you exert yourself with the methods of sublimation, the arsenic will not persist in it through any alteration. And it is easy, after the first degree of sublimation, to repeat it a second time, as we described in the sublimation of marcasite <note>note: a common iron sulfide mineral often used in alchemical experiments</note>. It is also whitened in another way: you may project sublimated arsenic onto the Moon <note>Silver</note>, and then project this whole mixture upon Venus; for it whitens remarkably. Or first mix litharge, or dissolved burnt lead, with the Moon; then afterwards cast arsenic over this. This whole mixture, when projected upon Venus, whitens it, and it is a good whitening of the first order.

<summary>This page details specific alchemical recipes for the "whitening" of copper (Venus) using mercury, arsenic, and silver. It emphasizes the necessity of repeated sublimation and fixation to achieve a stable color change in base metals.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Venus, copper, quicksilver, mercury, arsenic, sublimation, calcination, litharge, silver, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of metallic transformations, specifically focusing on the whitening of copper and iron, following the previous sections on the purification of lead and tin.</meta>

->BOOK THREE<-

Or let sublimated arsenic <note>original: "arsenicum sublimatum"; likely arsenic trioxide, used to whiten metals</note> be cast upon dissolved and reduced litharge <note>original: "litargirium"; lead oxide</note> alone, and all of this cast upon a fusion of Venus <note>term: Venus (copper)</note>: for this whitens it with a courtly appearance. Alternatively, let Luna <note>term: Luna (silver)</note> and Venus be mixed together, and over these let every whitening medicine be cast. For Luna is a greater friend to arsenic than any other of the bodies. And for that reason, it removes the brittleness caused by it. Saturn <note>term: Saturn (lead)</note> is the secondary friend: and therefore we mix it with them. In another way, we melt sublimated arsenic until it forms into lumps; then we cast it lump by lump upon the Venus. Indeed, we command it to be cast in lumps rather than in powder: because powder ignites more easily than a lump, and therefore vanishes more easily and is consumed before it falls upon the ignited body. Its redness is also removed in another way, namely with tutty <note>original: "tutia"; zinc oxide found in the flues of furnaces</note>, and it is whitened. But because the whitening of tutty is not sufficient, it only turns it yellow. And any yellowing is related to whitening. The method for this is that every kind of tutty be dissolved and calcined <note>term: calcined (reduced to powder by heat)</note>, then likewise the Venus; let both these solutions be joined, and with these let the substance of the Venus be turned yellow; and if you work with tutty, you will find it profitable. It is also whitened by sublimated marcasite <note>original: "marchesita"; likely iron pyrites</note>, just as with sublimated quicksilver <note>term: quicksilver (mercury)</note>; and the method is the same.

### THE WHITENING of Mars.
### Chapter 74.

It remains, therefore, to narrate the whitenings of Mars <note>term: Mars (iron)</note> created from medicines proper to it, which are according to the essence of the first order, according to which it does not have a proper fusion: therefore it is expedient for us to whiten it with a medicine that causes it to melt. Thus, every whitening medicine for Venus is likewise for Mars, with the preparation of the same order. But its specific flux <note>original: "fusiua"; a substance used to promote melting</note> is arsenic of any kind. Therefore, with whatever it is whitened and melted, it is expedient that it be joined with quicksilver and washed until all impurity is removed from it, and it becomes white and fusible. Or let it be heated with the vehemence of fire, and let arsenic be cast upon it: and when it has been melted, cast a quantity of Luna upon it. For when it is united with it, it is not separated from it by any light artifice. Or let it be calcined and every soluble alum-like quality be washed from it (which brings the infection of corruption) by the method of solution already described. Then, let cleaned arsenic be sublimated from it through several sublimations; and let this be repeated many times until some of it is fixed <note>term: fixed (made resistant to heat)</note> with it. Then, let it be soaked with a solution of litharge in alternating turns, by mixing, and stirring, and roasting in turns, and finally reducing it with the fire which we taught in the reduction of Jupiter <note>term: Jupiter (tin)</note> from its lime. For from this, it will emerge white, clean, and fusible. Or let it be reduced in its lime with sublimated arsenic alone, and it will emerge white, clean, and fusible.

# BOOK

But it is necessary for the craftsman to apply the same caution, just as we taught regarding the deep fixing of arsenic in the repetition of the sublimation of Venus <note>original: "Veneris," referring to copper</note>. It is likewise whitened through marcasite and tutty <note>a crude zinc oxide found in the chimneys of furnaces</note>, with the skill and industry that we have described to you. But the whitening or cleansing of these is not sufficient.

### CONCERNING THE MEDICINE
->For the yellowing of Silver.<-

### Chapter 75.

Following therefore the order of the aforementioned things, let us speak with certain truth regarding the medicine for Silver <note>original: "Lunae"</note>, a substance that turns it yellow <note>original: "citrinantem"</note> in the order of the first kind. It is indeed a medicine which adheres to it in its depths, and by adhering colors it, whether through its own nature or through the artifice of this mastery. Let us therefore speak of the medicine which adheres to it by its own innate root. Then, indeed, the artifices by which we make a thing of any kind adhere with a firm entry. We extract it either from sulfur, or from quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, or from a mixture of both. But through sulfur it is indeed more diminished; through quicksilver, however, it is more perfect. It is likewise extracted through certain useless mineral things which are not of this kind: such as vitriol and copperas <note>iron or copper sulfates</note>, which is also called the gum of copper, or the drippings of the same. Let us therefore assert in the following sections

<column-break/>

all the modes of medicines which arise from quicksilver. Then indeed those from sulfur, or from a mixture of both. Finally, those from the gum of copper or similar things. The method for that which is made through quicksilver is as follows. Take the precipitate, killed <note>chemically altered/oxidized</note> and fixed by its own precipitation. Then let it be placed in a furnace of great heat, in the manner of preserving lime <note>calx</note> which we have taught, until it grows red in the likeness of cinnabar <note>original: "viifur," likely a corruption of "vinnabari" or "cinabrio"</note>. If indeed it does not grow red, take a portion of quicksilver that has not been killed, and repeat its sublimation with sulfur. However, let the sulfur be cleansed of all impurity, and likewise the quicksilver. And after you have repeated its sublimation twenty times over the precipitate itself, dissolve it with the sharpness of dissolving waters <note>acids</note>; and calcine it again, and dissolve it again, until it is abundantly sufficient. After this, dissolve a portion of silver, and when it has been dissolved, mix the solutions and coagulate them; and cast this upon the molten silver. For it will yellow it with a very profitable yellowness. If indeed the quicksilver grew red in the precipitation toward the projection of its perfection, the said administration is sufficient without the mixture of a thing to dye it. It reddens indeed through sulfur, but its reddening is difficult and immensely laborious. Silver is likewise yellowed with a solution of Mars <note>iron</note>. The necessity of the work leads us to this: that we first calcine and fix that which is a great labor. Then, indeed, let us administer it by this same preparation and the same projection upon Silver.
