# BOOK THREE
-> **XLIII** <-

We may pour in the substance. And yet, its shining yellowing <note>original: "citrinatio"; the process of turning a metal gold-colored</note> does not spring forth; rather, it appears dark and leaden, a deadly sort of yellow. However, the method of yellowing which is accomplished through vitriol <note>iron or copper sulfates</note> or copperas <note>a green sulfate of iron</note> is as follows. Let a certain quantity of each of these be taken, and let that part which can be sublimated <note>vaporized and solidified</note> be raised until it is totally expressed. After this, let that which was sublimated be raised again with a fire specifically suited to it, so that part after part is fixed <note>made resistant to fire</note> until the greater portion of it is stable. Afterward, let it be calcined <note>reduced to powder by heat</note> with careful attention to the fire, so that a greater heat can be applied for its perfection. After this, let it be dissolved in a most red water, to which there is no equal. Then, you must find a way to grant it entry into the lunar body <note>silver</note>. These methods have been sufficiently shown to you, provided you are a diligent seeker of the work we have described. And because we see these things adhere deeply and harmoniously to the Moon <note>silver</note>, we have considered it, and it is certain that these things share their root; therefore, it happens that they are altered by them. These are all the medicines of the first order that we have brought forward. Many more methods for them can be multiplied, provided the essence of the coloring materials remains the same despite the variety of methods. Indeed, quicksilver <note>mercury</note> is not a medicine in this first order, since it is not a medicine that alters it by a single quality, but rather in its total completion. Some have found more medicines, but one of two things must necessarily happen: either they must create medicine from the same things (or things having the same nature), or they compose a medicine that is equivalent to nothing in its alteration, contributing neither to the world nor to the parts of the world, until the Prime Mover rests incorruptible in the shifting heights of nature.

<column-break/>

### ON MEDICINES
#### of the second order.

#### Chapter 76.

It remains for us, therefore, to pass on to the medicines of the second order, with the true sufficiency required by this discourse, and with manifest proofs and true experience. Since there is one medicine for changing bodies <note>metals</note> and another for perfectly curdling quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, we shall first describe the medicines for the bodies completely. Then, we shall turn to the medicine for that same quicksilver, which curdles it into true gold-making or silver-making material. A medicine of the second order, therefore, is one which completes imperfect things by a single quality of perfection. However, since there are many causes of corruption in each of the imperfect bodies—namely, in Saturn <note>lead</note> there is a volatile sulfurousness and the flight of quicksilver, through which corruption necessarily occurs, as well as its earthiness—a medicine exists which either removes one of these entirely or hides it with a decorative coating, while leaving the other causes of imperfection behind. Because there is something unchangeable in bodies, which in the roots of bodies...

<summary>This page concludes the description of "first order" alchemical medicines, specifically those using vitriol to color silver, and begins explaining "second order" medicines which target specific impurities in metals like lead.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, citrination, vitriol, sublimation, quicksilver, silver, lead, sulfur, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the limitations of first-order medicines; this page continues the technical distinction between second and third-order alchemical preparations, focusing on the transformation of "imperfect" metals into gold or silver.</meta>

# BOOK

It is innate, as they say, that which cannot be removed by a medicine of the second order <note>The author categorizes alchemical agents by "orders" of potency; second-order medicines can change appearances and some properties, but only the third order achieves total transmutation.</note>: therefore, every medicine that removes that [innate quality] from the mixture is called a medicine not of the second order, but of the third and higher order. And because we have found that the superfluities of volatile substances are removed through the method of **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize or dry it.</note>, and through reduction the non-innate earthiness is removed: it was therefore necessary to find a medicine of the second order which might cloak the innate, soften the hard, and harden the soft—specifically in hard and soft bodies—according to a completion that is not sophisticated <note>In this context, "sophisticated" means "adulterated" or "false."</note> but **sun-making** <note>original: "solificum" — the creation of gold.</note> or **moon-making** <note>original: "lunificum" — the creation of silver.</note> of imperfect bodies, perfectly establishing a true moon-making.

Since it is evident, therefore, that in bodies which are soft, the haste of their melting cannot be removed by the ingenuity of these artistic works, nor can the impurity innate in the root of their principles be removed, it necessarily follows that a medicine must be reached: which indeed, upon being cast onto them, might thicken their thinness, and by thickening, harden them to the sufficiency of their melting and ignition; in hard bodies, it might thin the thickness, and by thinning, bring them to a sufficient speed of fusion with the property of ignition, and decorate the dross of both kinds of bodies by cloaking it: transforming the latter into white, and the former into the most perfect yellow <note>White refers to silver; yellow/citrine refers to gold.</note>. This medicine does not differ from the medicine of the third order, except through the imperfection of a lesser preparation. And it does not differ from itself in the casting upon various bodies and the acceptance of pigments: but in the manner of preparation. Similarly, the medicine for soft bodies, thickening their thinness, requires one industry of pre-

<column-break/>

paration: while another, thinning the thickness of hard bodies, requires another. The latter indeed requires the method of consuming fire: while the former requires the administration of the conservation of moisture.

Let us therefore assert a universal discourse on the medicines of this second order, complete with certain and true determination: and first, all the **moon-makings** <note>The processes for creating silver.</note> of all bodies diminished from perfection, with the differences of their preparation. Then indeed the **sun-makings** <note>The processes for creating gold.</note>, likewise with their own differences. We have already proved by our discourses that **sulfur** of any kind is corruptive of perfection. **Mercury** <note>original: "Argentum vivum" — literally "living silver."</note>, however, is perfecting in the works of nature through complete regimens. Therefore, we are by no means changing nature, but imitating it: in those things in which it is possible for us to follow its works. And similarly, we take mercury in the mastery of this work, namely in the medicine of each perfection, both the solar and that of the imperfect bodies and of the coagulable mercury itself. Since, however, from the discourses most recently spoken, we have mentioned a twofold difference of medicine: one indeed of bodies, the other indeed of truly coagulable mercury. Let us hand down a certain account of the medicine of bodies first, and thereafter of mercury. The matter of this medicine is therefore united by its own kind: and it is that which is already sufficiently known. Take it, therefore, and if you wish to proceed toward the lunar [work], according to the order promised to you, render yourself practiced, and prepare it with the methods of this mastery.

# BOOK THREE

the marks of the <term>magistery</term> <note>The "magistery" refers to the mastery of the alchemical process or the completed "Great Work" itself.</note>. The intention of these is that you divide the pure substance from it; one part you should fix <note>To "fix" a substance means to make it resistant to fire so it no longer evaporates.</note>, but the other part leave for <term>waxing</term> <note>Ceration: a process of softening a substance until it has a wax-like consistency, making it easier to melt or mix.</note>. Thus, follow the whole magistery until you complete its attempted fusion. If it melts suddenly, it is perfected in hard bodies; in soft ones, however, it is the opposite. For this <term>medicine</term> <note>In alchemy, the "medicine" or "elixir" is the substance used to transmute base metals into noble ones.</note>, when cast upon any of the imperfect bodies, changes them into a perfected lunar body <note>Silver</note>, provided that the known preparations precede this medicine. If not, it leaves the body diminished; it perfects it in another degree of perfection only as much as depends on the administration of this kind of second-order medicine. However, as much as depends on the third order, it perfects by projection alone without any prior administration.

The solar <note>Gold-making</note> medicine of this second order is of the same material as that of the imperfect bodies, sharing the same regime of administration. In this, however, it has a difference: namely, in a greater refinement of the parts through specific methods, and the addition of a mixture administered by a regime of subtle preparation of <term>sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur was viewed as the "father" of metals, representing the principle of combustion and color.</note> according to the known method. And the regime is namely the fixation of that most pure sulphur and its solution by method. For with this the medicine is tinged: and when cast upon any of the bodies diminished from perfection, it completes them in the solar fulfillment—as much as depends on the preparation and administration of this second-order medicine, provided the known and certain preparation of the diminished body precedes it. And cast upon moon-metal <note>Silver</note> also, it perfects it in

<column-break/>

a very particular solar fulfillment.

### ON THE MEDICINE
### for coagulating quicksilver.

### Chapter 77.

According to the order of the premises, it remains from the completion of the work (which we showed we would determine from the beginning) to pass the narrative into the medicine for coagulating <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury</note>. We say, therefore, that it is drawn from that same material, namely from that noted in the multiple chapters of this work. And this is because, since quicksilver is fugitive <note>Volatile; easily evaporated by heat.</note> and easily lost without any inflammation, it needs a medicine which suddenly, before its flight, adheres to it in its depths, joins with it in its smallest parts, thickens it, and by its own fixation preserves it in the fire until there comes to it a tolerance for a greater fire to consume its humidity. Through this benefit, it converts it in a moment into true <term>solific</term> <note>Gold-making</note> or <term>lunific</term> <note>Silver-making</note> substance, according to that for which the medicine was prepared. Since, therefore, we have found nothing more suitable to it than that which is of its own nature, we have therefore judged to complete its medicine with it: and we have devised how to provide the form of the medicine to it through ingenuity. And it is this: that it be prepared with its methods already mentioned, with the persistence of long labor; by which,

<summary>This page describes the "second order" of alchemical medicines used to transform base metals into silver or gold and introduces the specific method for "fixing" volatile mercury into a solid, noble state.</summary>

Its entire substance is said to be perfectly pure and white in the Moon <note>original: "Luna"; referring to Silver</note>, but an intense yellow in the Sun <note>original: "Sole"; referring to Gold</note>. And indeed, this is not completed so that it creates a yellow color without the mixture of a tinting agent which is of its own nature. From that point, let it be perfected with this purest substance of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti viui"; Mercury</note>, using the ingenious methods of this masterful work, a medicine <note>The "medicine" refers to the alchemical elixir or catalyst</note> which adheres most strongly to quicksilver: let it be melted very easily, and let it coagulate that mercury. For it will convert this into a true sun-maker <note>original: "solificum"; gold-maker</note> and moon-maker <note>original: "lunificum"; silver-maker</note>, provided its preparation has been performed beforehand. But it is often asked from which sources this substance of quicksilver can best be drawn. And we, responding, declare that it is extracted from those things in which it already exists. It is found both in the bodies <note>The seven base metals</note> and in quicksilver itself according to nature, since they are found to be of one nature. But it is more difficult to extract from the bodies: yet in quicksilver it is more perfect. Therefore, of whatever kind the medicine may be—whether in the bodies or in the substance of quicksilver itself—the medicine of the precious stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> seeks it out. But since it sometimes happens that the medicine mixes well, and sometimes not: therefore we shall describe the method of mixing, namely how each thing may acquire a very deep entry into a body, or why a medicine might not enter. And the method is through the dissolution of that which enters, and through the dissolution of that which does not enter, and through the mixing of both solutions. For it makes everything of any kind "ingressive" <note>capable of penetrating or soaking into a metal</note> when it is joined to it at the smallest level. This is completed through solution: and

<column-break/>

fusion is completed through solution in things that are not naturally meltable: and for this reason, they are more fit to enter and alter the metal. And this is the reason why we calcine <note>term: calcination; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder</note> certain things which are not of this nature, namely so that they may be better dissolved: and they are dissolved for this purpose, so that the bodies may better receive an impression from them, and similarly be prepared and cleansed by them through these means. Alternatively, we grant entry to those things which do not allow entry due to their own thickness, by the multiple repetition of sublimation <note>term: sublimation; heating a substance so it turns directly from solid to vapor and back to solid, used for purification</note> of non-flammable spirits over them—namely of arsenic and non-fixed quicksilver: or with the multiple repetition of the solution of that which has no entry. However, it is a good precaution for giving entry to unmixable things, that the body which is intended to be changed and altered by these means should be dissolved: and the things whose intention is to enter with alteration should also be dissolved. Yet let there not be a solution of all parts, but only of some: then let that body, and no other, be soaked <note>original: "imbibatur"; the process of imbibition or feeding a solid with liquid</note> time after time. For by this benefit, it necessarily has entry into that alone: but this does not necessarily happen in any other body. From these ingenious methods, therefore, it is necessary for any thing to have entry—which depends on the benefit of its nature—and to alter it with the discovered mixture. By this discourse, therefore, the number of the ten medicines is completed, with the sufficiency of their tradition. It remains, therefore, for us to pass on to the medicine of the third order.

<summary>This text describes the preparation of alchemical "medicines" (elixirs) capable of transmuting metals into gold or silver by ensuring the substance can deeply penetrate or "enter" the metal through dissolution and sublimation. It concludes the discussion on the ten medicines of the second order before moving to the third.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, quicksilver, mercury, transmutation, gold-making, silver-making, calcination, sublimation, dissolution, elixir, medicine of the stone</keywords>

### ON MEDICINE
### of the third order.

### Chapter 78.

The medicine of this order is twofold, namely solar <note>gold-making</note> and lunar <note>silver-making</note>. Yet it is one in essence, and the method of acting is similar; therefore it is called a single medicine by our ancestors, whose writings we have read thoroughly. There is, however, an addition of a lemon-yellow color, which is perfected by the purest substance of fixed sulfur. The difference between this and that—namely the lunar and the solar—is that this one contains that substance within itself, while the other does not. This third order is called the "order of the greater work" <note>Latin: *maioris operis*, referring to the Great Work or Magnum Opus</note>; and this is because it requires the industry of greater wisdom in its administration, and in the preparation of its perfection, and a longer labor for the completion of the truth. Therefore, the medicine of this order does not differ from the second order in essence in any way, except through the most subtle degrees of preparation in its creation, and through a more lasting persistence of labor. We shall recount all these steps at the conclusion of our discourse, along with the complete method of preparation with its causes and manifest experiments, and the many degrees of the methods of administration for this third order. For the solar medicine requires one degree in the complete preparation of the pigments, while the lunar requires another. This one indeed requires the administration of sulfur to color it; the other truly does not.

<column-break/>

First, therefore, let us recount the method of administration for the lunar medicine. It is this: that you take its known stone <note>the *Lapis*, or Philosopher's Stone in its initial form</note>; and by the method of separation, divide its purest part and set it aside. Then, fix <term>Fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</term> a portion of that which is the purest part, and leave a portion of it unfixed. When it has been fixed, dissolve that which was soluble from it; but that which is not soluble, send to calcination <term>Calcination: reducing a solid to powder through high heat</term>, and from there repeat the solution upon the same until, again, whatever is soluble from it is entirely dissolved. Thus, let this order be observed until the greater quantity is dissolved. After this, mix all the solutions together and coagulate them. Then, by roasting lightly, keep it in a tempered fire until a greater heat can be administered to it according to its requirements. After this, observe the first order of solution until everything that is soluble from it is dissolved again, and coagulate it again, and keep it again in a tempered fire until, once more, a greater fire can be administered for its perfection. Repeat all these orders of preparation upon it four times; and finally, calcine it in its proper manner; and thus you have sufficiently governed the most precious earth of the stone by administration. Then, join the quantity of the reserved non-fixed part with this part of the administered earth by a subtle method of ingenuity, down to the smallest particles. And thus this ingenuity of the intention of its lifting is called the method of sublimation <term>Sublimation: heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</term>, until the fixed is lifted entirely with the non-fixed. But if this does not occur, let a quantity of the non-fixed part be added to it again in turn, until it is raised...

<summary>This page defines the "Third Order" of alchemical medicine, distinguishing between the solar (gold) and lunar (silver) paths, and begins detailing the rigorous purification process for the lunar medicine involving repeated dissolution, calcination, and sublimation.</summary>
<keywords>Third Order, solar medicine, lunar medicine, sulfur, calcination, coagulation, sublimation, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy</keywords>

->BOOK<-

of its purification. Therefore, when it has been elevated <note>original: "elevata"; referring to the process of sublimation where a substance is heated until it rises as vapor and solidifies again</note>, repeat its sublimation until the whole is fixed through this repetition of the process. When it has been fixed, soak it again with a quantity of the non-fixed part, bit by bit, through the technique known to you, until the whole is elevated once more. Then let it be fixed again until it provides an easy fusion <note>meaning it melts easily like wax</note> when ignited. For this is the medicine which transforms every body diminished from perfection, and every kind of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, into a most perfect lunar body <note>pure silver</note>.

### Chapter 79.

For the preparation of this solar medicine <note>the medicine for making gold</note>, an addition is made of non-burning sulfur, by means of fixing and calcining <note>reducing to powder through heat</note>, with the skill of industrious administration and a manifold method of solution, with much repetition until it becomes pure. These things are preceded by a perfect administration which is completed through sublimation. And the method of this addition is through the repetition of the sublimation of the non-fixed part of the stone, with the skill of joining them until it rises with it, and is again fixed with it so that it remains stable. And the more the order of this overflowing completion is repeated, the more the abundance of this medicine is multiplied, its quality is increased, and the growth of its perfection is greatly multiplied. 

And indeed, lest we be bitten by the wicked, we describe the entire completion of this mastery <note>term: magisterium; the alchemical process of creating the Stone</note> under a brief, complete, and well-known discourse. Its intention is that the stone and its addition be purified most perfectly through the method of sublimation; and from there, by skillful means, the volatile part of them may be fixed. Then, let the fixed become volatile, and the volatile again become fixed; and in this order is completed the most precious secret, which is above every secret of the sciences of this world, and is an incomparable treasure. 

And you indeed should exercise yourself toward it with the greatest persistence of labor and with the duration of immense meditation. For with these you shall find it, and without them, you shall not. And for this medicine, the repetition of the goodness of the administration, with the skill of such caution, can occur in the preparation of the stone until it changes quicksilver into infinite, true solar <note>gold</note> and lunar <note>silver</note> substance; and it depends on nothing except its multiplication. 

Now, therefore, let the sublime God of natures be praised, blessed and glorious, who revealed to us the sequence of all medicines with the experience of it; which we sought out by the goodness of His instigation and the persistence of our labor, and we have seen the kiss and touched with our hand its completion, investigated by our mastery. But if indeed we have veiled this, let the son of doctrine <note>a dedicated student of alchemy</note> not wonder. For we have not veiled it from him, but we have delivered it in such speech to the evil and the wicked, which must necessarily remain hidden from the foolish; and by the same speech, the prudent are drawn to the search for its discovery.

<column-break/>

Therefore, sons of learning, seek out this most excellent gift of God, reserved for you alone. But you, foolish sons of wickedness and of immense, malevolent depravity, flee from this science: for it is hostile and adverse to you, and it will cast you into the misery of poverty; because by divine providence and judgment, this gift of God is utterly hidden from you and denied to you entirely. Having therefore examined the methods of all medicines, and following the beginning of our proposal, we must now move on to those things which signify the perfection of this mastery <note>Latin: magisterium; the completed alchemical process or "Great Work"</note>, along with the reasons for their proofs.

### ON THE TESTS for the perfection of the work.

#### Chapter 80.

Leaving aside the obvious tests which we have not mentioned—since they are known and certain to everyone without the need for cleverness, such as weight, color, and malleability under the hammer—let us cautiously attempt the technical tests to see if the projection <note>The act of transforming base metal into gold or silver</note> of this art’s administration brings about a completion with truth. These tests are: the cupel <note>Latin: cineritium; a test using a shallow porous bowl made of bone ash to purify precious metals</note>, cementing <note>A process of heating metals with powdered materials to purify them</note>, ignition, fusion, exposure to sharp vapors, testing by the mixture of burning sulfur, quenching, the repetition of calcination <note>Reducing a metal to powder by heat</note> and reduction, and the easy or difficult reception of quicksilver <note>Latin: argentum vivum; mercury</note>. We must begin with the first in order, and then proceed perfectly to the others according to our promise, along with their known causes.

### ON THE CUPEL.

#### Chapter 81.

We shall now speak regarding the cupel, with all its most evident causes and the method of its flight <note>Referring to the evaporation or "flying off" of impurities</note>. It is only the substance of the Sun <note>Gold</note> and the Moon <note>Silver</note> that endures in the examination of the cupel. Seeking, therefore, the true differences in the substance of these perfect bodies, and likewise the reasons for the cupel—why some metals endure more and others less in the examination of this mastery when they are diminished from perfection—we shall investigate. However, the secret of these two bodies has been sufficiently narrated by us in the depths of their substance. And it is this: because their primary root was a great quantity of quicksilver, and its essence was most pure and most subtle at first, but afterwards thickened until it could undergo fusion with ignition. Therefore, whichever metals are diminished from perfection and have more earthiness, they endure less in this examination; but whichever have less, endure more. For these [perfect metals] adhere more closely because of the subtlety of their parts, which mix and unite themselves to the highest degree. And those bodies which are likewise of greater thinness, or on the contrary those which—

He explains that only gold and silver survive the cupel because of their high mercury content and purity of substance.</summary>

# BOOK

->**On the Separation of Metals**<-

Bodies of greater density must necessarily be separated entirely from a mixture more so than those that exist in perfection <note>The "perfect" metals are Gold and Silver; "imperfect" are base metals like Lead and Iron.</note>, because they are not of the same melting point, and therefore they are separated. And those which participate in a smaller quantity of **mercury** <note>original: "argenti vivi"; the author follows the "sulfur-mercury" theory where metals are composed of these two principles.</note> are more easily separated from the mixture. It is clear, therefore, that since **Saturn** <note>term: Saturn (Lead)</note> possesses much earthiness, a small quantity of mercury, and a thinness of liquefaction <note>i.e., a low melting point</note>, it is most opposed to the test of perfection by the **ash-cupel** <note>term: cineritium; a vessel made of bone ash used to refine gold and silver</note>. Therefore, among other bodies, it lasts the least in the mixture during the craft of the cupel; rather, it separates and yields most quickly. And because it yields more than the other diminished bodies, this test of the mastery is more appropriate to it; and this is because it yields faster and draws each of the imperfect things out of the mixture with itself. Because of this, a greater quantity of the perfect metal is saved from the strong burning of the fire-test, since the perfect metal does not remain for a long time in the consumption of the test; thus, through the test of lead, it is burned less and purified more easily.

But because the substance of **Jupiter** <note>term: Jupiter (Tin)</note> was capable of holding more mercury and a smaller quantity of earthiness—or rather participating in greater purity and a more subtle substance—it is saved in the mixture longer than **Saturn** or **Venus** <note>term: Venus (Copper)</note>, because it adheres more deeply to the mixture. This is the reason why a great quantity of the perfect metal is destroyed before the joined metal is separated from it. **Venus**, however, offers melting with ignition <note>glowing heat</note>. But because its melting is slower than that of the perfect metal, it is therefore

<column-break/>

separated from the mixture, yet more slowly than **Saturn** because of the ignition of its fusible substance. Because it has a smaller quantity of mercury than **Jupiter**, and greater earthiness and a denser substance, it is more easily removed from the mixture than **Jupiter**, since **Jupiter** adheres more deeply than **Venus**. **Mars** <note>term: Mars (Iron)</note>, however, does not possess fluidity, and therefore is not mixed; this happens because of the deprivation of its moisture. But even if it should happen to be mixed because of the vehemence of the fire, since it lacks moisture, it unites with the **Moon** <note>term: Luna (Silver)</note> or **Sun** <note>term: Sol (Gold)</note> by drinking up their moisture, joining with them in the smallest parts. And so, although it has much earthiness, a scarcity of mercury, and a lack of fluidity, it is not separated from them by any easy craft.

By this, therefore, the industry of the craftsman is expanded toward the true rectification of any body, if he rightly knows the efficacy of what we have written. If, however, he applies his intellect to it in a fantastic <note>imaginary or delusional</note> way, he will know nothing of the truth of it. There are, however, two bodies of perfection in the endurance of this test, namely the **Sun** and the **Moon**, on account of their good composition—which results from a good mixture—and their pure substance. Let us therefore describe the method of it: take sifted ash, or lime, or the powder of burnt animal bones, or a mixture of all or some of these. Then, when it is moistened with water, let the hand be pressed upon it so that a firm and solid layer is made; and in the middle of the layer let a round and solid pit be made; and upon the bottom of it let some quantity of crushed glass be sprinkled.

# BOOK THREE

Then, indeed, let them be allowed to dry out: and when it is dry, let the object intended to undergo this <term>testing</term><note>original: "examen"; here referring to the process of cupellation or assaying</note> be placed into the said pit. Over it, let coals of a strong fire be kindled, and let the fire be blown upon the surface of the body to be tested until it melts. Once melted, let us cast in <term>Lead</term><note>original: "Saturni"; literally "of Saturn," the alchemical name for lead</note> piece by piece, and let it be blown upon with a flame of strong ignition. And while you see it agitated and moving with a strong shaking motion, it is not pure. Wait, therefore, until all the lead vanishes. If it has vanished and that motion does not cease, it is not yet purified. Therefore, cast lead upon it again, and blow upon its surface again until the lead is separated. If it does not become still, repeat the casting of lead and the blowing upon its surface until it rests, and you see it clean and bright on its surface. After this, open the coals and scatter the fire, and pour water upon its surface: for you will find this perfectly tested. And if at any time during the blowing of this test you cast in glass, it will be purified better and more perfectly, because it carries away the filth and entangles it. However, in place of glass, salt may be cast in, or <term>borax</term>, or some kind of alum. Similarly, this <term>ash-test</term><note>original: "cineritij"; referring to the cupel made of bone ash</note> can be performed in an earthen crucible, blowing around it and upon its surface likewise, so that the material to be tested is melted all around. These things having been sufficiently described, let us pass to the test of <term>cement</term><note>original: "cemento"; a dry refining process using salts and acids to purify gold</note> with its causes and its manifest and known experiments.

### ON CEMENT.

### Chapter 82.

We have said, therefore, that some bodies are burned more and some less by the method of fire's <term>calcination</term><note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize it</note>: those containing a greater quantity of combustible <term>sulfur</term> are burned more; those with less, less. Because <term>Gold</term><note>original: "Sol"; literally "the Sun," the alchemical name for gold</note> is of a smaller quantity of sulfur among the other bodies, it is therefore burned [the least] among the others by any inflammation of fire. <term>Silver</term><note>original: "Luna"; literally "the Moon," the alchemical name for silver</note>, indeed, after Gold, participates in a smaller quantity of sulfur than all remaining bodies, but more than Gold. Therefore, according to this, it can tolerate the ignition of inflammation for a shorter space of time than Gold and things of a similar burning nature. <term>Copper</term><note>original: "Venus"</note> [tolerates] less: because it has more sulfur and a greater <term>earthiness</term><note>original: "terreitatis"; referring to the non-metallic or dross-like impurities</note> than Gold and Silver; therefore it tolerates the inflammation of fire less. <term>Tin</term><note>original: "Iupiter"</note>, however, participates in more sulfurousness and earthiness than Copper, but less than Gold and Silver <note>The author likely means it is less stable than Copper, but the Latin phrasing here compares its impurities to the noble metals</note>: and therefore it is burned by inflammation more than Copper, and much more than Gold and Silver. <term>Lead</term><note>original: "Saturnius"</note> truly has preserved more earthiness and sulfurousness by nature in its mixture than the bodies just mentioned: and therefore it is inflamed more quickly and easily than all the said bodies, and through inflam—

->**On the Nature of Combustion and Cementation**<-

...mation <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "transformation" or "inflammation"</note> it is burned more quickly because it has a very close connection to sulfurousness, and is more fixed than **Jupiter** <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note>. **Mars** <note>The alchemical name for Iron</note>, however, is not burned by its own nature but by accident. For when it is mixed with bodies of great moisture, it drinks that moisture up due to its own lack of humidity; therefore, when joined, it is neither inflamed nor burned, provided the bodies united to it are not themselves flammable or combustible. If, however, combustible bodies are mixed with it, **Mars** is necessarily burned and inflamed according to the nature of their combustion. 

Since, therefore, **cement** <term>Cementum: a dry mixture of corrosive minerals used to refine gold by eating away base metals</term> is composed of flammable things, the reason for its necessary invention is clear: it was so that all combustible things might be scorched away. Since there is only one body that is incombustible <note>Referring to Gold, or "Sol"</note>, only that body—or one prepared according to its nature—is released from the cement. Yet some things endure longer in the cement, and some less. Which endure more and which less is known by the reasons already stated. Thus, **Luna** <note>Silver</note> endures the most, **Mars** <note>Iron</note> less, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> even less, **Venus** <note>Copper</note> still less, and **Saturn** <note>Lead</note> the least of all. 

Let us therefore describe the method of the cement. Since it is most necessary for us to know this for the examination of perfection, we say that its composition is made with flammable things. These are all things of a blackening, fleeing, penetrating, and burning nature: such as **vitriol** <term>Sulfate minerals</term>, **sal ammoniac** <term>Ammonium chloride</term>, **flower of copper** <term>Verdigris or copper oxide</term>, crushed old pottery shards, and a certain quantity of sulfur, or perhaps none at all,

<column-break/>

and male urine, and similar sharp and penetrating substances. All these are mixed into a paste with the male urine and spread over thin plates of the metal whose purity is to be tested by this judgment. Then, the plates should be laid out upon an iron grate inside a closed earthenware vessel; however, they must be placed so that one does not touch another, so that the power of the fire may run through them equally and freely. Thus, they should be kept in the earthenware vessel in a strong fire for three days. 

Care must be taken, however, that the plates are heated to redness but not melted. After the third day, you will find the plates clean of all impurity if the body was of a perfect nature. If it was not, they will be entirely corrupted and consumed by **calcination** <term>The process of reducing a metal to a powder or ash through heat</term>. Some people, however, place the plates in the fire without the cement composition, and they are purified similarly if they are bodies of perfection. If not, they are entirely burned. However, they require a longer space of time for combustion in this latter examination, which is performed by the flame of fire alone, than in the one examined by the judgment of the cement. 

But since **Luna** <note>Silver</note> does not differ much from the nature of **Sol** <note>Gold</note>, it remains stable in the judgment with only a small amount of management. Nor does a separation of the bodies from one another occur in these two types of examination, except because of the diversity of the composition of their substances; from this results a difference in melting, as well as thickness and thinness, which are the causes of separation. Because of their strong composition, their substance is not corrupted by the substance of a foreign body; since...

...so that a mixture of them does not occur through their smallest parts. And therefore it is necessary for that mixture to be separated from one another, without the total corruption of their essence. For this reason, the complete treatment of imperfect bodies <note>The "imperfect bodies" usually refer to base metals like lead, tin, copper, or iron, as opposed to the "perfect" gold and silver.</note> is recognized as finished when, through the administration of fusion, ignition, and solidification, they are found to be transformed by ingenious skill.

### ON IGNITION.

#### Chapter 82.

It remains, therefore, for us to deliver the chapter on ignition. We say, then, that because bodies of the greatest perfection have been found to receive fire with a determined ignition before their fusion. And therefore we say, if we endeavor to find their complete alteration, it is necessary to bring the treated bodies to their fusion. And it is this: that before the bodies of perfection are melted, they should receive ignition with a flame and a pleasantness of celestial color <note>original: "caelestini coloris"; likely referring to a sky-blue or bright ethereal flame</note>: before their ignition reaches the whiteness of fire, which the eye can by no means look upon. It is clear, therefore, that their perfect ignition is completed before fusion, with an intense redness, and not with a whiteness that the eye cannot behold. For if the treated bodies are melted before they are ignited, they are not in a state of completion. If, however, they are ignited only with great labor and a strong expression of fire, their treatment is not

<column-break/>

true. And this indeed occurs in soft metals; the same may be gathered regarding Mars <note>term: Mars (the alchemical name for Iron)</note> alone. For those things that are not easily ignitable do not receive the ignition of preparation; nor do those not easily fusible receive the right fusion, which we find in those things perfected according to nature. And if the treated material does not extend a flame with the pleasantness of a celestial color along with the ignition, their treatment is not complete. And if anything is diminished—that is, of weight, color, beauty, ignition, and similar qualities found through the cunning of the various types of good preparation—the investigation of the craftsman was not sufficient; therefore, let him search again, until he finds it through the methods granted by Divine Goodness.

### ON FUSION.

#### Chapter 84.

In the narration of fusion, therefore, let us provide sufficiency, according to which there is a test of all bodies for their certain ignition. We say, then, that there is a single fusion of perfection with ignition: but not with just any kind of ignition, but with an ignition in which the body does not turn entirely white: and with an ignition in which no darkness comes upon the fire: and in which the body is not suddenly melted and liquefied so that it flows immediately after ignition. Therefore, when a body has melted itself from a very slight pressure of weak fire, either without ignition or with a dark ignition: the result of this preparation

It is necessary that a body of imperfection exists whenever any of the imperfect bodies are found in a diminished state of craftsmanship. And if, after melting, it is left to cool, and its glowing heat is turned entirely and suddenly into blackness—and because of this it loses its glow before it hardens—that body is not in a state of perfection, regardless of what kind it may have been. Rather, it is useful to judge that this body belongs to the classes of imperfect bodies characterized by softness. And if, indeed, through the laborious and strong expression of a violent fire, its glowing heat before melting becomes entirely white with a ray of inestimable brightness: then the body has been altered, not toward perfection, but toward hardness. And for this reason, if it is removed from the fire after its melting and suddenly hardens so that it does not flow, while its bright glow remains: then it is not a body of Lunar <note>original: "lunaris"; referring to Silver</note> or Solar <note>original: "solaris"; referring to Gold</note> perfection, regardless of the kind or preparation of the body administered: but it should be placed under the nature of the varieties of Mars <note>original: "Martis"; referring to Iron</note>. It is clear, therefore, from what has been said, that a threefold glowing or liquefaction of these fusible substances is gathered by experiment: namely, one that is dark; another that is red and clear; and a third that is brightest white, shining with a ray. The first is proven by the test of reason to belong to soft bodies; the second to perfect bodies; and the third to hard bodies. But he who desires to seek out the degree of all these glows should smelt all fusible bodies and consider the sufficiency of the fire for the complete perfection of the melting: and by considering, he should gather the difference in the degrees of all the signs

<column-break/>

of melting, and thus indeed he shall find it: but otherwise, he will not. And let this be brought to you as a model in all the manners of examination determined and to be determined by us. And so, let these things said concerning melting suffice.

---

### ON THE EXPOSURE to the vapors of sharp substances.

#### Chapter 85.

Following therefore the completion of our discourse, let us speak of the exposure of bodies to the vapors of sharp substances <term>vapores acutorum: acidic or corrosive fumes used to test or color metals</term>. Let us say, then, that we see the bodies of perfection exposed to the vapor of sharp things—namely, those that are harsh, pungent, and vinegary—either do not bloom at all, or they emit a most beautiful celestial <note>sky-blue</note> flower. But we find that the purest Sun <note>Gold</note> does not bloom: yet the Moon <note>Silver</note>, or a Sun that is not pure, when exposed to the vapors of sharp things, does bloom: and it produces a most beautiful celestial color, more beautifully in the Sun than in the Moon. And for this reason, imitating nature, we likewise discern a celestial color in prepared bodies: which is perfected through the goodness of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti vivi"; Mercury</note>: as has been sufficiently narrated by us in the preceding parts of our discourse. Whichever prepared bodies, therefore, are placed over the vapors of sharp things and do not create a celestial color of beauty, they are not in the completion of their preparation.

<summary>This page describes how to identify metals by the color of their glow when heated and their reaction to acidic vapors. It distinguishes between "perfect" metals (Gold and Silver) and "imperfect" or "hard" metals (like Iron) based on these visual tests.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, alchemy, gold, silver, iron, mercury, smelting, chemical vapors, color theory, planetary metals</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical testing (examination) of metals, moving from surface appearances to the behavior of metals when quenched or treated with chemicals.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

total. Therefore, there is a certain type among the bodies which, in the examination of pungent substances <note>original: "ponticorum"; likely referring to acidic or astringent mineral salts</note>, blooms on its surface with a reddish-brown or lemon-brown color mixed with green: of this kind is **Mars** <note>Iron</note>. Another kind blooms green-brown on the surface, mixed with a cloudy sky-blue: and of this kind is **Venus** <note>Copper</note>. Another is a dull white: and of this kind **Saturn** <note>Lead</note> is covered. Another is a bright white, and of this kind is **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>. Therefore, because the most perfect body <note>Gold</note> blooms the least, or not at all—or if it does, only after a very long space of time—and because Jupiter blooms the least and most slowly among those bodies diminished from the fullness of perfection due to its gumminess <note>viscosity or oily impurities</note>, we consider by this test of the mastery <note>the alchemical process</note> that Jupiter approaches most closely to perfection in the work of the higher order. Through this examination, therefore, it can be investigated in which tempered state or genus a body consists, if you correctly consider the order of those things we have described in this chapter. If you do otherwise, attribute it to your own reckless foolishness.

### ON QUENCHING.

#### Chapter 86.

Let us now bring forward the full account regarding the examination by quenching <note>the cooling of hot metal in liquid</note>. There are multiple ways to experience this, through which one knows whether the mastery consists in perfection. First, if a glowing

<column-break/>

body is quenched in liquid, and the silver-like <note>original: "lunare"</note> does not become white, and the gold-like <note>original: "solare"</note> does not become a shining lemon-yellow, but is changed into an alien color, the alteration of the mastery is not at completion. For example, if during the repetition of its heating and quenching in waters created with the administration of salts or alums of any kind, a dross <note>scoria</note> akin to blackness is stretched out upon its surface; or if, by quenching it in sulfurs and by many repetitions of heating and cooling, it vanishes; or if it infects itself with a foul blackness; or if it breaks apart entirely under the stroke of the hammer, the artifice of the work is deceptive. Or if, by a cementing mixture of salt ammoniac, verdigris <note>green of copper</note>, and the urine of a boy, or things of a similar nature, it is placed in the fire; and if after heating and quenching it loses its proper silver or gold color entirely during hammering, or creates dross, it is certain the body still remains in a sophisticated <note>adulterated or "fake"</note> corruption. However, we hand down to you one certain general rule: if in any of the examinations mentioned or to be mentioned by us, the body changes any of its characteristics of perfection—namely weight or color—the craftsman has investigated the work not rightly, but fantastically <note>imaginary or false</note>; such a work is not profitable, but rather leads to loss.

### ON THE MIXTURE OF ADDED SULFUR.

#### Chapter 87.

<summary>This page describes how to identify metals by the colors they produce when exposed to corrosive substances and details the "quenching" test to verify if a metal has been truly transformed into gold or silver. The author warns that if the metal becomes brittle, blackens, or loses weight during these trials, the alchemical transformation is a "sophisticated" or false imitation.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, metallurgy, Mars (iron), Venus (copper), Saturn (lead), Jupiter (tin), quenching, salt ammoniac, verdigris, dross, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical testing, specifically focusing on how different metals react to sulfur and fire to determine their purity and "perfection."</meta>

# BOOK

Whether the mastery <note>Latin: *magisterium*; the successful completion of the alchemical process</note> consists in the mixture of sulfur is similarly proven. For we have found through our experience that sulfur mixed with bodies <note>metals</note> burns some more and others less; and we have found by our art that some return from that burning, while others do not. And from this, therefore, a difference can be noted between those bodies diminished from perfection, prepared in a sophisticated completion <note>referring to "sophisticated" or imitation metals made to look like gold or silver</note>. Therefore, since among the other bodies of every kind we find that the Sun <note>Gold</note> is burned the least by sulfur. After this and next is Jupiter <note>Tin</note>. Then indeed the Moon <note>Silver</note>; after this Saturn <note>Lead</note>; and more easily than all these is Venus <note>Copper</note>; and most easily of all is Mars <note>Iron</note> burned by the oiliness of sulfur. Therefore, note by this which metal approaches more or less to the nature of the perfect. And from the diversity of colors after the burning of the bodies, it can be investigated in which altered genus a body consists from the root of its nature: since the Sun indeed produces an intense lemon-yellow or a clear red; the Moon a black mixed with sky-blue; Jupiter a black mixed with a slight tint of redness; Saturn a dark black mixed with much redness and lividity; Venus a black mixed with lividity when much burning of sulfur is present; but when little burning is present, it extends a most shining and pleasant violet color from the mixture of sulfur. Mars, however, in every kind of burning creates a very dark blackish-brown color. From

<column-break/>

the reduction <note>the process of restoring a burnt metal back to its metallic state</note> from the burning of sulfur, a diversity in the bodies is similarly noted. For some return, while some recede <note>vanish or are destroyed</note> by the expression of fire during reduction with sulfur, either totally or in their greater quantity; some return from burning to a body in the nature of their own bodies, while others return into something other than their own nature. The bodies of the Sun <note>Gold</note> and the Moon <note>Silver</note> return to their proper nature from the burning of sulfur; however, Jupiter <note>Tin</note> and Saturn <note>Lead</note> recede. Jupiter recedes either totally or according to its greater part. Saturn does not recede entirely, but sometimes a larger and sometimes a smaller part of it is destroyed. The diversity of these occurs because of the nature of the bodies and the difference in their administration in the work of preparation: namely, because Jupiter happens to be destroyed by the sudden expression of fire in reduction. But by a successive and gradual reduction, both Saturn and Jupiter are saved. Yet their reduction tends toward the nature of a foreign body rather than their own. It has been found by our experience that the reduction of Jupiter is converted into a bright antimony <note>likely referring to a brittle, metallic-looking substance</note>, while that of Saturn into a dark one. Venus <note>Copper</note> is diminished in the impression of the fire of reduction, and Mars <note>Iron</note> even more so. But the reduction of Venus is heavy, and a dark lemon-yellow, and soft, participating in blackness, with an increase in the weight of its body. From these things, therefore, the nature of all altered bodies can be investigated.

### ON CALCINATION
### and reduction.
### Chapter 88.

We must now investigate the examination of repeated <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or "calx."</note> and <term>reduction</term> <note>The process of restoring a metallic powder back to its solid metallic state.</note>. We suggest, therefore, that the bodies found to be perfect—due to their inherent qualities—lose nothing of their color, weight, or quantity (of which one should be very careful), nor do they lose their luster, no matter how many times these operations are repeated upon them. Therefore, if through the repetition of these methods of calcination and reduction from a powder, any kind of altered metal loses any of its quality, you should consider that the craftsman has pursued his search through <term>sophistry</term> <note>In this context, a deceptive or false alchemical method.</note>. For this reason, you should practice these methods so that you may recognize them.

### ON THE EASY RECEPTION
### of quicksilver.
### Chapter 89.

It has now been shown to you truthfully that the perfect bodies consist of a very great quantity of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury (Latin: argentum vivum).</note>; and for that reason, they are most friendly toward quicksilver. Therefore, it must be thought that those bodies which most amicably drink up quicksilver are closer to perfection. A sign of this is the easy reception of quicksilver by a <term>solar</term> <note>Gold.</note> or <term>lunar</term> <note>Silver.</note> body of perfection. For this reason, if an altered body does not easily receive quicksilver into its substance, it must necessarily be very far from the fulfillment of perfection.

<column-break/>

### ON THE FULFILLMENT
### of the whole work.
### Chapter 90.

Since we have treated the experiences of our findings regarding the sufficiency of the causes of this <term>mastery</term> <note>The "Magisterium" or the Great Work of alchemy.</note>, according to the requirements of our proposed discourse, it remains for us to reach the fulfillment of the entire divine work in one chapter, and to draw together into a summary the mastery that has been scattered throughout the shortened chapters. We say, therefore, that the sum of the intention of the whole work is nothing other than that the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> (known from the previous chapters) be taken; then, with diligent labor, the work of the first degree of <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it.</note> should be applied to it. Through this, it is cleansed of corrupting impurity—this is the perfection of sublimation—and by it, the Stone is refined until it reaches the ultimate purity of subtlety and finally becomes volatile. After this, it should be fixed by the methods of <term>fixation</term> <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note>, until it rests in the harshness of the fire.

<summary>This page describes how to test the purity of metals through repeated heating and cooling, and begins to summarize the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone through sublimation and fixation.</summary>
<keywords>calcination, reduction, mercury, gold, silver, sublimation, fixation, Philosopher's Stone, alchemical purity</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the final stages of the alchemical Great Work, specifically the multiplication of the medicine's power and the philosophical justification for the author's cryptic writing style.</meta>

# BOOK

The second stage of preparation stands as a goal. But in a similar way, the Stone is administered in a third stage, which consists of the final completion of the preparation. This is, namely, so that you may now make the fixed Stone volatile by means of sublimation, and the volatile fixed, and the fixed dissolved, and the dissolved volatile once again, and the volatile fixed once again: until it flows and alters in a certain solar <note>original: "solifico," referring to the production of gold</note> and lunar <note>original: "lunifico," referring to the production of silver</note> completion. Therefore, from the reiteration of this third stage of preparation, there results a multiplication of the goodness of the alteration in the medicine. 

From the diversity of the reiteration of the work upon the Stone in its stages, there results a diversity in the multiplication of the goodness of the alteration: so that from these medicines, some might transmute a body sevenfold, some tenfold, some a hundredfold, some a thousandfold, and some into an infinite solar and true lunar perfection. From this point, then, let it be tested at last whether the Mastery <note>The "Magisterium" or the completed alchemical process</note> consists in perfection.

### Chapter 91.

But lest we be bitten by the envious, we declare that we have not handed down our science in a continuous discourse; rather, we have scattered it in different chapters. And this is because, if it had been handed down continuously, the wicked as well as the upright would have usurped it unworthily. And we have likewise hidden it where we have spoken most openly;

<column-break/>

nevertheless, we have not spoken in riddles, but in a plain series of discourse we have addressed the artisan. We have recorded it in a manner of speech which can only be gathered by the mind of the Most High God—blessed, sublime, and glorious—and by our own mind, we who wrote it; or by one infused with the goodness of divine grace, who grants and withdraws it to whom He wills. 

Therefore, let the son of doctrine not despair: for if he seeks it, he shall find it; not by following doctrine alone, but by the investigation of his own natural movement. For he who seeks through the goodness of his own industry shall find the science. But he who seeks through the mere pursuit of books shall arrive most slowly at this most precious art. Because we have published for ourselves alone the art investigated by ourselves alone, and not for others; yet it is most true and entirely certain. 

We have, therefore, enticed only the prudent to the art, and through the talents granted to us, we have exposed the way of investigation to them. We have not written that it was found by any except ourselves; but we have written both the method of discovery and the ingenuity of those methods. Therefore, through these things which we have handed down, let the artisan of good mind exercise himself, and he will rejoice to have found for himself the gift of the Most High God. For the searching out of this sublime art, 
->let these words suffice.<-

->**THE END.**<-

### THE BOOK BEGINS
->of the three words.<-

#### Chapter 92.

This stone from which this work is made contains within itself all colors. For it is white, a most ruddy red, a most intense yellow, celestial blue, and a heavy green. In this stone are the four elements. For it is watery, airy, fiery, and earthy. In this stone, heat and dryness are hidden <note>original: "in occulto"</note>; coldness and moisture are manifest <note>original: "in manifesto"</note>. It is necessary, therefore, to hide the manifest and to make manifest the hidden. That which is hidden—namely, heat and dryness—is the oil, and this is the dry principle which tints <note>The "tincture" or "tinging" refers to the alchemical power to transform the color and nature of a metal</note>; for the dryness of alchemy tints, and nothing else. And that which is manifest, the cold and moist, is a corrupting smoke. It is necessary, therefore, for the cold and moist to receive the others: and thus, what was hidden and manifest becomes one substance. That coldness and moisture is a corrupting and watery smoke, of which it is said that watery and burning moisture corrupts the work and tints it with blackness. These infirmities must therefore be destroyed in the fire through the degrees of fire.

#### Chapter 93.

This is the book of the three words, the book of the precious stone: which is an airy and volatile body, cold and moist, watery and burning. Therefore, the Persian philosophers say that watery and burning coldness and moisture are not friendly to heat and dryness: because heat and dryness destroy the cold, moist, watery, and burning elements by divine power. And then this spirit is turned into a most noble body, and it does not flee from the fire, and it flows like oil: which is a living tincture, multiplying, consolidating, most beautiful, everlasting, penetrating, and preserving.

<column-break/>

#### Chapter 94.

The wondrous work of the three words is that which is read concerning three things; it is understood differently by some, yet all understand it clearly and openly. Nevertheless, in these three words, inquiry is made concerning the moist and the cold, in which heat and dryness are hidden. And we must know this so that we may manifest the hidden and hide the manifest. This hidden thing is of the nature of the Sun <note>The Sun represents Gold or the masculine principle in alchemy</note> and of fire: and it is the most precious oil of all hidden things, and a living tincture: and the permanent water <note>original: "aqua permanens," a term for the universal solvent or mercury of the philosophers</note> which always lives and remains: and the vinegar of the philosophers <note>original: "acetum philosophorum," a sharp, acidic spirit used for dissolution</note>, that is, the penetrating spirit. It is the secret of those things that tint, and it is a gatherer and a rectifier: which rectifies and illuminates all the dead, and makes them rise again, after its heat and dryness no longer flee from the fire.

<summary>This text describes the alchemical "Stone" as a paradox of hidden and manifest qualities, explaining how the practitioner must use fire to transform "corrupting smoke" into a "living tincture." It introduces the "Book of Three Words," a Persian-influenced theory on balancing the four primary qualities to achieve a permanent, non-volatile substance.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Four Elements, Tincture, Persian Philosophers, Hidden and Manifest, Permanent Water, Vinegar of the Philosophers, Transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse from Book I, focusing on the transformation of matter and the astrological influences on development.</meta>

# BOOK I.

### Chapter 95.

Therefore, this spirit is turned into body, and the body into spirit, and again the spirit into body; and then a friendship is made between the cold and the moist, and the hot and the dry <note>The four primary qualities of the elements in Aristotelian physics</note>: and this is done by the power of God, and by the tempering of the fire, and the moderation of the limits of the fire. And of this length, two and seven. For from three, two is understood. And these are precious words, hidden and open: given not to the wicked, nor the ungodly, nor the unfaithful, but to the faithful and the poor, from the beginning even unto the end.

### Chapter 96.

I say therefore, in Mercury are the works of the planets, and their imaginings in their places, just as in a fetus, it works in its parts and times. For in the fetus, in the first month when the seed is conceived by the womb, Saturn works, congealing and constricting the matter itself into one mass by its coldness and dryness. In the second month Jupiter works, by its heat and moisture digesting it into a certain fleshy mass: which is called an embryo <note>original: "embrio"</note>. In the third month Mars works, acting upon the matter, and by its heat and dryness it divides and separates the mass itself, and disposes the limbs. In the fourth month truly the Sun, as lord, sends in the spirit: and thus it

<column-break/>

begins to live. In the fifth month Mercury works: who makes the openings and breathing-holes. In the sixth month Venus disposes and arranges the eyebrows, and the eyes, and the male organs. In the seventh month the Moon, by its coldness and moisture, labors to expel the fetus. And if it were born then, it would be able to live: but if it is not born, then it is weakened, and again Saturn in the eighth month, by its coldness and dryness, begins to work: and again by constricting the fetus itself in the womb. And if it were born then, it would not be able to live. In the ninth month Jupiter works again by its heat and moisture, and by relaxing it brings back the fetus itself, and otherwise restores the strengths to the fetus itself: and so, the month being completed, it is born, and is able to live. And these are true words. Water preserves the fetus for three months in the womb. Fire guards it for just as many. Air also fosters it for three months, which completes the same number, which was fostered as blood in the umbilical cord: which entered as a precious thing descending to the maternal breasts. There, after the narrow straits of birth, it assumes the whiteness of snow <note>The transformation of blood into breast milk</note>. The infant, drawing in breaths, having finally come forth, opens its mouth and is suckled. To the infant truly an exit will now lie open, until the mouth of the infant draws in all the breaths of the air: having come forth, however, it opens its mouth and is suckled.

### Chapter 97.

From these three months, we must with sharp wit compose and extract two: because from these words, two and seven are understood. <note>The author likely refers to the division of the nine months of gestation into stages of two and seven, or the relationship between the two luminaries (Sun/Moon) and the seven planets.</note> Whence all who desire to know this art, let them sharpen their wit to open the treasure from three words: in which are hidden the entire preparation and power of the stone: in which are hidden the dry and living oil, the living tincture, and the hot and moist oil. And this is the explanation of the three words, because just as from the beginning of conception until the birth of the infant, each Planet gives its own imagination <note>original: "imaginationem" - here meaning the formative influence or astrological imprint</note> by the divine power so creating: so in every work of alchemy, each planet in its place makes its impression until the completion of the work, and then the virtuous alchemy is born. And for this reason, many err in the work: and few reach the end. For in this work there is the leap of the Moon, the circle of the Sun, and three degrees. The first is weak: the second firm: the third perfect. The first is when the Sun enters Aries, and is in its exaltation. The second is when the Sun is in Leo. The third is when the Sun is in Sagittarius. Understand therefore, that according to these degrees the work of alchemy is completed.

->❦<-

<column-break/>

### Chapter 98.

Let us return therefore to the explanation of the three words: in which the entire art consists. Water preserves the fetus in the womb for three months. Air nourishes it for three months. Fire guards it for just as many. Know that all of this is said of Mercury <term>Mercury: the "Quick-silver" of the philosophers, considered the primary matter of the Stone</term> by way of a likeness. And this word is obscure, a dark term: and yet it is fit for understanding the truth. For there is one nature in a pregnant woman, and another in Mercury, through the likeness of the heat in the womb. For indeed fire has been found and extracted, which is of three degrees. Whence that word which is said, "fire guards for just as many," is obscure: and many err in it. For from three, you should understand two. The first degree completes the water and the air. The second degree completes all that we have said concerning "just as many": because it is the gift of God.

And for this reason, many err in the work: and few reach the end. For in this work is the leap of the Moon, the circle of the Sun, and three degrees. The first is weak: the second firm: the third perfect. The first is when the Sun enters Aries, and is in its exaltation. The second is when the Sun is in Leo. The third is when the Sun is in Sagittarius. Understand therefore, that according to these degrees the work of alchemy is completed.

->❦<-

<column-break/>

### Chapter 98.

Let us return therefore to the explanation of the three words: in which the whole art consists. Water preserves the fetus in the womb for three months. Air nourishes it for three months. Fire guards it for just as many. Know that all of this is said of Mercury <term>Mercury: the "Quick-silver" of the philosophers, representing the fluid, transformative principle of the work</term> through a likeness. And this word is obscure, the term is obscure: and yet it is fit for understanding the truth. For there is one nature in a pregnant woman, and another in Mercury, through the likeness of the heat in the womb. For indeed fire has been found and extracted, which is of three degrees. Whence that word which is said, "fire guards for just as many," is obscure: and many err in it. For from three, you should understand two. The first degree completes the water and the air. The second degree completes all that we have said concerning "just as many": because it is the gift of God.

### Chapter 99.

->❦<-

<summary>This page explains the symbolic gestation of the Alchemical Stone, comparing its development over nine months to the influence of the planets and the zodiacal signs of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. It emphasizes that the process is governed by the elements of Water, Air, and Fire acting upon the philosophical Mercury.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the "Book of Three Words," a classic alchemical treatise. It follows the discussion of the initial preparation of the "stone" and moves into the final stages of its transformation and the tests for its perfection.</meta>

# BOOK

The seed <note>original: "Sperma"</note> of this stone is cold, moist, and liquid. This liquid first forms a globe where it ought to; and then the limbs and the imaginative faculty <note>the formative power that shapes the substance</note> are formed according to their nature by God Most High. It comes to its rising and shines; and these are two degrees and seven. But the seed is like this stone alone. Nor is there anything else like it in this world, which impregnates itself, conceives from itself, and gives birth to itself. And after this living thing dies by a "red death," the cause of the dead thing is an equal heat and dryness. It does not flee from the fire, nor emit smoke, nor make a sound; it melts like wax, and is like oil, and it is dry—which dryness provides the dye <note>original: "tinctura," the transformative essence</note>. And with this tincture all dead things are rectified, so that they may live forever. It is a living tincture, both red and white. Note that in fire alone, without any other separation, all distillations, subtilizations, calcinations, reddenings, and deaths of Mercury <note>original: "Mercurij"</note> itself can be performed, as well as the deaths, meltings, and all resolutions and congealings, just as they ought to be. Therefore, use your wit and think deeply on how these things are done. For when friendship has been established between it and the fire, it flows like oil in a single stroke; this oil is ingressive, penetrating, mixing, coagulating, and preserving. For it conquers every subtle thing and will penetrate every solid thing. Therefore, let all who seek this science sharpen their wits as to how these things are done, and what the species of this art are. The proofs of this stone, however, are these:

<column-break/>

that when it has reached its end and second limit, a little of it should be placed upon a glowing coal: if it flows like wax, and emits no smoke, and adheres firmly to the metal, then it is perfect. But if these signs do not appear, then let the cooking <note>original: "decoctio"</note> continue until it becomes so. This is the proof above all proofs; and this proof ought to be made between the first and second solution and congealing. And it must be known that the first limit mortifies <note>kills or fixes</note> the coldness and moisture. The second limit completes the death of the first dead thing, increases the redness, and makes a dark mass shaped like a pine cone <note>original: "pineata"</note>, bright and shining like leaves of gold. And this is the rectification of Mercury, which is a fixed oil that rectifies all corrupt metals and leads them to health and to a better nature.

->Therefore, let<-
->God be praised:<-
->who created so much from the most vile thing.<-

### Here ends the book
### of the three
### words.

->Thanks be to God.<-
